Tensors, entanglement, separability, and their complexity
Shmuel Friedland

Abstract
. One of the most challenging problems in quantum physics is to quantify the entanglement of d d -partite states and their separability. We show here that these problems are best addressed using tensors. The geometric measure of entanglement of a pure state is one of most natural ways to quantify the entanglement, which is simply related to the spectral norm of a tensor state. On the other hand, the logarithm of the nuclear norm of the state and density tensors can be considered as its “energy”. We first show that the most geometric measure entangled d d -partite state has the minimum spectral norm and maximum nuclear norm. Second, we introduce the notion of Hermitian and density tensors, and the subspace of bi-symmetric Hermitian tensors, which correspond to Bosons. We show that separable density tensors, and strongly separable bi-symmetric density tensors are characterized by the value (equal to one) of their corresponding nuclear norms. In general, these characterizations are NP-hard to verify. Third, we show that the above quantities are computed in polynomial time when we restrict our attentions to Bosons: symmetric d d -qubits, or more generally to symmetric d d -qunits in ℂ n \mathbb{C}^{n} , and the corresponding bi-symmetric Hermtian density tensors, for a fixed value of n n .



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
05C50,15A69,15A75,68Q04,68Q17,68W25,81P16,81P40,90C05,90C08

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Statement of the main results

Let
\mathcal{H}
be an
n
-dimensional Hilbert space. Denote
[n]:=\{1,\ldots,n\}
. We identify
\mathcal{H}
with
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with the inner product
\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle=\mathbf{x}^{\dagger}\mathbf{y}
, where
\mathbf{x}^{\dagger}=\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\top}
.
A
d
-partite space is given by
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}:=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}},\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{d})
.
A
d
-partite state is a tensor
\mathcal{T}=[t_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}]\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}},\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\in[\mathbf{n}]=[n_{1}]\times\cdots\times[n_{d}]
, where
\|\mathcal{T}\|=\sqrt{\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in[\mathbf{n}]}|t_{\mathbf{i}}|^{2}}=1
. A bi-partite state is given by a matrix
T=[t_{i,j}]\in\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}\times n_{2}}
.
Denote by
\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}=\{\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}:\mathbf{x}_{j}\in\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}},\|\mathbf{x}_{j}\|=1,j\in[d]\}
, the product states in
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}
.
The spectral norm of
\mathcal{T}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}
is defined by
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty}=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}}|\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{T}\rangle|
. For
d=2
,
\|T\|_{\infty}
is the spectral norm of
T
, which is equal to
\sigma_{1}(T)
-the first Schmidt value-the first singular value of
T
. The geometric measure of entanglement (GME) is the distance of a state
\mathcal{T}
to the prodiuct states
\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}
and is equal to
\sqrt{2(1-\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty}})
. Thus, the most entangled states with respect to GME are correspond to the minimum
\alpha_{\infty,\mathbf{n}}:=\min_{\mathcal{T}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{T}\|=1}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty}
.
For bi-partite states the most entangled states are well known. For
n_{1}=n_{2}=n
they are
T=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}U
, where
U
is a unitary matrix. For
d\geqslant 3
we know only the following cases of most GME
d
-qubits (
n=2
):
d=3
and
d=4
. For
d=3
this is the
W
-state (
3.5
)
[
53
]
. In subsection
3.3
we give a complete proof of the result in
[
14
]
that the Higuchi-Sudbery
4
-qubit
[
34
]
is the most GME
4
-quibit. Let
n^{\times d}=n(1,\ldots,1)\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
. For
d
-qubit for
d\gg 1
we show in subsection
3.4
that
-2\log_{2}\alpha_{\infty,2^{\times d}}=d+O(\log_{2}d)
.

Next we consider the GME for Bosons, which correspond to symmetric tensors states in the subspace of symmetric tensors
\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n^{\times d}}
. Most GME Bosons correspond to the minimum
\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},s}=\min_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty}
.
Note that
\dim\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}={n+d-1\choose d}
which is usually much smaller than
\dim\mathbb{C}^{n^{\times d}}=n^{d}
. Fix
n\geqslant 2
and consider
d\geqslant 1
. Observe that
\dim\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}=O(n^{d-1})
. We show that for
d\gg 1
we have
-2\log_{2}\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},s}=O(\log_{2}{n+d-1\choose d})
.
We also consider the Fermions, which correspond to the skew-symmetric states
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
, which live in the exterior algebra of wedge products.

Another measure of entanglement of states is the nuclear norm, which is the dual norm of the spectral norm, denoted as
\|\cdot\|_{1}
on
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}
. For
d=2

\|T\|_{1}
is the sum of Schmidt’s numbers: the sum of singular values of the matrix
T
.
It can be characterized as a minimum problem (
2.4
). The quantity
2\log\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1}
can be viewed as an energy of to create a state
\mathcal{T}
from the product states.
We show a known inequality
2\log\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1}\geqslant-2\log\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty}
for a state
\mathcal{T}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}
. The most entangled state with respect to GME are also most entangled state with respect to the nuclear norm.

Let
\mathrm{H}_{n}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
be the real space of Hermitian matrices. Denote by
\mathrm{H}_{n,+}\supset\mathrm{H}_{n,+,1}
the cone of positive semi-definite matrices, and the convex set of positive semi-definite matrices of trace one.
Recall that the notion of a mixed state on
\mathbb{C}^{n}
corresponds to a density matrix
\rho\in\mathrm{H}_{n,+,1}
. A pure density matrix is
\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^{\dagger},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1
, and a mixed state is a convex combination of pure density states.
To generalize this notion to
d
-partite mixed states, called here
density tensors
we introduce the notion of Hermitian tensors, denoted as
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}\times\mathbf{n}}:=\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}
if for
\mathcal{T}=[t_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}],\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in[\mathbf{n}]
the following condition hold:
t_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}=\overline{t_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{i}}}
for
\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in[\mathbf{n}]
.
Denote by
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+}\supset\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}
the cone of positive semi-definite tensors, viewed as matrices, and the cone of positive semi-definite tensors with trace one. A pure density tensor is of the form
\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1
. The “right” norm on
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
is
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}:=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}}|\langle\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{B}\rangle|
. Note that
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}\leqslant\|\mathcal{B}\|_{\infty}
, and equality holds if
\pm\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+}
. A density tensor is called
separable
if it is a convex combination of pure density tensors corresponding to product states
\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}
, a generalization of
[
45
]
. Denote by
\|\cdot\|_{nuc}
the dual norm of
\|\cdot\|_{spec}
on
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
. A generalization of the results
[
48
,
46
]
states that a density tensor
\mathcal{R}
is separable if and only
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{nuc}=1
.

Denote by
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}},\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}\subset\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
the subspace of
bi-symmetric
and
bi-skew-symmetric
Hermitian tensors. respectively. Each of this subspace is a linear span of pure density tensors
\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}
, where
\mathcal{X}
is either in
\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
or
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
respectively. The density tensors in
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
and
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
correspond to the Bosons and Fermions density tensors respectively. The spectral norm on
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
is
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{bspec}:=\max_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1}|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{B}\rangle|
, while on
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
one takes the restriction of the spectral norm on
\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
. Denote by
\|\cdot\|_{bnuc}
and
\|\cdot\|_{anuc}
the dual norms the
\|\cdot\|_{bspec}
and
\|\cdot\|_{nuc}
on
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
and
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
respectively. A density tensor in
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
is called strongly separable if it is a convex combination of pure density tensors of the form
\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes d}
, where
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1
.
We show that a bi-symmetric density tensor
\mathcal{R}
is separable if and only if
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bnuc}=1
. A bi-skew-symmetric density tensor is called strongly separable if it is a convex combination of
(\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d})\otimes(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{d})
, where
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d}
is an orthonormal set in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Then
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
is strongly separable if and only if
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{anuc}=d!
.

We discuss the computability of tensor norms and separability of given tensors.
It is well known that most problems in tensors are NP-hard. (See subsection
5.1
for a definition of NP-hardness.) Similarly, a bipartite separability for
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2},+,1}
is NP-hard. The main result of this paper is that for fixed
n\geqslant 2
the computations of the spectral and nuclear norms on
\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n},\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
and strong separability in
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
are poly-time computable.

1.2.
Related works

The notion of entanglement goes back to the famous papers of Einstein-Podolsky-Rosen
[
17
]
and Schrödinger
[
50
,
51
]
.
Maximally entangled bi-partite qubits appear in Bell’s inequalities as Bell states
[
4
]
.
Shimony introduced the notion of the geometric measure of entanglement in
[
52
]
. We now mention those papers which are related to the results of our paper. Barnum-Linden
[
3
]
discuss multi-partite case, but not the GME.
They discuss Slater rank for Bosons (symmetric tensors), and Fermions (skew-symmetric tensors), that we briefly touch upon in subsection
2.3
.
Wei-Goldbar
[
54
]
discuss GME for multi-partite state without introducing the spectral norm. The also discuss briefly the GME of special Bosons. For density tensor entanglement they use the
convex roof
that we don’t discuss. Tamaryan-Wei-Park
[
53
]
give the proof that the W-state is the most entangled
3
-qubit state with respect to the GME, see also
[
9
]
. Hübener et al
[
39
]
disucuss the GME for Bosons. They rediscover Banach’s theorem
[
2
]
that the closest
d
-partite product state to a given Boson can be chosen to be a Boson
\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1
. Aulbach-Markham-Murao
[
1
]
give examples of
d
-symmetric qubits for
d=4,\ldots,12
, which they assume to have the maximal GME. Their examples are motivated by the Majorana model. In
[
24
]
we verified the numerical values of these examples using our software, and we could not find symmetric qubits with higher GME. Friedland-Wang
[
24
]
describe a way to compute the GME of Bosons using solutions of polynomial equations.
Gross-Flammia-Eisert
[
27
]
show that most of
d
-qubits are maximally entangled for
d\gg 1
, and are not useful as computational resources. Friedland-Kemp
[
20
]
showed that most of
d
-Bosons in a fixed
\mathbb{C}^{n}
are maximally entangled for
d\gg 1
.

Werner
[
55
]
showed that a bi-partite separable density tensor can be modeled by a hidden-variable theory. Werner density tensors are separable for
p\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
and entangled for
p<\frac{1}{2}
. Peres
[
45
]
introduced the notion of the partial positivity transport (PPT) for separability.
All entangled Werner states violate the PPT separability criterion. M.-P.-R. Horodecki
[
37
]
showed that for
2
-qubits and
2-3
bi-partite qubits the density tensor is separable if and only if the PPT property holds. P. Horodecki
[
38
]
showed that there exists inseparable bipartite 3-qubits which have PPT property.
Rudolph
[
48
]
showed that a bi-partite tensor is separable if and only if its nuclear norm is one. This result was extended to
d
-partite density tensors for
d\geqslant 3
by Perez-Garcia
[
46
]
. Gurvits-Barnum
[
29
]
found the maximum ball of separable density tensors centered at the maximally mixed bipartite density tensor, which is a scalar times the identity operator. A remarkable result of Gurvits
[
30
]
claims that it is NP-hard to determine if a given density tensor is within
\varepsilon>0
distance to the set of separable bi-partite tensors. See also S. Gharibian
[
25
]
. Chen-Chu-Qian-Shen,
[
8
]
discuss density tensors which are symmeric, (they call such tensors supersymmetric). This tensors are real valued. They show that a
2d
-symmetric density tensor is separable if and only if it strongly separable: it is a convex combinations of
\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(2d)},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1
. Recent paper
[
12
]
discusses chiral symmetries and multiparticle entanglement.

1.3.
A short summary of the paper

Section
2
discusses some results in tensors that we use in this paper.
We consider our tensors over a field
\mathbb{F}
either of real numbers
\mathbb{R}
or the complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
.
Subsection
2.1
discusses some basic notions in tensors: rank of tensor,
product states, spectral and nuclear norms, and some of their properties.
Subsection
2.2
discusses
d
-symmetric tensors. We recall the one-to-one correspondence between the symmetric polynomials and homogeneous polynomials of degree
d
. Next we recall the nice and useful Banach characterization of the spectral norm which implies an analogous characterization of the nuclear norm.
Subsection
2.3
discusses
Fermions
, which are
skew-symmetric
tensors of norm one. Skew-symmetric tensors are elements of the Exterior (Grassmann) algebra. It turns out that one has an analog to Banach’s theorem for skew-symmetric tensors. Subsection
2.4
discusses briefly the notion of the nuclear rank. Nuclear rank, unlike the tensor rank, is lower semicontinuous.

Section
3
discusses two major notions of entanglements: the geometric measure of entanglement (GME) and the nuclear norm of tensor states. Subsection
3.1
deals with two measures of entanglement of tensor states expressed in terms of the spectral and nuclear norms. The maximum entangled states are the same with respect to theses two measurements.
Subsection
3.2
deals with the minimum value of the tensor norm of the state tensors, and gives some lower bounds. Subsection
3.3
reproduces the unpublished result of
[
14
]
, with some more details, that the Higuchi-Sudbery
4
-qubit
[
34
]
is the most geometric entangled 4-qubit.
Subsection
3.4
discusses entanglement of
d
-qubits and
d
-Boson qubits for big
d
. It is known that most of such quibits are almost maximally entangled.

Section
4
discusses the notion of density tensors and their separability.
In subsection
4.1
we first define the notion of Hermitian tensors

\H_{\mathbf{n}}\subset\otimes^{2}\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}},
which can be viewed as a linear transformation on
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}
. A Hermitian tensor has a spectral decomposition

\mathcal{B}=\sum_{k=1}^{N(\mathbf{n})}\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{B})\mathcal{X}_{k}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}_{k}},\mathcal{X}_{k}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}},\langle\mathcal{X}_{k},\mathcal{X}_{l}\rangle=\delta_{kl},k,l\in[N(\mathbf{n})]
.
On
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
we define
a spectral norm
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}

|\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}\mathcal{X}\rangle|
, which is majorized by
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{\infty}
. The dual norm to
\|\cdot\|_{spec}
on
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
is
\|\cdot\|_{nuc}
, which majorizes the norm
\|\cdot\|_{1}
.
Theorem
4.4
states that the set of separable states is
\textrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
is characterised by
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{nuc}=1
. Furthermore,
\textrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
is a maximal dimensional face of the unit ball centered at the origin with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{nuc}
.
We also characterize the maximal entangled density tensors that maximize the nuclear norm. In subsection
4.2
we discuss the bi-symmetric Hermitian tensors, denoted as
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
. We show that they have the following spectral decomposition:

\mathcal{B}=\sum_{k=1}^{{n+d-1\choose d}}\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{B})\mathcal{X}_{k}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}_{k}},\mathcal{X}_{k}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n},\langle\mathcal{X}_{k},\mathcal{X}_{l}\rangle=\delta_{kl},k,l\in[{n+d-1\choose d}]
.
On
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
we define another spectral norm
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{bspec}=\max_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1}|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\times d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{B}\rangle|
,
which is majorized
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}
. The dual norm of
\|\cdot\|_{bspec}
is denoted by
\|\cdot\|_{bnuc}
, which majorizes
\|\cdot\|_{nuc}
. A bi-symmetric Hermitian density tensor is called strongly separable if it is a convex combination of
\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes d}
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1
. Denote by
\textrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d}}
the set of strongly separable density tensors.
Theorem
4.11
is an analog Theorem
4.4
. In particular, it shows that a bi-symmetric Hermitian density tensor
\mathcal{R}
is strongly separable if and only if
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bnuc}=1
. Subsection
4.3
discusses a subspace of bi-symmetric Hermitian tensors, which are symmetric tensors:
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}:=\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}\cap\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
. Symmetric tensors are called supersymmetric in
[
8
]
. Theorem
4.15
is an analog of Theorem
4.11
. In particular, we show that a symmetric density tensor
\mathcal{R}
is separable as real density tensor if and only if
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}=1
. This result gives another proof of Theorem
[
8
, Theorem 14]
: Every real separable symmetric density tensor is a convex combination of symmetric rank one density tensors
\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(2d)},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1
. Subsection
4.4
deals with bi-skew-symmetric Hermitian tensors, denoted by
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
. These tensors have a spectral decomposition

\mathcal{B}=\sum_{k=1}^{{n\choose d}}\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{B})\mathcal{X}_{k}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}_{k}},\mathcal{X}_{k}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n},\langle\mathcal{X}_{k},\mathcal{X}_{l}\rangle=\delta_{kl},k,l\in[{n\choose d}]
.
The spectral norm on
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
is the restriction of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
. A basic separable
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
density tensor of Slater rank one is
(\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d})\otimes(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{d})
, where
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d}
is an orthonormal set of vectors in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
A bi-skew-density tensor is called separable if it is a convex combinations of basic separable tensors in
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
.
Denote by
\|\cdot\|_{anuc}
the dual norm to
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
on
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
. we show

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}\geqslant\|\mathcal{B}\|_{anuc}\geqslant\|\mathcal{B}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}
for bi-skew-Hermitian tensors.
Theorem
4.21
is analog of Theorem
4.11
. In particular we show that
a bi-skew-symmetric density tensor is separable if and only if
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{anuc}=d!
.
The main result of subsection
4.5
is the claim that the
separability sets
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
,
\mathrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d}}
and their restrictions to
\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+,1}
are semi-algebraic sets.

Section
5
discusses the complexity results on tensors, entanglement and separablity. Subsection
5.1
explains briefly the notions of
poly-time, NP and NP-hard problems. The main results of these section is that the following norms are NP-hard to compute:
\|\cdot\|_{bspec}
and
\|\cdot\|_{bnuc}\|
for tensors in
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2},+,1}
and
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2}}
respectively;
the norms
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}
for tensors in
\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2m},+,1}
and
\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2m}}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
respectively;
the norms
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}
for tensors in
\Re\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2m},+,1}
and
\Re\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2m}}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
respectively;
the norms
\|\cdot\|_{spec}
and
\|\cdot\|_{nuc}
for tensors in
\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2m},+,1}
and
\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2m}}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
respectively;
the norms
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
are NP-hard to compute on
\mathbb{F}^{n^{\times(4m)}}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
. Subsection
5.2
shows how to code symmetric and bi-symmetric Hermitian tensors as polynomials. Subsection
5.3
shows how to approximate spectral and nuclear norms of symmetric and bi-symmetric Hermitian tensor using the results of subsection
5.2
. Subsection
5.4
proves the most important result of this paper. The following norms are poly-time computable in
d
for a fixed
n\geqslant 2
:
the norms
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
on
\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
,
the norms
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
for
\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}\otimes\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
;
the norms
\|\cdot\|_{bspec}
and
\|\cdot\|_{bnuc}
for
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
.
In particular, the strong separability of bi-symmetric Hermitian density tensors, and separability of symmetric density tensors are poly-time computable.

Section
6
states a small number of open problems.
Appendix
A
compare a general norm
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
of
\mathbb{C}^{n}
to the Euclidean norm. The important result is that the maximal and the minimum distortions
of
\nu
nad its dual
\nu^{*}
are reciprocal. In subsection
A.1
we dicuss the notion of
\nu
\rank
.

2.
Tensors

2.1.
Basic notions

Let

(2.1)

\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d},\quad d\in\mathbb{N},\quad[\mathbf{n}]=[n_{1}]\times\cdots\times[n_{d}],\quad\mathbf{1}_{d}=(1,\ldots,1)\in\mathbb{N}^{d}.

Assume that
\mathbb{F}
is a field.
The space of
d
-mode tensors is the tensor product
\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n_{j}}
denoted as
\mathbb{F}^{\mathbf{n}}=\mathbb{F}^{n_{1}\times\cdots\times n_{d}}
, where
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d},d\in\mathbb{N}
. The cases:
d=1
corresponds to vectors
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}
;
d=2
corresponds to matrices
X=[x_{i,j}]\in\mathbb{F}^{m\times n}
;
d\geqslant 3
corresponds to
\mathcal{X}=[x_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}]\in\mathbb{F}^{\mathbf{n}}
. For
d\geqslant 2
a tensor
\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{F}^{\mathbf{n}}
is a rank-one tensor if
\mathcal{X}=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}=[x_{i_{1},1}\cdots x_{i_{d},d}]\neq 0
. Denote by
\Sigma_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}\subset\mathbb{F}^{\mathbf{n}}
the Segre variety of rank-one tensors
[
40
]
. The rank of
\mathcal{X}\neq 0
is the minimal
r
such that

\mathcal{X}
is is a sum of
r
rank-one tensors. It is shown in
[
32
]
that determining the rank of
\mathcal{X}
for
d\geqslant 3
is an NP-complete problem for a finite field
\mathbb{F}
, and an NP-hard problem for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
.

In what follows we assume that
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
. Whenever the field
\mathbb{F}
is omitted, we assume that
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
. A standard inner product on
\mathbb{F}^{\mathbf{n}}
is

\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\rangle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{s})\in[\mathbf{n}]}\overline{x_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}}y_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}
, and the Euclidean norm
\|\mathcal{X}\|=\sqrt{\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}\rangle}
.
Clearly, for
\mathcal{X}=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}
we have the equality
\|\mathcal{X}\|=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|
. Denote by
\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}\subset\Sigma_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}
the set of rank one tensors whose norm is one:

(2.2)

\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}=\{\otimes_{j=1}^{d},\mathbf{x}_{j}\in\mathbb{F}^{n_{j}},\|\mathbf{x}_{j}\|=1,j\in[d]\},

which is called the set of product states.

The following norms for
\mathcal{X}\in\mathcal{H}
are called the spectral and nuclear norms, respectively
[
22
]
:

(2.3)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=\max_{\mathcal{Y}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}}\Re\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X}\rangle=\max_{\mathcal{Y}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}}|\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X}\rangle|,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\inf_{\mathcal{X}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{X}_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i}\in\Sigma_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}},r\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\|\mathcal{X}_{i}\|

The spectral norm was probably considered first by Banach
[
2
]
, and the nuclear norm by Grothendick
[
28
]
, see
[
42
]
. Since we are dealing with the finite dimensional case one can put an explicit upper bound on
r
. This upper bound and other results are stated in the following theorem:

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
d\geqslant 2
and
2\mathbf{1}_{d}\leqslant\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
. Assume that
0\neq\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{F}^{\mathbf{n}}
. Then

(1)

The following characterization holds:

(2.4)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\min_{\mathcal{X}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{X}_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i}\in\Sigma_{\mathbf{n}},r\leqslant r(\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F})}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\|\mathcal{X}_{i}\|

\displaystyle r(\mathbf{n},\mathbb{R})=N(\mathbf{n})+1,\quad r(\mathbf{n},\mathbb{C})=2N(\mathbf{n})+1.

(2)

The following sharp inequalities hold

(2.5)

\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}\leqslant\|\mathcal{X}\|\leqslant\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1}.

Equality in one the inequalities hold if and only if
\mathcal{X}
is a rank-one matrix.

(3)

Let
\alpha_{s,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}\leqslant\beta_{s,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}
be the constants defined in (
A.6
) for the norms
\nu_{s}=\|\cdot\|_{s,\mathbb{F}}
for
s=\infty,1
:

(2.6)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}=\min_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{F}^{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}},\quad\beta_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{F}^{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}},

\displaystyle\alpha_{1,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}=\min_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{F}^{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}},\quad\beta_{\infty,\mathbf{n}\mathbb{F}}=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{F}^{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}.

Then

(2.7)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}<1=\beta_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}},

\displaystyle\alpha_{1,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}=1<\beta_{1,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}}.

Furthermore for
\|\mathcal{X}\|=1
one has the implications

(2.8)

\alpha_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}=\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}\iff\beta_{1,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}=\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}.

(4)

Let
e\in\mathbb{N},\mathbf{m}\in\mathbb{N}^{e}
.
Assume that
0\neq\mathcal{Y}\in\mathbb{F}^{\mathbf{m}}
. Then

(2.9)

\|\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}\|\mathcal{Y}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}},\quad\|\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}\|\mathcal{Y}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}.

Proof.

(1)
. It is enough to prove the characterization (
2.4
), where
\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=1
.
We now repeat briefly the arguments in
[
22
]
that the extreme points of
\mathrm{B}_{\|\cdot\|_{1}}(\mathbf{0},1)
is
\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}
. In view of the characterization of
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}
given in (
2.3
) it follows that cospan
(\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}})
is a unit ball of a norm
\nu
. The characterization (
A.3
) yields that
\nu^{*}=\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}
. Assume that
\mathcal{X}=\otimes_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}
= Clearly,

\max_{\mathcal{Y}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}}\Re\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X}\rangle\leqslant 1
,
where equality holds if and only if
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{X}
. Hence,
\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}
is a closed set of the extreme points of
\mathrm{B}_{\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}}(\mathbf{0},1)
. As
\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=1
it follows that

\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{B}_{\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}}(\mathbf{0},1)
.
Assume first that
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
. As the real dimension of
\mathbb{R}^{\mathbf{n}}
is
N(\mathbf{n})
, Caratheodory’s theorem yields that
\mathcal{X}
is a convex combination of at most
N(\mathbf{n})+1
vectors of
\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}
. In particular,
\mathcal{X}
is a convex combination of exactly
r\leqslant N(\mathbf{n})+1
vectors of
\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}
:

\mathcal{X}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{X}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\mathcal{Y}_{i},\quad\mathcal{X}_{i}=a_{i}\mathcal{Y}_{i},a_{i}>0,\mathcal{Y}_{i}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}},i\in[r],\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}=\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=1.

Assume now that
\mathcal{X}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{X}_{i}
, and
\mathcal{X}_{i}\in\Sigma_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{R}},i\in[r]
. Clearly,
\mathcal{X}_{i}=\|\mathcal{X}_{i}\|\mathcal{Y}_{i}
, where
\mathcal{Y}_{i}=\frac{1}{\|\mathcal{X}_{i}\|}\mathcal{X}_{i}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{R}},i\in[r]
. Use the triangle inequality to deduce

1=\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}=\|\sum_{i=1}^{r}\|\mathcal{X}_{i}\|\mathcal{Y}_{i}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{r}\|\mathcal{X}_{i}\|.

This proves the characterizations in (
2.3
) and (
2.4
) for
\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}
.

To show the the characterizations in (
2.3
) and (
2.4
) for
\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}
, observe that
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}\sim\mathbb{R}^{\mathbf{n}}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{\mathbf{n}}
. That is, the real dimension of
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}
is
2\mathbb{N}(\mathbf{n})
. Then use Caratheodory’s theorem.

(2)
As

\|\mathcal{X}\|=\max_{\mathcal{Y},\|\mathcal{Y}\|=1}\Re\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X}\rangle\geqslant\max_{\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Y}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}}\Re\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X}\rangle=\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}},

we deduce the first inequality in (
2.5
).
Assume that
\|\mathcal{X}\|=1
.
Clearly, if
\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}
then
\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=1
. Suppose that
\mathcal{X}\not\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}
. Observe that for
\|\mathcal{Y}\|=1
one has the inequality
\Re\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X}\rangle\leqslant 1
, and equality holds if and only if and only if
\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{X}
. Hence,
\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}<1
.

Let
0<\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}
, where
a_{i}>0
and
\mathcal{X}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\mathcal{Y}_{i},\mathcal{Y}_{i}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}
. Clearly,

\|\mathcal{X}\|\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{r}||a_{i}\mathcal{Y}_{i}\|=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}=\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}},

which establishes the second inequality in (
2.5
). Suppose that
\|\mathcal{X}\|=\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
. We claim that
\mathcal{Y}_{1}=\cdots=\mathcal{Y}_{r}
. For
r=1
there is nothing to prove. Assume that
r>1
. Then we have an equality in the triangle inequality for the Euclidean norm. Thus implies that
a_{i}Y_{i}=t_{i}a_{1}Y_{1}
for
t_{i}>0
and
i\geqslant 2
.
Hence,
\mathcal{Y}_{i}=\mathcal{Y}_{1}
for
i>1
, and
\mathcal{X}\in\Sigma_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}
.

(3)
The results of
(2)
yield that

\alpha_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}<\beta_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}=\alpha_{1,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}=1<\beta_{1,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}.

As the norms
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}
are conjugate norms,
(
A.7
) yields that
\alpha_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}\beta_{1,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}=1
. This proves
(
2.7
). The implication (
2.8
) folows from (
A.9
).

(4)
The first equality in (
2.7
) follows straightforward from the observation that
\Pi_{(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m}),\mathbb{F}}=\Pi_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}\otimes\Pi_{\mathbf{m},\mathbb{F}}
and the definition of
\|\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}
.
To prove the second equality we argue as follows. Let
\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
and
\|\mathcal{Y}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
correspond to the following decompositions of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
respectively:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\mathcal{X}_{i},\quad\mathcal{X}_{i}\in\Sigma_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}},\quad\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\|\mathcal{X}_{i}\|,

\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}=\sum_{j=1}^{q}\mathcal{Y}_{j}\quad\mathcal{Y}_{j}\in\Sigma_{\mathbf{m},\mathbb{F}},\quad\|\mathcal{Y}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\sum_{j=1}^{q}\|\mathcal{Y}_{j}\|.

Hence,

\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}=\sum_{i=j=1}^{p,q}\mathcal{X}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{j}\Rightarrow\|\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}\leqslant\sum_{i=j=1}^{p,q}\|\mathcal{X}_{i}\otimes\mathcal{Y}_{j}\|\leqslant\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}\|\mathcal{Y}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}.

We now show the reverse inequality. Clearly,
\|\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\max_{\mathcal{Z}\in\mathbb{F}^{(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m})},\|\mathcal{Z}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=1}|\langle\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}\rangle|
.
Now choose

\mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{Z}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{Z}_{2},\mathcal{Z}_{1}\in\mathbb{F}^{\mathbf{n}},\mathcal{Z}_{2}\in\mathbb{F}^{\mathbf{m}},\|\mathcal{Z}_{1}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=\|\mathcal{Z}_{2}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=1\Rightarrow\|\mathcal{Z}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=1.

Observe that
\langle\mathcal{Z}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{Z}_{2},\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{Z}_{1},\mathcal{X}\rangle\langle\mathcal{Z}_{2},\mathcal{Y}\rangle
. Take maximum of
|\langle\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}\rangle|
on all such
\mathcal{Z}
to deduce:
\|\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}\geqslant\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}\|\mathcal{Y}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
. Combine this inequality with a previous one to deduce
\|\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}\|\mathcal{Y}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
.
∎

The following result is well known for matrices.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
1<m\leqslant n
be integers. Let
T\in\mathbb{F}^{m\times n}
. Let
\sigma_{1}\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\sigma_{m}\geqslant 0
the the singular values of
T
.
Then

(2.10)

\|T\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=\sigma_{1},\quad\|T\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}.

Assume
\|T\|=1
. Then

(1)

The equality
\|T\|_{1}=\beta_{1,(m,n),\mathbb{F}}
holds if and only if the
m
singular values of
T
are
\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}
.

(2)

The equality
\|T\|_{\infty}=\alpha_{\infty,(m,n),\mathbb{F}}
holds if and only if the
m
singular values of
T
are
\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}
.

In particular

(2.11)

\beta_{1,(m,n),\mathbb{F}}=\sqrt{m},\quad\alpha_{\infty,(m,n),\mathbb{F}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}.

Proof.

The first equality of (
2.10
) is well known. The second equality is shown in
[
19
, Corollary 7.5.13]
.
Assume that
\|T\|=1

Then
1=\|T\|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}^{2}
. Use Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to deduce that

\|T\|_{1}^{2}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}\right)^{2}\leqslant m\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}^{2}\right)=m
. Equality holds if and only if all singular values of
T
are
\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}
.

Observe next that from the equality
1=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\sigma_{i}^{2}
we deduce that
1\leqslant m\sigma_{1}^{2}
. Hence
\|T\|_{\infty}=\sigma_{1}\geqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}
. Equality holds if and only if all singular values of
T
are
\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}
.
∎

Definition 2.3
.

Denote by
\mathrm{U}_{n}\supset\mathrm{S}\mathrm{U}_{n}
the group of unitary matrices and the subgroup of the special unitary matrices of order
n
. Let
\mu_{n}
be th Haar measure on
\mathrm{U}_{n}
.
Denote

\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{n}}:=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathrm{U}_{n_{j}},\quad\mathrm{S}\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{n}}=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathrm{S}\mathrm{U}_{n_{j}},

are subgroups of
\mathrm{U}_{N(\mathbf{n})}
and
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{U}_{N(\mathbf{n})}
respectively that act on
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}
:
\mathcal{X}\mapsto U\mathcal{X}
.
Denote by
\phi_{d}:\mathrm{U}_{n}\to\mathrm{U}_{n^{\times d}}
the diagonal embedding
A\mapsto\otimes^{d}A
, where

(2.12)

n^{\times d}=(\underbrace{n,\ldots,n}_{d}).

Observe that
\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{n}}
is a subgroup of isometries of the spectral and nuclear norms on
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}
.

2.2.
Symmetric tensors

A tensor
\mathcal{S}=[s_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}]\in\otimes^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
is called symmetric if
s_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}=s_{i_{\omega(1)},\ldots,i_{\omega(d)}}
for every permutation
\omega:[d]\to[d]
.
Denote by
\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}\subset\otimes^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
the vector space of
d
-mode symmetric tensors on
\mathbb{F}^{n}
.
It is well known that
\dim\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}={n+d-1\choose d}

[
20
]
.
In what follows we assume that
\mathcal{S}
is a symmetric tensor and
d\geqslant 2
, unless stated otherwise. A tensor

\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
defines a unique homogeneous polynomial of degree
d
in
n
variables

(2.13)

f(\mathbf{x})=\langle\overline{\mathcal{S}},\otimes^{d}\mathbf{x}\rangle=\sum_{0\leqslant j_{k}\leqslant d,k\in[n],j_{1}+\cdots+j_{n}=d}\frac{d!}{j_{1}!\cdots j_{n}!}f_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}x_{1}^{j_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{j_{n}}.

Conversely, a homogeneous polynomial
f(\mathbf{x})
of degree
d
in
n
variables defines a unique symmetric
\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
by the following relation.
Consider the multiset
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}\}
, where each
i_{l}\in[n]
. Let
j_{k}
be the number of times the integer
k\in[n]
appears in the multiset
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}\}
.
Then
s_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}=f_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}
. Furthermore

(2.14)

\|\mathcal{S}\|^{2}=\sum_{0\leqslant j_{k}\leqslant d,k\in[n],j_{1}+\cdots+j_{n}=d}\frac{d!}{j_{1}!\cdots j_{n}!}|f_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}|^{2},

where
s_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}=f_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}
.

The remarkable result of Banach
[
2
]
claims that the spectral norm of a symmetric
tensor can be computed as a maximum on the set of rank one symmetric tensors:

(2.15)

\|\mathcal{S}\|_{\sigma,\mathbb{F}}=\max_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1}\pm\Re\langle\otimes^{d}\mathbf{x},\mathcal{S}\rangle.

(The
\pm
sign needed only if
\mathbb{F}=R
and
d
is even.)
This result was rediscovered several times since 1938. In quantum information theory (QIT), for the case
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
, it appeared in
[
39
]
. In mathematical literature, for the case
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
, it appeared in
[
7
,
18
]
. (Observe that a natural generalization of Banach’s theorem to partially symmetric tensors is given in
[
18
]
.)

The analog of Banach’s theorem for the nuclear norm of symmetric tensors was stated in
[
22
]
. Namely, for
0\neq\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
we have the following minimal characterization

(2.16)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\min_{\mathcal{S}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\varepsilon_{i}\otimes^{d}\mathbf{x}_{i},\;\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\},\varepsilon_{i}\in\{1,-1\},r\leqslant r(n,d,\mathbb{F})}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|^{d},

\displaystyle r(n,d,\mathbb{R})={n+d-1\choose d}+1,\quad r(n,d,\mathbb{C})=2{n+d-1\choose d}+1.

We can assume that
\varepsilon_{i}=1
unless
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
and
d
is even.
Furthermore, Caratheodory’s theorem yields that
r\leqslant{n+d-1\choose d}
for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
and
r\leqslant 2{n+d-1\choose d}
for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
.

Let

(2.17)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{F}}=\min_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}},\quad\beta_{\infty,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{F}}=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}},

\displaystyle\alpha_{1,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{F}}=\min_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}},\quad\beta_{1,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{F}}=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}.

We state an analog of Theorem
2.1
and
2.2
. The proof of this theorem is similar to Theorem
2.1
and Lemma
2.2

and we leave it to the reader.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
n,d\geqslant 2
be integers. Then

(1)

(2.18)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{F}}<1=\beta_{\infty,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{F}},

\displaystyle\alpha_{1,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{F}}=1<\beta_{1,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{F}}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{F}}}.

(2)

Assume that
\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
and
\|\mathcal{S}\|=1
. Then
\|\mathcal{S}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{F}}
if and only if
\|\mathcal{S}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\beta_{1,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{F}}
.

(3)

\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times 2},s,\mathbb{F}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}},\,\beta_{1,n^{\times 2},s,\mathbb{F}}=\sqrt{n}
.

(4)

Assume that
S
is an
n\times n
complex valued symmetric matrix having Frobenius norm one,
\|S\|=1
. Then
\|S\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\beta_{1,n^{\times 2},s,\mathbb{F}}
if and only if
\sqrt{n}S
is a unitary matrix.

2.3.
Fermions

Denote by
\Omega_{d}
the group of permutations acting on
[d]
. Then sign
:\Omega_{d}\to\{-1,1\}
is the character such that sign
(\tau)=-1
for a transposition
\tau
.
The group
\Omega
acts on
\otimes^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}=\mathbb{F}^{n^{\times d}}
by permutations on the
d
factors of
\otimes^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
:

(2.19)

T_{d,n}(\sigma)(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j})=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}x_{\sigma(j)},\quad\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{F}^{n},i\in[d],2\leqslant d\in\mathbb{N},\sigma\in\Omega_{d}.

Denote by
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}\subset\mathbb{F}^{n^{\times d}}
the subspace of fermions,
i.e., antisymmetric (skew-symmetric) tensors. That is,
\mathcal{F}=[f_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}]\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
if
f_{\tau(i_{1}),\ldots,\tau(i_{d})}=-f_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}
for each transposition
\tau:[d]\to[d]
.
Note that
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}=\{0\}
if
d>n
. Thus we assume that
d\leqslant n
.

Lemma 2.5
.

(1)

For a rank-one tensor
\otimes_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{i}
denote

(2.20)

\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d!}}\sum_{\sigma\in\Omega_{d}}\mathrm{sign}(\sigma)\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{\sigma(j)}.

Then
\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}=0
if and only if
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d}
are linearly dependent. Assume that
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d}
are linearly independent. Let
\mathbf{W}=\mathrm{span}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d})\subset\mathbb{F}^{n}
. Then, for each
\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{d}\in\mathbf{W}
the tensor
\mathbf{y}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{y}_{d}
is colinear with
\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}
.
Furthermore,
\|\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}\|\geqslant\|\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}\|
. Equality holds if and only if
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d}
is an orthogonal set of vectors.
Assume that
\|\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}\|=1
. Then
\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}=\zeta(\mathbf{u}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{u}_{d})
, where
\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{d}
is any orthonormal basis in
\mathbf{W}
, and
\zeta\in\mathbb{F},|\zeta|=1
is uniquely determined by
\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}
.

(2)

Let
\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\sigma):\otimes^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}\to\otimes^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
be defined by (
2.19
). Then for
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\in\otimes^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
and
\sigma,\eta\in\Omega_{d}
the following equalities hold:

(2.21)

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\sigma)\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\eta)=\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\sigma\eta),

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\sigma)\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{X},\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\sigma^{-1})\mathcal{Y}\}\rangle,

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\sigma)\mathcal{X}\|=\|\mathcal{X}\|,\quad\|\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\sigma)\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}},\quad\|\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\sigma)\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}.

(3)

The linear operator

(2.22)

\mathrm{P}_{d,n}=\frac{1}{d!}\sum_{\sigma\in\Omega_{d}}\mathrm{sign}(\sigma)\mathrm{T}_{d,n}:\otimes^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}\to\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}

is an orthogonal projection on
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
.
Furthermore,
\mathrm{P}_{d,n}
is nonexpansive, (norm decreasing) with respect to the spectral and nuclear norms

Proof.

(1)
It is straightforward to show that

(2.23)

\mathrm{T}(\sigma)\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}=\textrm{sign}(\sigma)\mathbf{x}_{\sigma(1)}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{\sigma(d)}.

Hence,
\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
. Observe that
\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}
is multi linear. Hence,
\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}=0
if
x_{i}\neq x_{j}
are linearly dependent for some
i\neq j
. Furthermore,
\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}=0
if
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}
are linearly dependent. Assume, that
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d}
are linearly independent. Let
\mathbf{y}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}a_{i,j}\mathbf{x}_{j}
for
i\in[d]
. Let
A=[a_{i,j}]\in\mathbb{F}^{d\times d}
. It is straightforward to show that
\mathbf{y}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{y}_{d}=(\det A)\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}
. This shows that the tensor
\mathbf{y}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{y}_{d}
is colinear with
\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}
.

Perform the Gram-Schmidt process without normalization on
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d}
to obtain
an orthogonal set of vectors
\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{d}
such that
y_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{y}_{d}=\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}
. Recall that
\|\mathbf{y}_{i}\|\leqslant\||\mathbf{x}_{i}\|
for
i\in[d]
.
As
\mathbf{y}_{1},\dots,y_{d}
is an orthogonal set if follows that

\|\mathbf{y}_{1}\|\cdots\|\mathbf{y}_{d}\|=\|\mathbf{y}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{y}_{d}\|=\|\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}\|\leqslant\|\mathbf{x}_{1}\|\cdots\|\mathbf{x}_{d}\|.

Assume that
\|\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}\|=1
. In the above formula we can renormalize that orthogonal vectors
z_{i}=t_{i}\mathbf{y}_{i},a_{i}>0,i\in[d]
such that
\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{d}
is an orthonormal basis in
\mathbf{W}
. Change the orthonormal basis
\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{d}
of
\mathbf{W}
to an orthonormal basis
\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{d}
in
\mathbf{W}
to deduce the last
statement of
(1)
.

(2)
Assume that
\mathcal{X}=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathcal{Y}=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{y}_{i}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\sigma)\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\eta)\mathcal{X}=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{\sigma(\eta(j))}=\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\sigma\eta)\mathcal{X},

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\sigma)\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\rangle=\prod_{j=1}\langle\mathbf{x}_{\sigma(j)},\mathbf{y}_{j}\rangle=\prod_{k=1}\langle\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{\sigma^{-1}(k)}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{X},\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\sigma^{-1})\mathcal{Y}\}\rangle.

For a general
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
write down
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{Y}
as a sum of rank-one matrices, and use the above equalities to deduce the first two equalities in (
2.21
).

Next observe

\|\mathrm{T}(\sigma)\mathcal{X}\|^{2}=\langle\mathrm{T}(\sigma)\mathcal{X},\mathrm{T}(\sigma)\mathcal{X}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{X},\mathrm{T}(\sigma^{-1})\mathrm{T}(\sigma)\mathcal{X}\rangle=\|\mathcal{X}\|^{2}.

This establishes the third equality in (
2.21
).

Let
\mathcal{Y}\in\Pi_{n^{\times^{d}}}
. Observe that
\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\sigma)\mathcal{X}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\sigma^{-1})\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X}\rangle
. Clearly,
\mathrm{T}_{d,n}(\sigma^{-1})\Pi_{n^{\times^{d}}}=\Pi_{n^{\times^{d}}}
. Use the characterization of
\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}
to deduce the fourth equality in (
2.21
). To deduce the last equality in (
2.21
) use the characterization of a norm
\nu^{*}
in terms of the norm
\nu
given in (
A.1
).

(3)
Observe that

(2.24)

\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}=\sqrt{d!}\,\mathrm{P}_{d,n}\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}.

Express
0\neq\mathcal{X}
as a sum of rank-one tensors to deduce that
\mathrm{P}_{d,n}\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
.
Observe that the equality (
2.23
) yields that
\mathrm{P}_{d,n}\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}=\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}
. Hence,
\mathrm{P}_{d,n}\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}
for
\mathcal{X}
is skew-symmetric. Assume that
\mathcal{Y}\in\otimes^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
is orthogonal to
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
. Hence,
\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X}\rangle=0
for
\mathcal{X}=\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}
. The equalities in (
2.21
) and (
2.23
) yield

\langle\mathrm{T}(\sigma)\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{X}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathrm{T}(\sigma^{-1})\mathcal{X}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathrm{sign}(\sigma^{-1})\mathcal{X}\rangle=0.

This shows that
\mathrm{T}(\sigma)\mathcal{Y}
is orthogonal to
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
. Hence,
\mathrm{P}_{n,d}
is an orthogonal projection on
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
.

The equalities last two equalities in (
2.21
) yield:

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{P}_{d,n}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}\leqslant\frac{1}{d!}\sum_{\sigma\in\Omega_{d}}\|\mathrm{T}(\sigma)\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}\leqslant 1,

\displaystyle\|\mathrm{P}_{d,n}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}\leqslant\frac{1}{d!}\sum_{\sigma\in\Omega_{d}}\|\mathrm{T}(\sigma)\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}\leqslant 1.

∎

Recall that the variety of all
d
-dimensional subspaces
\mathbf{W}
in
\mathbb{F}^{n}
is called Grassmannian Gr
(d,\mathbb{F}^{n})
. The span of all
\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}
is
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
, which is also denoted as
\bigwedge^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
. The following lemma is well know, and we bring its proof for completeness.

Lemma 2.6
.

(1)

The linear subspace
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
of
\mathbb{F}^{n^{\times d}}
has dimension
n\choose d
.

(2)

Let
\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{n}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{F}^{n}
. Then the set

\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{e}_{i_{d}}
for
1\leqslant i_{1}<\cdots<i_{d}\leqslant n

is an orthonormal basis of
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
.

(3)

Let
0\neq\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
. Then there exists a unique subspace
\mathbf{W}
of a minimal dimension
k\geqslant d
in
\mathbb{F}^{n}
such that
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbf{W}
.

Proof.

(1)+(2)
The proof is by induction on
d\geqslant 2
. For
d=2
a skew-symmetric 2-tensor correspond to a skew-symmetric matrix. Clearly,
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathbf{e}_{i}\mathbf{e}_{j}^{\top}-\mathbf{e}_{j}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{\top})
is an orthonormal basis for skew symmetric matrices. Assume the claim holds for
k,2\leqslant k<n-1
, and let
d=k+1
. Assume that
0\neq\mathcal{F}=[f_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}]\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
. Set

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\in[n]^{d}}f_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{e}_{i_{j}},

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{l}=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k})\in[n]^{k}}f_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k},l}\otimes_{j=1}^{k}\mathbf{e}_{i_{j}}.

Observe that
\mathcal{F}_{l}
can be viewed as a tensor in
\mathrm{A}^{k}\mathbb{F}
. The induction hypothesis yields that each
\mathcal{F}_{i}
is a linear combination of
\mathbf{e}_{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{e}_{i_{k}}
for
1\leqslant i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leqslant n
.
Observe that
\mathcal{F}=\sum_{l=1}^{n}\mathcal{F}_{l}\otimes\mathbf{e}_{l}
. Combine the above two observations and the fact that
\mathcal{F}
is a skew-symmetric tensor to deduce
(1)+(2)
.

(3)
. Unfold
\mathcal{F}
as a matrix
F
whose rows are
\mathcal{F}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{F}_{n}
. Then
\mathbf{W}
is the column space of
F
.
∎

Definition 2.7
.

(1)

For a given
k\in[n]
and
\mathbf{W}\in\mathrm{Gr}(k,\mathbb{F}^{n})
denote by
[\mathbf{W}]
the set of all vectors
\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{k}
, where
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{k}
is a basis in
\mathbf{W}
, and by

[\mathbf{W}]_{1}
the set of vectors
\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{k}
, where
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{k}
is an orthonormal basis in
\mathbf{W}
. Then
[\mathbf{W}]_{1}=\{\mathcal{Y}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}:\mathcal{Y}=\zeta\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{k},\zeta\in\mathbb{F},|\zeta|=1\}
for a fixed orthonormal basis
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{k}
in
\mathbf{W}
.

(2)

Let
0\neq\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
.
Then
\mathrm{frank}\,\mathcal{F}
is the minimal number of
\mathbf{W}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{W}_{r}\in\mathrm{Gr}(d,\mathbb{F}^{n})
such that
\mathcal{F}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{Y}_{i},\mathcal{Y}_{i}\in[\mathbf{W}_{i}],i\in[r]
.

The frank is called Slater rank in
[
3
]
.

Theorem 2.8
.

Assume that
0\neq\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
, where
n\geqslant d
. Then

(2.25)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{F}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=\max_{\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{d}\in\mathbb{F}^{n},\langle\mathbf{y}_{j},\mathbf{y}_{k}\rangle=\delta_{j,k},j,k\in[d]}\Re\langle\frac{1}{\sqrt{d!}}\mathbf{y}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{y}_{d},\mathcal{F}\rangle=

\displaystyle\max_{\mathcal{Y}\in[\mathbf{W}]_{1},\mathbf{W}\in\mathrm{Gr}(d,\mathbb{F}^{n})}\Re\langle\frac{1}{\sqrt{d!}}\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{F}\rangle,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{F}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\min_{\mathcal{F}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{1}{\sqrt{d!}}\mathcal{Y}_{i},\mathcal{Y}_{i}\in[\mathbf{W}_{i}]\setminus\{0\},\mathbf{W}_{i}\in\mathrm{Gr}(d,\mathbb{F}^{n}),r\leqslant r(n,d,\mathbb{F})}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\|\mathcal{Y}_{i}\|,

\displaystyle r(n,d,\mathbb{R})={n\choose d}+1,\quad r(n,d,\mathbb{C})=2{n\choose d}+1.

In particular, the restrictions of
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
on
\otimes^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
to
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
are conjugate norms.

Proof.

Recall that for
0\neq\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
the following characterization holds:

\|\mathcal{F}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=\max_{\mathbf{x}_{j}\in\mathbb{F}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}_{j}\|=1,j\in[d]}\Re\langle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathcal{F}\rangle=\langle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{y}_{j},\mathcal{F}\rangle>0,\quad\|\mathbf{y}_{j}\|=1,j\in[d].

Use the equality
\mathrm{P}_{d,n}\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}
, the second equaity in (
2.21
), and(
2.24
) to deduce

(2.26)

\displaystyle\langle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathcal{F}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d!}}\langle\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d},\mathcal{F}\rangle,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{F}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=\langle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathcal{F}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d!}}\langle\mathbf{y}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{y}_{d},\mathcal{F}\rangle.

As
\|\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}\|\leqslant 1
the above equality yields that
\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{d}
is an orthonormal set of vectors. This proves the first characterization in (
2.25
).

We next recall that
\|\mathcal{F}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\max_{\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}\leqslant 1,\mathcal{X}\in\otimes^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}}\Re\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F}\rangle
. As
\mathrm{P}_{d,n}
is an orthogonal projection on
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
we deduce
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{X},\mathrm{P}_{d,n}\mathcal{F}\rangle=\langle\mathrm{P}_{d,n}\mathcal{X},\mathcal{F}\rangle
. Assume that
\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}\leqslant 1
. Then
\mathcal{Y}=\mathrm{P}_{d,n}\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
, and part
(3)
of Lemma
2.5
yields that
\|\mathcal{Y}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}\leqslant\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}\leqslant 1
. Hence,

(2.27)

\|\mathcal{F}\|_{1,\infty}=\max_{\mathcal{Y}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n},\|\mathcal{Y}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}\leqslant 1}\Re\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{F}\rangle.

That is, the restrictions of
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
on
\otimes^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
to
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
, denoted as
\nu
and
\nu^{*}
respectivel,y are conjugate norms. on
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
.
As in the proof of Theorem
2.1
, the characterization (
2.25
) yields that the compact set of
\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{d!}}\mathcal{Y}:\mathcal{Y}\in[\mathbf{W}]_{1},\mathbf{W}\in\mathrm{Gr}(d,\mathbb{F}^{n})\}
is the set of the extreme points of the
\nu^{*}
. We deduce the characterization of
\|\mathcal{F}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
in (
2.25
) as in the proof of Theorem
2.1
.
∎

The first equality in (
2.25
) appears in
[
56
, Proposition 16]
.
The analog of the constants
\alpha_{s,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}
and
\beta_{s,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}
for
s=\infty,1
given in (
2.6
) are

(2.28)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},a,\mathbb{F}}=\min_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}},\quad\beta_{\infty,n^{\times d},a,\mathbb{F}}=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}},

\displaystyle\alpha_{1,n^{\times d},a,\mathbb{F}}=\min_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{F}^{n},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}},\quad\beta_{\infty,d,n\mathbb{F}}=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}.

The following theorem is an analog of Theorem
2.4
.

Theorem 2.9
.

Let
2\leqslant d<n
be integers. Then

(1)

The following inequalities and equalites hold.

(2.29)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},a,\mathbb{F}}<\beta_{\infty,n^{\times d},a,\mathbb{F}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d!}},

\displaystyle\alpha_{1,n^{\times d},a,\mathbb{F}}=\sqrt{d!}<\beta_{1,n^{\times d},a,\mathbb{F}}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},a,\mathbb{F}}}.

The equalites
\beta_{\infty,n^{\times d},a,\mathbb{F}}=\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}},\alpha_{1,n^{\times d},a,\mathbb{F}}=\|\mathcal{Y}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
achieved only for tensors of the form
\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}
, where
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d}
are orthonormal vectors in
\mathbb{F}^{n}
.

(2)

Assume that
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
and
\|\mathcal{F}\|=1
. Then
\|\mathcal{F}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=\alpha_{\infty,d,n,\mathbb{F}}
if and only if
\|\mathcal{F}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\beta_{1,d,n,\mathbb{F}}
.

(3)

For an even
n
:
\alpha_{\infty,2,n,\mathbb{F}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\,\;\beta_{1,2,n,\mathbb{F}}=\sqrt{n}
. Assume that
F
is an
n\times n
complex valued skew-symmetric matrix having Frobenius norm one:
\|F\|=1
. Then
\|F\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\beta_{1,2,n,\mathbb{F}}
if and only if

\sqrt{n}F
is a unitary matrix.

(4)

For an odd
n
:
\alpha_{\infty,2,n,\mathbb{F}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n-1}}\,\;\beta_{1,2,n,\mathbb{F}}=\sqrt{n-1}
. Assume that
G
is an
n\times n
complex valued skew-symmetric matrix having Frobenius norm one:
\|G\|=1
. Then
\|G\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\beta_{1,2,n,\mathbb{F}}
if and only if
G=F\oplus 0
and

\sqrt{n-1}F
is a unitary matrix.

Proof.

(1)

Recall that
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
has a following orthonormal basis:

(2.30)

\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{i_{d}},1\leqslant i_{1}<\cdots<i_{d}\leqslant n,\quad\langle\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij},i,j\in[n].

Let
\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{n}
be an orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{F}^{n}
.
Assume
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n},\|\mathcal{F}\|=1
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\sum_{1\leqslant i_{1}<\cdots<i_{d}\leqslant n}c_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}\mathbf{y}_{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{y}_{i_{d}},\sum_{1\leqslant i_{1}<\cdots<i_{d}\leqslant n}|c_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}|^{2}=1,

\displaystyle c_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}=\langle\mathbf{y}_{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{y}_{i_{d}},\mathcal{F}\rangle,\quad 1\leqslant i_{1}<\cdots<i_{d}\leqslant n.

Then
|\frac{1}{\sqrt{d!}}\langle\mathbf{y}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{y}_{d},\mathcal{F}\rangle|=\frac{|c_{1,\ldots,d}|}{\sqrt{d!}}|\leqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{d!}}
. Thus,
|\frac{1}{\sqrt{d!}}\langle\mathbf{y}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{y}_{d},\mathcal{F}\rangle|=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d!}}
if and only if
\mathcal{F}=\mathbf{z}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{z}_{d}
for some set of
d
orthonormal vectors
\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{d}
. This shows the first set of inequalities in (
2.29
).
The second set of inequalities follows from the equalities

\beta_{1,n^{\times d},a,\mathbb{F}}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},a,\mathbb{F}}}
and
\beta_{\infty,n^{\times d},a,\mathbb{F}}=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1,n^{\times d},a,\mathbb{F}}}
that follows from (
A.7
).

(2)
. Follows from (
A.9
).

(3)-(4)
. Follow from the observation that the determinant of an odd skew-symmetric matrix is zero
[
36
]
.

∎

2.4.
Nuclear rank

The notion of the nuclear rank was introduced in
[
22
]
, see also
[
6
]
:

Definition 2.10
.

Let
2\leqslant d\in\mathbb{N}
.

(1)

Let
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}
. Assume that
0\neq\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{F}^{\mathbf{n}}
.
Then the nuclear rank of
\mathcal{X}
, denoted as
\mathrm{nrank}\,\mathcal{X}
, is the minimal
r
such that

\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\|\mathcal{X}_{i}\|,\quad\mathcal{X}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{X}_{i},\mathcal{X}_{i}\in\Sigma_{\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}},i\in[r].

(2)

Let
0\neq\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{S}^{s}\mathbb{F}^{n}
. Denote by
\mathrm{snrank}\,\mathcal{S}
the minimal
r
such that

(2.31)

\mathcal{S}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\varepsilon_{i}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}_{i},\quad\mathbf{0}\in\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{F}^{n},\varepsilon_{i}=\pm 1,i\in[r],\quad\|\mathcal{S}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|^{r}.

(3)

Let
0\neq\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
. Denote by
\mathrm{fnrank}\,\mathcal{F}
the minimal
r
such that

(2.32)

\mathcal{F}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{1}{\sqrt{d!}}\mathcal{Y}_{i},\mathcal{Y}_{i}\in[\mathbf{W}_{i}]\setminus\{0\},\mathbf{W}_{i}\in\mathrm{Gr}(d,\mathbb{F}^{n}),\quad\|\mathcal{F}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\|\mathcal{Y}_{i}\|.

For a zero tensor all the above nuclear ranks are
0
.

Note that the ranks above depend on the field
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
.
Observe that the notion of the nuclear rank coincides with the notion of the
\nu
rank, where
\nu
is the nuclear norm on
\mathbb{F}^{n},\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n},\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
respectively, see subsection
A.1
.

Observe that for
d=2

(2.33)

\displaystyle\textrm{nrank}\,T=\rank T,\quad T\in\mathbb{F}^{m\times n},

\displaystyle\textrm{snrank}\,T=\rank T,\quad T\in\mathrm{S}^{2}\mathbb{F}^{n},

\displaystyle\nu\textrm{fnrank}\,T=\frac{\rank T}{2},\quad T\in\mathrm{A}^{2}\mathbb{F}^{n}.

Let

(2.34)

\displaystyle\textrm{maxnrank}(\mathbf{n}):=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{F}^{\mathbf{n}}}\textrm{nrank}\,\mathcal{X},

\displaystyle\textrm{maxsnrank}(n^{\times d}):=\max_{\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}}\textrm{snrank}\,\mathcal{S},

\displaystyle\textrm{maxfnrank}(d,n):=\max_{\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}}\textrm{fnrank}\,\mathcal{F}.

3.
Tensor formulations of entanglement

3.1.
Two measurements of entanglement

Assume that
\mathcal{H}
is a finite dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{F}
with an inner product
\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle
and the norm
\|\mathbf{x}\|=\sqrt{\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}\rangle}
. By considering a standard orthonormal basis
\mathbf{e}_{i}=(\delta_{1i},\ldots,\delta_{ni})^{\top},i\in[n]:=\{1,\ldots,n\}
we identify
\mathcal{H}
with
\mathbb{F}^{n}
. In the rest of this section we assume that
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
. Furthermore, when we omit the field
\mathbb{F}
, we assume that
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
.
Dirac’s notation for the standard basis in
\mathcal{H}
of dimension
n
is
|0\rangle,\ldots,|n-1\rangle
. Then
\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{x}|\mathbf{y}\rangle
, which corresponds to
\mathbf{x}^{\dagger}\mathbf{y}
in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. In quantum physics,
the set of states in
\mathcal{H}
is
\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{H})=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{H}:\|\mathbf{x}\|=1\}
. Two states
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{H})
are identical if
\mathbf{y}=z\mathbf{x}
for
z\in\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{C})=\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
Let
\mathcal{H}_{j}
be a Hilbert space of dimension
n_{j}
with a basis
\mathbf{e}_{1,j},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{n_{j},j}
for
j\in[d]
, where
d\geqslant 2
. Set
\mathcal{H}:=\otimes_{i=1}^{d}\mathcal{H}_{i}
, and
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{d})\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
. Then
\dim\mathcal{H}=N(\mathbf{n})
, and

\mathcal{H}\sim\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}:=\otimes_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n_{i}}
. A standard basis in
\mathcal{H}
in Dirac notation is
|(i_{1}-1)\cdots(i_{d}-1)\rangle,(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\in\mathbf{n}
.

A pure (unentanged) state is a rank-one tensor of norm one. The set of pure states is

\otimes_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{H}_{i})=\{\otimes_{i=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{i},\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|=1,i\in[d]\}=\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}
is the Segre variety of unentangled states in
\mathcal{H}

[
40
]
.
The rank of a state
\mathcal{X}

is the first simple measurement of entanglement:
\mathcal{X}
entangled if and only if
\rank\mathcal{X}\geqslant 2
. In
[
6
]
the authors discuss the rank of tensors in the context of entanglement. The problem with the quantity
\rank\mathcal{X}
is that it is not stable with respect to perturbation. In particular, every tensor can be approximated by a tensor of generic rank
[
6
]
.

The geometric measure of entanglement (GME) of a state
\mathcal{X}
is dist
(\mathcal{X},\otimes_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{H}_{i}))

[
52
,
3
,
54
]
. It is straightforward to show that dist
(\mathcal{X},\otimes_{i=1}^{d}\mathbb{S}(\mathcal{H}_{i}))=\sqrt{2(1-\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}})}
. For the bi-partite state the GME is
\sqrt{2(1-\sigma_{1}(X))}
. The maximal geometric entangled (MGE) state in
\mathcal{H}
is
\mathcal{X}^{\star}
corresponding to
\alpha_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{C}}
defined in (
2.6
).
It is well known that for the bi-partite case the MGE state is the Bell state:
X=U[m^{-1/2}I_{m}|0]V
, where
U,V
are unitary matrices, and
2\leqslant m\leqslant n
. That is,
\alpha_{\infty,(m,n),\mathbb{F}}=m^{-1/2}
.
In
[
20
]
the authors introduced the following measurement of entanglement
-2\log\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}
. It is zero if and only if a state
\mathcal{X}
is pure state, and otherwise is positive. It is shown in
[
27
]
and
[
20
]
that most states and boson states respectively are almost maximally entangled.

The nuclear norm of a state is another measure of entanglement. Namely, for a state
\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}
, express
\mathcal{X}
as a linear combination of
r
pure states:

\mathcal{X}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\mathcal{Y}_{i},
where
\mathcal{Y}_{i}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}
. The characterization (
2.3
) yields that
2\log\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}
is the minimal
energy
to create
\mathcal{X}
from pure states. Thus
2\log\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}\geqslant 0
and equality holds if and only
\mathcal{X}
is a pure state. Then inequality (
A.4
) yields that

(3.1)

2\log\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}\geqslant-2\log\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}},\quad\mathcal{X}\in\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathcal{H}_{j},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1.

The equality
\alpha_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}\beta_{1,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}=1
in (
2.7
) yields

(3.2)

\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}-2log\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}2log\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}=-2\log\alpha_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{C}}.

Furthermore, (
2.7
) implies that a GME state
\mathcal{X}
is also the most entangled with respect to the nuclear norm.

3.2.
Some known results on
\alpha_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}

The following lower and upper estimate are known, and we state them for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
:

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
2\leqslant d,2\leqslant n_{1}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant n_{d+1}
. Then

(3.3)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,(n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{d+1}),\mathbb{F}}\geqslant\frac{\alpha_{\infty,(n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{d}),\mathbb{F}}}{\sqrt{n_{d+1}}},

\displaystyle\beta_{1,(n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{d+1}),\mathbb{F}}\leqslant\sqrt{n_{d+1}}\beta_{1,(n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{d}),\mathbb{F}}.

In particular,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,(n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{d+1}),\mathbb{F}}\geqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{1}\cdots n_{d-1}}}

\displaystyle\beta_{1,(n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{d+1}),\mathbb{F}}\leqslant\sqrt{n_{1}\cdots n_{d-1}}.

Proof.

Assume that
\alpha_{\infty,(n_{1},\ldots,n_{d+1},\mathbb{F}}=\|\mathcal{T}^{\star}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}
. Write

\mathcal{T}^{\star}=a_{1}\mathcal{T}_{1}\otimes|0\rangle+\cdots+a_{n_{d+1}}\mathcal{T}_{n_{d+1}}\otimes|n_{d+1}-1\rangle,

where
{\mathcal{T}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{T}}_{n_{d+1}}\in\mathbb{F}^{(n_{1},\ldots,n_{d})}
are states, and
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n_{d+1}})^{\top}\in\mathbb{F}^{n_{d+1}}
has norm one. For some
i
we have
|a_{i}|\geqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{d+1}}}
.
There exist a pure state
{\mathcal{X}}\in\Pi_{(n_{1},\ldots,n_{d}),\mathbb{F}}
with
\|\mathcal{X}_{i}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=|\langle{\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\rangle|\geqslant\alpha_{\infty,(n_{1},\dots,n_{d}),\mathbb{F}}
. Hence,

\|{\mathcal{T}}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}\geqslant|\langle{\mathcal{X}}\otimes|i-1\rangle,{\mathcal{T}}\rangle|=|a_{i}|\cdot|\langle{\mathcal{X}},{\mathcal{T}}_{i}\rangle|\geqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{d+1}}}\alpha_{\infty,(n_{1},\dots,n_{d}),\mathbb{F}}.

The second inequality in (
3.3
) follows from the equality

\alpha_{\infty,(n_{1},\dots,n_{k}),\mathbb{F}}\beta_{1,(n_{1},\dots,n_{k}),\mathbb{F}}=1
for
k=n_{d+1},n_{d}
.
The inequalities (
3.4
) follow from (
2.11
) and (
3.3
).
∎

We now discuss the MGE 3-qubit, i.e.
n=2,d=3,\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
.
Consider the tensor the 3-tensor
\mathcal{W}

[
16
]
in Dirac’s notation:

(3.5)

{\mathcal{W}}=|W\rangle=\frac{|100\rangle+|010\rangle+|001\rangle}{\sqrt{3}}.

By Banach’s theorem, the spectral norm of
|W\rangle
is achieved on a pure symmetric state

|S\rangle=(x\langle 0|+y\langle 1|)^{\otimes 3}=(x\langle 0|+y\langle 1|)\otimes(x\langle 0|+y\langle 1|)\otimes(x\langle 0|+y\langle 1|)

with value

|\langle S|W\rangle|=|\sqrt{3}yx^{2}|=\sqrt{3}|y||x|^{2}.

where
x,y\in\mathbb{C}
with
|x|^{2}+|y|^{2}=1
. An easy calculus exercise shows that the maximum is achieved when
|x|=\sqrt{2}/\sqrt{3}
and
|y|=1/\sqrt{3}
. We obtain

\|{\mathcal{W}}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}=\sqrt{3}\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{3}}\right)^{2}\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}=\frac{2}{3}\mbox{ and }\|{\mathcal{W}}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}=\frac{3}{2}.

(See also
[
22
, Section 6]
.) It is shown in
[
9
]
that a quitrit state
\mathcal{T}\in\otimes^{3}\mathbb{C}^{2}
is the MGE qutrit if and only if it is locally untiary equivalent to
\mathcal{W}
.
The orbit of
\mathcal{W}
is defined as

\textrm{orb}(\mathcal{W})=\{\mathcal{T},\;\mathcal{T}=(A_{1}\otimes A_{2}\otimes A_{3})\mathcal{W},A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}\in\mathrm{U}_{2}\}.

Thus,
\mathcal{T}
is MGE if and only if
\mathcal{T}\in
orb
(\mathcal{W})
. In particular,

(3.6)

\alpha_{\infty,2^{\times 3},\mathbb{C}}=\alpha_{\infty,2^{\times 3},s,\mathbb{C}}=\frac{2}{3},\quad\beta_{1,2^{\times 3},\mathbb{C}}=\beta_{1,2^{\times 3},s,\mathbb{C}}=\frac{3}{2}.

A symmetric rank-one decomposition that achieves the nuclear norm of
\mathcal{W}
is:

{\mathcal{W}}=\frac{1}{6\sqrt{3}}\left[\Big(\textstyle\sqrt{2}\,|0\rangle+|1\rangle\Big)^{\otimes 3}+\zeta^{2}\Big(\textstyle\sqrt{2}\,|0\rangle+\zeta|1\rangle\Big)^{\otimes 3}+\zeta\Big(\textstyle\sqrt{2}\,|0\rangle+\zeta^{2}|1\rangle\Big)^{\otimes 3}\right],\zeta=\frac{-1+\sqrt{3}\mathbf{i}}{2}.

Combine Lemmas
3.1
with (
2.11
), (
3.6
)
and the equality
\alpha_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}\beta_{1,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{F}}=1
to deduce

Corollary 3.2
.

(3.7)

\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times(d+1)},\mathbb{F}}\geqslant\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},\mathbb{F}}=\quad\textrm{ for }d\geqslant 2.

In particular

(3.8)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},\mathbb{R}}\geqslant n^{\frac{1-d}{2}},\quad\beta_{1,n^{\times d},\mathbb{R}}\leqslant n^{\frac{d-1}{2}},\quad d\geqslant 2,

(3.9)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,2^{\times d},\mathbb{C}}\geqslant\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)2^{\frac{-(d-3)}{2}},\quad\beta_{1,2^{\times d},\mathbb{C}}\leqslant\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)2^{\frac{(d-3)}{2}}\quad d\geqslant 3.

We now show that the inequality (
3.8
) is sharp for
n=2
and each
d\geqslant 2
.

For
1\leqslant d\in\mathbb{N}
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{C}
with
|\lambda|=1
, we define a tensor

(3.10)

{\mathcal{T}}_{d,\lambda}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\lambda\Big(\frac{|0\rangle+i|1\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)^{\otimes d}+\overline{\lambda}\Big(\frac{|0\rangle-i|1\rangle}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)^{\otimes d}\right)

and we will use the convention
{\mathcal{T}}_{d}={\mathcal{T}}_{d,1}
. For example, we have

{\mathcal{T}}_{3}=\frac{|000\rangle-|110\rangle-|101\rangle-|011\rangle}{2},

{\mathcal{T}}_{4}=\frac{|0000\rangle-|1100\rangle-|1010\rangle-|1001\rangle-|0110\rangle-|0101\rangle-|0011\rangle+|1111\rangle}{2\sqrt{2}}

and

{\mathcal{T}}_{4,-i}=\frac{|1000\rangle+|0100\rangle+|0010\rangle+|0001\rangle-|1110\rangle-|1101\rangle-|1011\rangle-|0111\rangle}{2\sqrt{2}}.

As a complex tensor, the state
{\mathcal{T}}_{d,\lambda}
is not much entangled in the sense of the nuclear or spectral norm.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
{\mathcal{T}}_{d,\lambda}
be defined as above. Then

(3.11)

\|{\mathcal{T}}_{d,\lambda}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}=1/\sqrt{2},\quad\|{\mathcal{T}}_{d,\lambda}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}=\sqrt{2}.

Proof.

Clearly,
\mathcal{T}_{d,\lambda}
is symmetric :

(3.12)

\mathcal{T}_{d,\lambda}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\otimes^{d}\mathbf{u}+\otimes^{d}\bar{\mathbf{u}}),\quad\mathbf{u}=\frac{\lambda^{1/d}}{\sqrt{2}}(1,\mathbf{i})^{\top}.

Furthermore,
\mathbf{u}
and
\bar{\mathbf{u}}
is an orthonormal basis in
\mathbb{C}^{2}
. For
d\geqslant 2
view the tensor
\mathcal{T}_{d,\lambda}
as a matrix
T
of dimension
2^{d_{1}}\times 2^{d_{2}}
,
where
d_{1}=\lfloor\frac{d}{2}\rfloor,d_{2}=\lceil\frac{d}{2}\rceil
. Hence (
3.12
) is a singular value decomposition of
T
. Thus
\sigma_{1}(T)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}

and
\|T\|_{1}=\sqrt{2}
. Therefore,
\|\mathcal{T}_{d,\lambda}\|_{\infty}\leqslant\sigma_{1}(T)
and
\|\mathcal{T}_{d,\lambda}\|_{1}\geqslant\|T\|_{1}
. However, this singular value decomposition are realized by
left and right singular vectors which are rank one tensors. Hence (
3.11
) holds.∎∎

Theorem 3.4
.

For every mixed real
d
-qubit state
{\mathcal{T}}
we have
\|{\mathcal{T}}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\geqslant 2^{(1-d)/2}
and
\|{\mathcal{T}}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant 2^{(d-1)/2}
and these inequalities are tight when
{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{T}}_{d,\lambda}
. In particular, we have

\alpha_{\infty,2^{\times n},\mathbb{R}}=2^{(d-1)/2}\mbox{ and }\beta_{1,2^{\times d},\mathbb{R}}=2^{(1-d)/2}.

Proof.

The inequalities
\|{\mathcal{T}}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\geqslant 2^{(1-d)/2}
and
\|{\mathcal{T}}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant 2^{(d-1)/2}
are the inequalities (
3.8
) for
n=2
.

To calculate
\|{\mathcal{T}}_{n,\lambda}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}
, we use Banach’s theorem. We have to optimize

\Big\langle(x\langle 0|+y\langle 0|)^{\otimes d},{\mathcal{T}}_{d,\lambda}\Big\rangle=\sqrt{2}\left|\Re\left(\lambda\Big(\frac{x+iy}{\sqrt{2}}\Big)^{d}\right)\right|

under the constraint
x^{2}+y^{2}=1
. The optimal value clearly is equal to
2^{(1-d)/2}
which shows that
\|{\mathcal{T}}_{d,\lambda}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}=2^{(1-d)/2}
.
It follows now that
{\mathcal{T}}_{d,\lambda}
also has an optimal nuclear norm, which must be equal to
2^{(d-1)/2}
.
∎

3.3.
The most geometric entangled 4-qubit

The following theorem was proven in
[
14
]
. We bring its detailed proof for reader’s convenience.

Theorem 3.5
.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{4}=|M_{4}\rangle
be the Higuchi-Sudbery state
[
34
]
:

(3.13)

\mathcal{M}_{4},=\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}\big(|0011\rangle+|1100\rangle+\zeta(|1010\rangle+|0101\rangle)+\zeta^{2}(|1001\rangle+|0110\rangle\big),\;\zeta=e^{2\pi\mathbf{i}/3}.

Then
\|\mathcal{M}_{4}\|_{\infty}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{3}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\alpha_{\infty,2^{\times 3},\mathbb{C}}
. Hence

(3.14)

\alpha_{\infty,2^{\times 4},\mathbb{C}}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{3},\quad\beta_{1,2^{\times 4},\mathbb{C}}=\frac{3}{\sqrt{2}},

and
\mathcal{M}_{4}
is the most entangled state.

Proof.

Let

(3.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(|110\rangle+\zeta|101\rangle+\zeta^{2}|011\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}(|001\rangle+\zeta|010\rangle+\zeta^{2}|100\rangle).

Let
\phi_{3}:\mathrm{U}_{2}\to\otimes^{3}\mathrm{U}_{2}
be the diagonal map
A\mapsto\otimes^{3}A
on the unitary
2\times 2
matrices. So
\phi_{3}(\mathrm{U}_{2})
acts on
3
-quibits.
Observe that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{4}=\mathcal{W}_{0}\otimes|0\rangle+\mathcal{W}_{1}\otimes|1\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{1}=(V_{1}\otimes V_{2}\otimes I_{2})\mathcal{W},\quad V_{1}=\diag(1,\zeta^{2}),V_{2}=\diag(1,\zeta)\Rightarrow\|\mathcal{W}_{1}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}=\frac{2}{3},

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{0}=\phi_{3}(T)\mathcal{W}_{1},\quad T=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}\Rightarrow\|\mathcal{W}_{0}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}=\frac{2}{3}.

We claim that the two dimensional space
\mathbf{W}=
span
(\mathcal{W}_{0},\mathcal{W}_{1})
is invariant under the action
\phi_{3}(\mathrm{U}_{2})
. Consider first:

(3.16)

(\otimes^{3}A)\mathcal{W}_{0}=ab^{2}\mathcal{W}_{0},(\otimes^{3}A)\mathcal{W}_{1}=a^{2}b\mathcal{W}_{1},\quad A=\diag(a,b),|a|=|b|=1.

Hence
\phi_{3}(A)\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{W}
. It is left to show that
\mathbf{W}
is invariant under the action of
\phi_{3}(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{U}_{2})
. Hence, it is enough to show
\mathbf{W}
is invariant under the action of Lie group of
\phi_{3}(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{U}_{2})
.
The generators of the
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{U}_{2}
are
i
times the Pauli matrices:

(3.17)

B_{1}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}i&0\\
0&-i\end{array}\right],B_{2}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
-1&0\end{array}\right],B_{3}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&i\\
i&0\end{array}\right].

The generators of
\phi_{3}(\mathrm{S}\mathrm{U}_{2})
are

(3.18)

C_{j}=B_{j}\otimes I_{2}\otimes I_{2}+I_{2}\otimes B_{j}\otimes I_{2}+I_{2}\otimes I_{2}\otimes B_{j},\quad j\in[3].

We need to show that
C_{j}\mathcal{W}_{k}\in\mathbf{W}
for
j\in[3]
and
k=0,1
.
In view of (
3.16
) it is enough to show the above containments for
j=2,3
.
Assume that
k=0
.
Observe

\displaystyle C_{2}(|110\rangle)=|010\rangle+|100\rangle-|111\rangle,

\displaystyle C_{2}\zeta|101\rangle=\zeta(|001\rangle-|111\rangle+|100\rangle),

\displaystyle C_{2}\zeta^{2}|011\rangle=\zeta^{2}(-|111\rangle+|001\rangle+|010\rangle).

As
1+\zeta+\zeta^{2}=0

C_{2}\mathcal{W}_{0}=(\zeta+\zeta^{2})\mathcal{W}_{1}

Observe next

\displaystyle\bar{i}C_{3}(|110\rangle=|010\rangle+|100\rangle+|111\rangle,

\displaystyle\bar{i}C_{3}\zeta|101\rangle=\zeta(|001\rangle+|111\rangle+|100\rangle,

\displaystyle\bar{i}C_{3}\zeta^{2}|011\rangle=\zeta^{2}(|111\rangle+|001\rangle+|010\rangle)

Hence

C_{3}\mathcal{W}_{0}=\mathbf{i}(\zeta+\zeta^{2})\mathcal{W}_{1}

Hence,
\phi_{3}(A)\mathcal{W}_{0}\in\mathbf{W}
for any
A\in\mathrm{U}_{2}
. Observe next that
\phi_{3}(T)\phi(A)=\phi(B)\phi_{2}(T)
, where
B=TAT

As
\phi_{3}(T)\mathcal{W}_{0}=\mathcal{W}_{1}
we deduce that
C_{j}\mathcal{W}_{1}\in\mathbf{W}
for
j\in[3]
.
Hence,
\mathbf{W}
invariant under the action
\phi_{3}(\mathrm{U}_{2})
.

Furthermore, the action of
\phi_{3}(\mathrm{U}_{2})
on
\mathbf{W}
is identical to the action of
\mathrm{U}_{2}
on the two dimensional subspace
\mathbf{W}
, with respect to the orthogonal
basis
\mathcal{W}_{0},\mathcal{W}_{1}
. That is, given a state
(a,b)^{\top}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
, there exists
A\in\mathrm{U}_{2}

such that
\phi_{3}(A)\mathcal{W}_{0}=a\mathcal{W}_{0}+b\mathcal{W}_{1}
. Hence

\|a\mathcal{W}_{0}+b\mathcal{W}_{1}\|_{\infty}=\|\phi_{3}(A)\mathcal{W}_{0}\|_{\infty}=\frac{2}{3}\quad\textrm{ for }|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}=1.

Observe that
