\mathcal{M}_{4}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\mathcal{W}_{0}\otimes|0\rangle+\mathcal{W}_{1}\otimes|1\rangle\right).

Let
\mathcal{X}=\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{y}\otimes\mathbf{u}\otimes\mathbf{v}=\mathcal{Y}\otimes\mathbf{v}
, where
\|\mathbf{x}\|=\|\mathbf{y}\|=\|\mathbf{u}\|=\|\mathbf{v}\|=1
, be a product state. Then

\sqrt{2}|\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{M}_{4}\rangle|=|\langle\mathcal{Y},a\mathcal{W}_{0}+b\mathcal{W}_{1}\rangle|,\quad a=\langle\mathbf{v},|0\rangle\rangle,b=\langle\mathbf{v},|1\rangle\rangle.

Clearly
|a|^{2}+|b|^{2}=\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2}=1
. Thus if we maximize on all product states
\mathcal{Y}
and keep
\mathbf{v}
fixed we get this this maximum is

\|a\mathcal{W}_{0}+b\mathcal{W}_{1}\|_{\infty}=\frac{2}{3}
. This shows that
\|\mathcal{M}_{4}\|_{\infty}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{3}
. The inequaltity (
3.9
) for
d=4

yields that
\alpha_{\infty,2^{\times 4},\mathbb{C}}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{3}
. This equality yields the second equality in (
3.14
). Furthermore,
\mathcal{M}_{4}
is the most entangled

4
-qubit.
∎

Numerical simulaitons point out that that (
3.9
) is not sharp for
d=5
.

3.4.
Entanglements of
d
-qubits for big
d

Inequality (
3.9
) yields that

-2\log_{2}\alpha_{\infty,2^{\times d}}\leqslant d-5+2\log_{2}3\textrm{ for }d\geqslant 3.

The concentration result of
[
27
]
claims that most of
d
-qubits with respect to the corresponding Haar measure, satisfy the inequality

-2\log_{2}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}\geqslant d-2\log_{2}d-3\textrm{ for most of }\mathcal{T}\in\otimes^{d}\mathbb{C}^{2}\textrm{ for }d\gg 1.

Hence, for a big
d
the ratio
\frac{\alpha_{\infty,2^{\times(d+1)},\mathbb{C}}}{\alpha_{\infty,2^{\times d},\mathbb{C}}}
approached to
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
.

Recall that the distance of a Boson
\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
to product rank-one state is
\sqrt{2(1-\|\mathcal{S}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}})}
.
Then
\mathcal{S}
is a MGE Boson if
\|\mathcal{S}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}=\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{C}}
, see (
2.17
).
Theorem
2.4
states that
\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times 2},s,\mathbb{F}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
.
As
|W\rangle
is a symmetric 3-qubit, we deduce that
\alpha_{\infty,2^{\times 3},s,\mathbb{C}}=\frac{2}{3}
.
The most geometric entangled symmetric
4
-qubit is conjectured to be
[
1
, Example 6.1]

\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}|0000\rangle+\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}(|0111\rangle+|1011\rangle+|1101\rangle+|1110\rangle).

Its spectral norm is
\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\approx 0.5774

[
1
]
.

We claim that

(3.19)

\frac{1}{\sqrt{{n+d-1\choose d}}}\leqslant\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{C}},\quad\beta_{1,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{C}}\leqslant\sqrt{{n+d-1\choose d}},\quad 2\leqslant d,n.

The first inequality is shown in
[
20
, Eq, (3.1)]
. The second inequality follows from part
(1)
of Theorem
2.4
. (We reprove this inequality in part
(4)
of Corollary
4.12
.) Hence

-2\log_{2}\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{C}}\leqslant\log_{2}{n+d-1\choose d}.

Furthermore,
[
20
, Eq. (1.2)]
shows:

-2\log_{2}\|\mathcal{S}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}\geqslant\log_{2}{n+d-1\choose d}-\log_{2}\log_{2}{n+d-1\choose d}-3\log_{2}n-1,\textrm{ for most }\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}\textrm{ for }d\gg 1.

It seems to us the correct version of the inequality (
3.3
) for
\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{C}}
is:

(3.20)

\alpha_{\infty,2^{\times(d+1)},s,\mathbb{C}}=\frac{\sqrt{d+1}}{\sqrt{d+n}}\left(1+O(1/d)\right)\alpha_{\infty,2^{\times d},s,\mathbb{C}}\textrm{ for }d\gg 1.

4.
Separabilty

4.1.
Density tensors and separabilty

Definition 4.1
.

Let
d,n_{1},\ldots,n_{d}\in\mathbb{N}
. Set
\mathbf{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{d})
. A tensor
\mathcal{B}=[b_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d},j_{1},\ldots,j_{d}}]\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}\times\mathbf{n}}:=\mathbb{C}^{(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n})}=\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}
is called Hermitian

b_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d},j_{1},\ldots,j_{d}}=\overline{b_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{d},i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})}}\textrm{ for all }(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}),(j_{1},\ldots,j_{d})\in[\mathbf{n}].

Denote by
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}\times\mathbf{n}}
the real space of Hermitian tensors.
One can view
\mathcal{B}
as a Hermitian matrix
B=[b_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}]\in\mathrm{H}_{N(\mathbf{n})}
,
where
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}),\mathbf{j}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{d})\in[n_{d}]
. (Thus,
\mathrm{H}_{N(\mathbf{n})}
is unfolding of
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
.)

A Hermitian tensor is veiwed a a linear transformation
\mathcal{B}:\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}\to\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}
:

(\mathcal{B}\mathcal{X})_{\mathbf{i}}=\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in[\mathbf{n}]}b_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}x_{\mathbf{j}},\quad\mathbf{i}\in[\mathbf{n}],\mathcal{X}=[x_{\mathbf{j}}]\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}.

It has the following spectral decomposition:

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}=\sum_{k=1}^{N(\mathbf{n})}\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{B})\mathcal{X}_{k}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}_{k}},

\displaystyle\lambda_{\max}(\mathcal{B})=\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{B})\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{N(\mathbf{n})}(\mathcal{B})=\lambda_{\min}(\mathcal{B}),

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{B})=(\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{B}),\ldots,\lambda_{N(\mathbf{n})}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}\mathcal{X}_{k}=\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{B})\mathcal{X}_{k},\quad\langle\mathcal{X}_{k},\mathcal{X}_{l}\rangle=\delta_{kl},k,l\in[N(\mathbf{n})].

Let

\displaystyle tr\mathcal{B}=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\in[\mathbf{n}]}b_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d},i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}=\sum_{k=1}^{N(\mathbf{n})}\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{B}),

\displaystyle\tr_{k}\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}_{k}=[b^{(k)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},i_{k+1},\ldots,i_{d},j_{1},\ldots,j_{k-1},j_{k+1},\ldots,j_{d}}]\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}_{k}\times\mathbf{n}_{k}},\mathbf{n}_{k}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k-1},n_{k+1},\ldots,n_{d}),

\displaystyle b^{(k)}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},i_{k+1},\ldots,i_{d},j_{1},\ldots,j_{k-1},j_{k+1},\ldots,j_{d}}=\sum_{i_{k}=1}^{n_{k}}b_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d},j_{1},\ldots,j_{k-1},i_{k},j_{k+1},\ldots,j_{d}},\quad k\in[d].

The set of positive semi-definite Hermitian tensors, denoted as
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+}
corresponds to
\mathrm{H}_{N(\mathbf{n}),+}
. The set of positive semi-definite Hermitian tensors with trace one is denoted by
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}
, is the convex set of density tensors.
We define the inner product on
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
by

\langle\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}\rangle=\sum_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in[\mathbf{n}]}\overline{b_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}}c_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}
, which is real valued.
One associates with a Hermitian tensor
\mathcal{B}
a multi-sesquilinear form

(4.2)

\displaystyle\langle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathcal{B}(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j})\rangle=\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}),(j_{1},\ldots,j_{d})\in[\mathbf{n}]}b_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d},j_{1},\ldots,j_{d}}\prod_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\in[\mathbf{n}]}\bar{x}_{i_{1},1}x_{j_{1},1}\cdots\bar{x}_{i_{d},d}x_{j_{d},d},

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}_{k}=(x_{1,k},\ldots,x_{n_{k},k})^{\top}\in\mathbb{C}^{n_{k}},k\in[d].

The notion of Hermitian tensor for
d=2
was introduced in
[
22
]
, and was called there bi-Hermitian.

Recall that the operator norm on
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
and its dual are the Schatten norms

\|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{B})\|_{\infty}
and
\|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{B})\|_{1}
.
Furthermore,

(4.3)

\displaystyle|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{B})\|_{\infty}=\max(\lambda_{\max}(\mathcal{B}),-\lambda_{\min}(\mathcal{B}))=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}|\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}\mathcal{X}\rangle|,

\displaystyle|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{B})\|_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{N(\mathbf{n})}|\lambda_{i}(\mathcal{B})|.

Hence, the extreme points of the Schatten norm
\|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{B})\|_{1}

are
\pm\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}
for
\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1
.

We define the spectral and the nuclear norms on
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
as follows:

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
2\leqslant d\in\mathbb{N},2\mathbf{1}_{d}\leqslant\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
. Assume that
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
. Let

(4.4)

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}:=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}}|\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}\mathcal{X}\rangle|.

Then
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}
is a norm on
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
. Furthermore,

(1)

(4.5)

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}\leqslant\|\mathcal{B}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}\leqslant\|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{B})\|_{\infty}.

Equality holds in the first inequaity holds if
\pm\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+}
. Equality in the second inequality holds if and only if
\mathcal{B}
has an eigenvector
\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}
which corresponds to an eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{B})
such that
|\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{B})|=\|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{B})\|_{\infty}
. In particular,
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}=\|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{B})\|_{\infty}\iff\|\mathcal{B}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}=\|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{B})\|_{\infty}
.

(2)

Let
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}
be the dual norm of
\|\cdot\|_{spec}
:

(4.6)

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}=\max_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{C}\|_{spec}=1}\langle\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}\rangle.

The extreme points of
\mathrm{B}_{\|\cdot\|_{nuc}}(0,1)
are
\pm\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}
. Hence,

(4.7)

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}=\min_{\mathcal{X}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\mathcal{X}_{i}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}_{i}},a_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\mathcal{X}_{i}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}},i\in[r],r\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{r}|a_{i}|.

Furthermore, the minimum is achieved for some
r\leqslant N^{2}(\mathbf{n})+1
.

(3)

The following inequalities hold:

(4.8)

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}\geqslant\|\mathcal{B}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}\geqslant\|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{B})\|_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{N(\mathbf{n})}|\lambda_{i}(\mathcal{B})|\geqslant|\tr\mathcal{B}|.

(4)

Denote

(4.9)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,\mathbf{n},h}=\min_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{spec},\quad\beta_{\infty,\mathbf{n},h}=\max_{\mathcal{X},\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{spec},

\displaystyle\alpha_{1,\mathbf{n},h}=\min_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{nuc},\quad\beta_{1,\mathbf{n},h}=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,nuc}.

Then

(4.10)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,\mathbf{n},h}<\beta_{\infty,\mathbf{n},h}=1,\quad\alpha_{1,\mathbf{n},h}=1<\beta_{1,\mathbf{n},h},

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,\mathbf{n},h}\beta_{1,\mathbf{n},h}=1.

(5)

Let
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}},\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{m}}
. Then
\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{H}_{(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m})}
. Moreover,

(4.11)

\|\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}\|_{spec}=\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}\|\mathcal{C}\|_{spec},\quad\|\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}\|_{nuc}=\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}\|\mathcal{C}\|_{nuc}.

(6)

The group
\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{n}}
acts on
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto((\otimes_{j=1}^{d}U_{j})\mathcal{X})\otimes\overline{((\otimes_{j=1}^{d}U_{j})\mathcal{X})},

\displaystyle(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j})\otimes(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{j})\mapsto(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}U_{j}\mathbf{x}_{j})\otimes(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\overline{U_{j}\mathbf{x}_{j}})

as a group of isometries of the norms
\|\cdot\|_{spec}
and
\|\cdot\|_{nuc}
.
Furthermore this action preserves the trace.
Let
\mu_{\mathbf{n}}
be the Haar measure on
\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{n}}
. Then for any
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}
the following equality holds:

(4.12)

\int_{\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{n}}}((\otimes_{j=1}^{d}U_{j})\mathcal{R}\overline{(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}U_{j})}d\mu_{\mathbf{n}}=\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}\otimes_{j=1}^{d}I_{n_{j}}.

Proof.

It is clear that we have the following three properties of a norm:

\displaystyle 0\leqslant\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec},

\displaystyle\|b\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}=|b|\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}\textrm{ for }b\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{B}_{1}+\mathcal{B}_{2}\|_{spec}\leqslant\|\mathcal{B}_{1}\|_{spec}+\|\mathcal{B}_{2}\|_{spec}.

Assume that
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}=0
. Let
0\neq\mathcal{X}=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}
. Then
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}\mathcal{X}\rangle
is a real multi-sesquilinear form which is zero identically.
We prove by induction on
d
that
\mathcal{B}=0
. If
d=1
,
then
\mathcal{B}=0
as
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}
is the spectral norm of a Hermitian matrix. Assume that the induction hypothesis holds for
d=m
and let
d=m+1
. Fix
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{m+1}\neq\mathbf{0}
. Then
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}\mathcal{X}\rangle
is a real sesquilinear form which is zero identically. Hence, we obtain
n_{m+1}^{2}
real sesquilinear forms in
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{m}
which identically zero.
The induction hypothesis implies that
\mathcal{B}=0
.

(1)
Observe that
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}\times\mathbf{n}}
. Hence

(4.13)

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}=\max_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}}\Re\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}\mathcal{X}\rangle=\max_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}}|\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}\mathcal{X}\rangle|.

Use the maximal characterizations (
4.3
), (
4.13
) and (
4.4
) of

|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{B})\|_{\infty}
,
\|\mathcal{B})\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}
and
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}
respectively, and the inclusion
\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}\subset\mathbb{S}(\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}})
to deduce (
4.5
). The equality case in the first inequality for a positive semi-definite
\mathcal{B}
follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

|\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}\mathcal{X}\rangle|^{2}\leqslant\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}\mathcal{Y}\rangle\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}\mathcal{X}\rangle\Rightarrow\|\mathcal{B}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}}\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{B}\mathcal{X}\rangle\textrm{ for }\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+}.

Same inequaity holds if
-\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+}
.

Assume the equality
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{\infty,\mathbf{n},\mathbb{C}}=\|\langle\mathcal{Y}^{\star},\mathcal{B}\mathcal{X}^{\star}\rangle=\|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{B})\|_{\infty}
for some
\mathcal{X}^{\star},\mathcal{Y}^{\star}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}
. Then
\mathcal{Y}^{*}=z\mathcal{X}^{\star}
, and
\mathcal{X}^{\star}
is an eigenvector
\mathcal{B}:\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}\to\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}
with eigenvalue
\lambda
such that
|\lambda|=\|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{B})\|_{\infty}
. Vice versa, assume that
\mathcal{B}
has an eigenvector
\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}
which corresponds to an eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{B})
such that
|\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{B})|=\|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{B})\|_{\infty}
.
Then
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}\geqslant\|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{B})\|_{\infty}
which yields equalities in (
4.5
).

(2)
The characterization (
4.4
) yields that the set of extreme points of the unit ball of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{nuc}
is contained in the set
\mathrm{E}:=\{\pm\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}:\,\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}\}
. Observe that
\mathrm{E}
is compact and contained in the set of the extreme points of the Euclidean norm in
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}\times\mathbf{n}}
. Hence,
\mathrm{E}
is the set of the extreme points of the unit ball of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{nuc}
. Therefore, as in the proof of the characterization of
\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
in (
2.3
) we deduce the characterization (
4.7
).
As
\dim\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}=\dim\mathrm{H}_{N(\mathbf{n})}=N^{2}(\mathbf{n})
, Caratheodory’s theorem yields that in the characterization (
4.7
) we can assume that
r\leqslant N^{2}(\mathbf{n})+1
.

(3)
Consider the minimal characterizations of the norms
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}
and
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{1,nuc}
given by (
2.3
) for
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}

and by (
4.7
) respectively. As the set of the extremal points of the unit ball
\|\cdot\|_{nuc}
is contained the of the extremal points of the unit ball
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}
we deduce the first inequality in (
4.8
). The second inequality in (
4.8
)
is deduced similarly. The last inequality in (
4.8
) follows from the first equality after (
4.1
).

(4)
Straightforward.

(5)
Given
\mathcal{B}=[b_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}]\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}},\mathcal{C}=[c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{l}}]\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{m}}
, we define
\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{D}=[d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}}]\in\mathrm{H}_{(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m})}
by the equality
d_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{k},\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}}:=b_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}c_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{l}}
.
It is straightforward to show that if
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}},\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{m}}
then
\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{H}_{(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{m})}
. The proof of equality (
4.11
) is similar to the proof of the equality (
2.9
).

(6)
Assume that
\mathcal{R}=(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j})\otimes(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{j})
, where
\|\mathbf{x}_{j}\|=1,j\in[d]
. Clearly,
1=\tr\mathcal{R}=\tr(\otimes_{j=1}U_{j})\mathcal{R}\overline{(\otimes_{j=1}U_{j})}
. As the extreme points of the unit ball
\|\cdot\|_{nuc}
are
\pm\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{n^{\times d}}
it follows that the action of
\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{n}}
on
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
preserve the trace.

Observe that for
d=1
the equality

\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n}}(U\mathbf{x})(U\mathbf{x})^{*}d\mu_{n}=\frac{1}{n}I_{n}
for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1
follows from the decomposition
\frac{1}{n}I_{n}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{y}_{i}\mathbf{y}_{i}^{*}
, where
\langle\mathbf{y}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j}\rangle=\delta_{i,j},i,j\in[n]
. As
d\mu_{\mathbf{n}}=\prod_{j=1}^{d}\mu_{n_{j}}
we deduce the equality (
4.12
). Assume now that
\mathcal{R}
is any density tensor
in
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}
. Let
\mathcal{Q}=\int_{\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{n}}}((\otimes_{j=1}^{d}U_{j})\mathcal{R}\overline{(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}U_{j})}d\mu_{\mathbf{n}}
. Observe the following equalities for
\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}
:

\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{X}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{Q}\rangle=\langle\int_{\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{n}}}(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}U_{j})\mathcal{X})\otimes\overline{(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}U_{j})\mathcal{X})},\mathcal{R}\rangle=\langle\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}\otimes_{j=1}^{d}I_{n_{j}},\mathcal{R}\rangle=\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}.

That is, the sesquilinear form
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Q}\mathcal{X}\rangle
is constant on
\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}
, which is equal to
\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}
. Use an induction on
d
to deduce (
4.12
).
∎

The set
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}
corresponds to the density tensors on the space
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}}
.
A density tensor
\rho\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}
is called a product density tensor if

\rho=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\rho_{j}
, where
\rho_{j}\in\mathrm{H}_{n_{j}},j\in[d]
. Viewing this tensor product as a Kronecker tensor product, we deduce that
\rank\rho
, viewed as a matrix in
\mathrm{H}_{N(\mathbf{n})}
is equal to
\prod_{j=1}^{d}\rank\rho_{j}
.
A density tensor of the form
\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}
is called a rank-one density tensor.
For each
\rho_{j}
write down its spectral decomposition. It now follows that a product density tensor is a convex combination of at most
N(\mathbf{n})
rank-one product density.

Definition 4.3
.

The set of convex combinations of rank-one product density tensors in
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}
is called the set of separable density tensors, and denoted by
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
.

The set of separable density tensors
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
is considered to be an analog of unentangled states see
[
55
]
.

Theorem 4.4
.

Let
1\leqslant d\in\mathbb{N}
and
2\leqslant n_{1},\ldots,n_{d}
.
Then

(1)

A density tensor
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}
satisfies the inequalities

(4.14)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}\leqslant\|\mathcal{R}\|_{spec}\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})\leqslant 1,

\displaystyle 1\leqslant\|\mathcal{R}\|_{nuc}\leqslant\beta^{\prime}_{1,\mathbf{n},h}:=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}\|_{nuc}\leqslant\beta_{1,\mathbf{n},h}.

Equalities in some of the above inequalities hold under the following conditions:

(4.15)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}\|_{spec}=\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})\iff\exists\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}\textrm{ such that }\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}_{k}\rangle=0\textrm{ if }\lambda_{k}<\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R}),

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}\|_{spec}=1\iff\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}},

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N(d)}=\|\mathcal{R}\|_{spec}\iff\mathcal{R}=\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}\otimes^{d}_{j=1}I_{n_{j}},

\displaystyle 1=\|\mathcal{R}\|_{nuc}\iff\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}\|_{nuc}=\beta^{\prime}_{1,\mathbf{n},h}\Leftarrow\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1,\|\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}\|_{1,\mathbf{n},h}=\beta^{\prime}_{1,\mathbf{n},h}.

(2)

The set
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
is a maximal face of the unit ball of
\|\cdot\|_{nuc}
of the maximal dimension
N^{2}(\mathbf{n})-1
. Its supporting hyperplane is

(4.16)

\tr\mathcal{B}=\langle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}I_{n_{j}},\mathcal{B}\rangle\leqslant 1\textrm{ for }\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}\leqslant 1.

Equality holds if and only if
\mathcal{B}
is separable.
In particular, any separable density tensor is a convex combinations of at most
N^{2}(\mathbf{n})
rank-one density tensors.

Proof.

(1)

Let
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}
. Consider the spectral decomposition
\mathcal{R}=\sum_{k\in[N(\mathbf{n})]}\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{R})\mathcal{X}_{k}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}_{k}}
. Then
\lambda_{i}(\mathcal{R})\geqslant 0
for
i\in[N(\mathbf{n})
and
\tr\mathcal{R}=\sum_{i=1}^{N(\mathbf{n})}\lambda_{i}(\mathcal{R})=1
.
Thus, for
\|\mathcal{X}\|=1
one has the inequalities

|\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{R}\mathcal{X}\rangle|=\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{R}\mathcal{X}\rangle=\sum_{k\in[N(\mathbf{n})]}\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{R})|\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}_{k}\rangle|^{2}\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})\sum_{k\in[N(\mathbf{n})]}|\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}_{k}\rangle|^{2}=\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})\leqslant 1.

This shows the last two inequalities in the first inequality in (
4.14
).
Assume that
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{spec}=\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})
. Then, there exists
\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}
such that
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{spec}=\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})=\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})\langle\mathcal{X},\rangle\mathcal{R}\mathcal{X}\rangle
. This equality holds if and only if that
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}_{k}\rangle=0
for
\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{R})<\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})
. This show that first equality in (
4.15
).

Assume that
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{spec}=1
then
\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})=1
, and
\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{R})=0
for
k>1
. Now use the implications for the equality
\|\mathcal{R}\|=\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})
to deduce the second equality case in (
4.15
).

Let
t=\min_{\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}}\|\mathcal{R}\|_{spec}=\|\mathcal{R}^{\star}\|_{spec}
. As
\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{n}}
acts as a group of isometries on
\|\cdot\|_{sep}
it follows that
\|U\mathcal{R}^{\star}\bar{U}\|_{spec}=t
for each
U\in\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{n}}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}=\|\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}\otimes_{j=1}^{d}I_{n_{j}}\|_{spec}=\|\int_{\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{n}}}((\otimes_{j=1}^{d}U_{j})\mathcal{R}\overline{(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}U_{j})}d\mu_{\mathbf{n}}\|_{spec}\leqslant

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{n}}}\|((\otimes_{j=1}^{d}U_{j})\mathcal{R}\overline{(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}U_{j})}\|d\mu_{\mathbf{n}}=t

This establishes the lower bound in the first inequality of (
4.14
). We now discuss the equality case.
Assume that
\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}=\|\mathcal{R}^{\star}\|_{spec}=\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{R}^{\star}\mathcal{X}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{R}\rangle
for some
\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}
. The equality case in the above inequality yields that for each
U\in\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{n}}
one has the equality
\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}=\langle(U\mathcal{X}),\mathcal{R}^{\star}(U\mathcal{X})\rangle
. That is, the sesqulinear form
\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{R}^{\star}\mathcal{Y}\rangle
has value
\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}
on
\Pi_{\mathbf{n}}
. As in the proof of part
(5)
of Lemma
4.2
we deduce that
\mathcal{R}^{\star}=\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}\otimes_{j=1}^{d}I_{n_{j}}
.

We now discuss the inequalities for
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{nuc}
in (
4.14
).
Use the decomposition of
\mathcal{R}
, and the triangle inequality to deduce

\|\mathcal{R}\|_{nuc}\leqslant\sum_{k\in[N(\mathbf{n})]}\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{R})\|\mathcal{X}_{k}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}_{k}}\|_{nuc}\leqslant\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbf{n}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}\|_{nuc}=\beta^{\prime}_{1,\mathbf{n},h}.

The definition (
4.14
) yields that
\beta^{\prime}_{1,\mathbf{n},h}\leqslant\beta_{1,\mathbf{n},h}
.
The last implication in (
4.15
) follows form the definitions.

The inequality (
4.8
) yields that
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{nuc}\geqslant 1
. Assume that
\mathcal{R}
is separable. Then
\mathcal{R}
is a convex combination of rank-one density tensors, which are extreme points of
\mathrm{B}_{\|\cdot\|_{nuc}(0,1)}
. Hence,

\|\mathcal{R}\|_{nuc}\leqslant 1
. Use the inequality (
4.8
) to deduce that
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{nuc}=1
. Assume that
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{nuc}=1
. Then
\mathcal{R}
is a convex combination of the extreme points of the ball
\|\mathcal{Q}\|_{nuc}\leqslant 1
. As
\tr\mathcal{R}=1
,
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
. This concluse the proof of part
(1)
.

(2)
Assume that
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}\leqslant 1
. Then
\mathcal{B}
is a convex combination of
\pm\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}},
where
\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}
is a rank-one densty tensor. Hence,
\tr\mathcal{B}\leqslant 1
, and equality holds if and only if
\mathcal{B}
is a convex combination of rank-one density tensor.
Therefore,
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
is a maximal face of
\mathrm{B}_{\mathbf{n}}(0,1)
. It is left to show that the dimension of the convex set
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
is
N^{2}(\mathbf{n})-1
.
This is the dimension of the hyperplane
\{\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}:\,\tr\mathcal{B}=1\}
.

We prove this claim by induction on
d\geqslant 1
. For
d=1
any density matrix is separable, hence the dimension of the set of density matrices in
n_{1}^{2}-1
. Assume that the dimension of
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}=N^{2}(\mathbf{n})-1
for
d=m\geqslant 1
and assume that
d=m+1
. Let
\mathbf{n}=(\mathbf{n}^{\prime},n_{m+1}),\mathbf{n}^{\prime}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{m})
.
The hyperplane generated by
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
is of the form
\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}\otimes_{j=1}^{m+1}I_{n_{j}}+\mathcal{B}
, where
\mathcal{B}
ranges in a subspace
\mathbf{W}\subset\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
.
Consider the convex subset
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}\otimes\rho\subset\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
for a fixed
\rho\in\mathrm{H}_{n_{m+1},+,1}
. The induction hypothesis yields that the dimension of the convex subspace is
N^{2}(\mathbf{n}^{\prime})-1
and every vector in the this hyperplane is of the form
\big((\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n}^{\prime})}\otimes_{j=1}^{m})I_{n_{j}}\big)\otimes\rho+\mathcal{B}
,

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}\otimes\rho
, where
\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
is in the subspace of
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
of tensors of the form
\mathbf{W}_{1}(\rho)=\{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}\otimes\rho:\,\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}},\tr\mathcal{C}^{\prime}=0\}
.
Let
\mathbf{W}_{1}
be the subspace spanned by
\cup_{\rho\in\mathrm{H}_{n_{m+1},+,1}}
.
As the span of
\mathrm{H}_{n_{m+1},+,1}
is
\mathrm{H}_{n_{m+1}}
we deduce that

\dim\mathbf{W}_{1}=(N(\mathbf{n}^{\prime})-1)n_{m+1}
.
Observe that the orthogonal complement
\mathbf{W}_{1}^{\perp}
is of the form
\{\mathcal{D}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{n}}:\,\mathcal{D}=(\otimes_{j=1}^{m}I_{n_{j}})\otimes D,D\in\mathrm{H}_{n_{m+1}}\}
.
Note that
\mathbf{W}_{1}\subset\mathbf{W}
.
Consider now the convex subset
\mathcal{R}\otimes\mathrm{H}_{n_{m+1},+,1}
for
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}
. The dimension of this convex set is
n_{m+1}-1
. Let

\mathbf{W}_{2}(\mathcal{R})=\{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\mathcal{R}\otimes B:\,B\in\mathrm{H}_{n_{m+1},}\tr B=0\}
. Denote by
\mathbf{W}_{2}
the subspace spanned by
\cup_{\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}}\mathbf{W}_{2}(\mathcal{R})
.
As the linear span of
\mathrm{B}{\|\cdot\|_{nuc}}(0,1)\subset\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}
spans
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}
, it follows that the linear span of
\cup_{\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}}\mathbf{W}_{2}(\mathcal{R})
spans
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}
.
Hence,
\mathbf{W}_{2}^{\perp}=\{\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\otimes I_{n_{m+1}}:\,\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}^{\prime}}
. Clearly
\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{W}_{1}+\mathbf{W}_{2}
. Hence,
\mathbf{W}^{\perp}=\mathbf{W}_{1}^{\perp}\cap\mathbf{W}_{2}^{\perp}
. Clearly,

\mathbf{W}_{1}^{\perp}\cap\mathbf{W}_{2}^{\perp}
is the line generated by
(\otimes_{j=1}^{m}I_{n_{j}})\otimes I_{n_{m+1}}
.
∎

The inequality
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{nuc}\geqslant 1
and the implication
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{nuc}=1\iff\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
in Theorem
4.4
was proved in
[
48
]
for
d=2
and for general
d
in
[
46
]
.

Corollary 4.5
.

Denote by
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n},int}
the relative interior of
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
.

(1)

The sets
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
and
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n},int}
are invariant under the action of
\mathrm{U}_{\mathbf{n}}
.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n},int}
. There exists
r(\mathcal{R})>0
such that
\mathrm{B}_{\|\cdot\|}(\mathcal{R},r(\mathcal{R}))\cap\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}\subset\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
.

(3)

The density tensor
\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}\otimes_{j=1}^{d}I_{n_{j}}
is in
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n},int}
, where
r(\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}\otimes_{j=1}^{d}I_{n_{j}})\geqslant\frac{2}{N(\mathbf{n})\beta_{1,\mathbf{n},h}}
.

Proof.

(1)
is straightforward.

(2)
follows from the fact that
\dim\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n},int}=N^{2}(\mathbf{n})-1
.

(3)
Let
0\neq\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}},\tr\mathcal{B}=0
. We claim that

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}=\sum_{l=1}^{p}a_{l}(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j,l})\otimes(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{j,l})-\sum_{m=1}^{q}b_{m}(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{y}_{j,m})\otimes(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\bar{\mathbf{y}}_{j,m}),

\displaystyle a_{l}>0,\|\mathbf{x}_{j,l}\|=1,b_{m}>0,\|\mathbf{y}_{j,m}\|=1,l\in[p],m\in[q],

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}=\sum_{l=1}^{p}a_{l}+\sum_{m=1}^{q}b_{m},\quad\sum_{l=1}^{p}a_{l}-\sum_{m=1}^{q}b_{m}=0.

The expansion of
\mathcal{B}
, and the first equality in the last row of the above equalities follow from the observation that
\frac{1}{\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}}\mathcal{B}

is a convex combination of the extreme points of the ball
\{\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}:\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}\leqslant 1\}
. The last equality follows from the assumption
\tr B=0
. Hence,
\sum_{l=1}^{p}a_{l}=\sum_{m=1}^{q}b_{m}=\frac{\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}}{2}
. Assume that
\|\mathcal{B}\|\leqslant r=\frac{2}{N(\mathbf{n})\beta_{1,\mathbf{n},h}}
.
Then

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}\leqslant r\beta_{1,\mathbf{n},h}=\frac{2}{N(\mathbf{n})}\Rightarrow\sum_{l=1}^{p}a_{l}=\sum_{m=1}^{q}b_{m}\leqslant\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}.

Fix
m\in[q]
. Complete the vector
\mathbf{y}_{j,m}
to an orthonormal basis
\mathbf{w}_{i_{j},j,m},i_{j}\in[n_{j}]
in
\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}}
such that
\mathbf{w}_{1,j,m}=\mathbf{y}_{j,m}
, for
j\in[d]
and
m\in[q]
.
Observe that
I_{n_{j}}=\sum_{i_{j}=1}^{q}\mathbf{w}_{i_{j},j,m}\mathbf{w}_{i_{j},j,m}^{\dagger}
.
Hence,

\displaystyle\otimes_{j=1}^{n_{j}}I_{n_{j}}=\sum_{[i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}]\in[\mathbf{n}]}(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{w}_{i_{j},j,m})\otimes(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\bar{\mathbf{w}}_{i_{j},j,m})\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})-1}(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}I_{n_{j}}-(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{w}_{1,j,m})\otimes(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\bar{\mathbf{w}}_{1,j,m})\in\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}\otimes_{j=1}^{d}I_{n_{j}}+\mathcal{B}=\sum_{l=1}^{p}a_{l}(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j,l})\otimes(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{j,l})+(\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}-\sum_{m=1}^{q}b_{m})\otimes_{j=1}^{n_{j}}I_{n_{j}}+

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{q}b_{m}(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}I_{n_{j}}-(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{w}_{1,j,m})\otimes(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\bar{\mathbf{w}}_{1,j,m}))\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}\otimes_{j=1}^{d}I_{n_{j}}+\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}.

∎

For
d=2
it is shown in
[
29
]
that
r=\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}
is the maximal
r
such that
\mathrm{B}_{\|\cdot\|}(\frac{1}{N(\mathbf{n})}\otimes_{j=1}^{d}I_{n_{j}}),r)\cap\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}\subset\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
.

4.2.
Bi-symmetric Hermitian tensors

Definition 4.6
.

Assume that
\mathbf{n}=n^{\times d}
and
n,d\geqslant 2
.
Denote
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\in[n^{\times d}]
. For a permutation
\omega:[d]\to[d]
denote
\omega(\mathbf{i})=(i_{\omega(1)},\ldots,i_{\omega(d)})
.
A Hermitian tensor
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
is called bi-symmetric Hermitian if for any permutation
\omega\in\Omega_{d}
on has the equality

(4.17)

t_{\omega(\mathbf{i}),\mathbf{j}}=t_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}\textrm{ for all }\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in[n^{\times d}],\omega\in\Omega_{d}\quad\mathcal{T}=[t_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}]\in\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}.

Denote by
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}\subset\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
the subspace of bi-symetric Hermitian tensors.

Observe that if
\mathcal{T}
is bi-symmetric Hermitian it follows that for permutations
\omega
and
\psi
we have the equalities
t_{\omega(\mathbf{i})\psi(\mathbf{j})}=t_{\mathbf{i}\mathbf{j}}
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
2\leqslant d

and
0\neq\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
. Denote by
\Lambda\subset[n^{d}]
the set of nonzero eigenvalues of
\mathcal{T}
given by (
4.1
).
Then,
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
for
k\in\Lambda
. Thus,
\mathrm{range}\,\mathrm{T}\subset\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
If in addition
\mathcal{T}
has real entries then
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
k\in\Lambda
.
Hence,

(4.18)

\displaystyle\dim\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}={n+d-1\choose d}^{2},

\displaystyle\dim\Re\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}={n+d-1\choose d}\left({n+d-1\choose d}+1\right)/2,

where
\Re\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}\subset\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
is the set of real valued bi-symmetric Hermitian tensors.

Proof.

Assume that
k\in\Lambda
. Then
\mathcal{X}_{k}=[x_{\mathbf{i},k}],\mathbf{i}\in[n^{\times d}]
is an eigenvector of
\mathcal{T}
of length one corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{T})\neq 0
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in[n^{\times d}]}t_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}x_{\mathbf{j},k}=\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{T})x_{\mathbf{i},k},\quad\mathbf{i}\in[n^{\times d}]\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{T})\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{i},k}=\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in[n^{\times d}]}t_{\omega(\mathbf{i}),\mathbf{j}}x_{\mathbf{j},k}=\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{T})x_{\omega(\mathbf{i}),k}\Rightarrow

\displaystyle x_{\mathbf{i},k}=x_{\omega(\mathbf{i}),k}\textrm{ for all }\mathbf{i}\in[n^{\times d}],\omega\in\Omega_{d},k\in\Lambda.

For
\mathcal{T}\in\Re\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
, use the real spectral decomposition of
\mathcal{T}
as a real symmetric matrix
T\in\mathrm{S}^{2}\mathbb{R}^{N(n^{\times d})}
.

As each
\mathcal{X}_{i}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
in the decomposition (
4.1
) we deduce
\mathrm{range}\,\mathrm{T}\subset\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
.
Recall that
\dim\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}={n+d-1\choose d}

[
24
]
. Hence, (
4.18
) holds.
∎

Corollary 4.8
.

The set of positive semidefinite bi-symmetric Hermitian tensors with trace one, denoted by
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
, correspond to the convex set of bi-symmetric density tensors generated by Bosons on
\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

Definition 4.9
.

A bi-symmetric Hermitian density tensor
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
is called strongly separable if
\mathcal{R}
is a convex combinations of
\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes d},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1
. Denote by
\mathrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d}}
the set of strongly separable bi-symmetric Hermitian density tensors in
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
2\leqslant n,d\in\mathbb{N}
. Denote by
\mathcal{I}_{{n+d-1\choose d}}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
the identity tensor acting on
\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
, which has a spectral decomposition as in Lemma
4.7
, whose nonzero eigenvalues are
1
. Assume that
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
.

(1)

Define

(4.19)

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{bspec}:=\max_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1}|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{B}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\rangle|=\max_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1}\max(\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{B}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\rangle,-\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{B}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\rangle).

Then
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{bspec}
is a norm on
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
, called b-spectral norm.

(2)

(4.20)

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{bspec}\leqslant\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}

Equality hold if
\mathcal{B}
is a positive semi-definite or negative semi-definite.

(3)

Let
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{bnuc}
be the dual norm of
\|\cdot\|_{bspec}
, called b-nuclear norm:

(4.21)

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{bnuc}=\max_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}},\|\mathcal{C}\|_{bspec}=1}\langle\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}\rangle.

The extreme points if
\mathrm{B}_{\|\cdot\|_{bnuc}}(0,1)
are
\pm\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes^{d}},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1
. Hence,

(4.22)

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{bnuc}=\min_{\mathcal{X}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}(\otimes^{d}\mathbf{x}_{i})\otimes(\otimes^{d}\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{i},)a_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\Pi_{\mathbf{n}},i\in[r],r\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{r}|a_{i}|.

Furthermore, the minimum is achieved for some
r\leqslant{n+d-1\choose d}^{2}+1
.

(4)

The following inequalities hold:

(4.23)

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{bnuc}\geqslant\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}.

(5)

Denote

(4.24)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},bh}=\min_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{bspec},\quad\beta_{\infty,n^{\times d},bh}=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{bspec},

\displaystyle\alpha_{1,n^{\times d},bh}=\min_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{bnuc},\quad\beta_{1,n^{\times d},bh}=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\|_{bnuc}.

Then

(4.25)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},bh}<\beta_{\infty,n^{\times d},bh}=1,\quad\alpha_{1,n^{\times d},bh}=1<\beta_{1,n^{\times d},bh},

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},bh}\beta_{1,n^{\times d},bh}=1.

(6)

The group
\mathrm{U}_{n}
acts on
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
via the diagonal action
\mathcal{R}\mapsto(\otimes^{d}U)\mathcal{R}\overline{(\otimes^{d}U)}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto((\otimes^{d}U)\mathcal{X})\otimes\overline{((\otimes^{d}U)\mathcal{X})},

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes d}\mapsto(U\mathbf{x})^{\otimes d}\otimes(\overline{U\mathbf{x}})^{\otimes d}.

as a group of isometries of the norms
\|\cdot\|_{bspec}
and
\|\cdot\|_{bnuc}
.
Let
\mu_{n}
be the Haar measure on
\mathrm{U}_{n}
. Then for any
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
the following equality holds:

(4.26)

\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n}}((\otimes^{d}U)\mathcal{R}\overline{(\otimes^{d}U)}d\mu_{n}=\frac{1}{{n+d-1\choose d}}\mathcal{I}_{{n+d-1\choose d}}.

Proof.

(1)
It is enough to show that
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec}=0\Rightarrow\mathcal{T}=0
.
Assume to the contrary that
\mathcal{T}\neq 0
and
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec}=0
, where
\mathcal{T}
has the expansion (
4.1
). We view
\mathcal{T}
as a linear operator
T:\otimes^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}\to\otimes^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
As
\mathcal{T}
is bi-symmetric Hermitian, we need to show that

(4.27)

\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\rangle=0\textrm{ for all }\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\Rightarrow\mathcal{T}=0.

Observe that since
\mathcal{T}
is bi-symmetric Hermitian follows that

(4.28)

\displaystyle\langle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathcal{T}\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{y}_{j}\rangle=\langle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{\omega(j)},\mathcal{T}\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{y}_{\psi(j)}\rangle=\overline{\langle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{y}_{\psi(j)},\mathcal{T}\otimes_{j=1}^{d}x_{\omega_{(}j)}}\rangle,\omega,\psi\in\Omega_{d},

\displaystyle\langle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathcal{T}\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}.

(The second equality in (
4.19
) follows from the observation that
\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{B}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}
.)
Assume the right hand side of (
4.27
). Replace
\mathbf{x}
by
\mathbf{x}+t\mathbf{x}_{1}
where
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{1}
are fixed and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Hence the coefficent in front of
t
is zero:

2d\Re\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(d-1)}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathcal{T}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\rangle=0.

Replace
\mathbf{x}_{1}
by
z\mathbf{x}_{1}
where
|z|=1
to deduce that

\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(d-1)}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathcal{T}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\rangle=0\textrm{ for all }\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{1}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}.

Substitute
\mathbf{x}
by
\mathbf{x}+t\mathbf{x}_{2}
for
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}
fixed and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. As the coefficient of
t
is zero we obtain the equality

\displaystyle(d-1)\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(d-2)}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{2}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathcal{T}x^{\otimes d}\rangle+d\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(d-1)}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathcal{T}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(d-1)}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{2}\rangle=0,

\displaystyle\textrm{ for all }\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}.

Replace
\mathbf{x}_{2}
by
z\mathbf{x}_{2}
, where
|z|=1
to obtain the equality

\displaystyle(d-1)\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(d-2)}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{2}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathcal{T}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\rangle+dz^{2}\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(d-1)}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathcal{T}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(d-1)}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{2}\rangle=0,

\displaystyle\textrm{ for all }\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},|z|=1.

Hence
\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(d-2)}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{2}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathcal{T}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\rangle=0
.
Repeat this argument untill we deduce the equality
\langle\mathbf{x}_{d}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{d-1}\otimes\cdots\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathcal{T}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\rangle=0
. Since
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d}
are arbitrary we deduce that
T\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}=0
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Let
x=\sum_{j=1}^{d}t_{j}\mathbf{x}_{j}
, here
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{j}
are fixed and
t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}\in\mathbb{R}
.
Hence the coefficient of
t_{1}\cdots t_{d}
is zero. It now follows that in the spectral decomposition of
\mathcal{T}
(
4.1
)
\mathcal{X}_{i}=0
for
i\in\Lambda
contrary to our assumption that
\mathcal{T}\neq 0
.

(2)
As the set
\{\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}:\,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1\}\subset\Pi_{n^{\times d}}
we deduce the inequality (
4.20
).
We claim

(4.29)

\|\mathcal{T}\|_{spec}\leqslant\max_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=\|\mathbf{y}\|=1}|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d}\rangle|,\quad\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}.

Assume that
\mathcal{T}
has the spectral decomposition (
4.1
). Fix
\mathcal{Y}=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{y}_{j}\in\Pi_{n^{\times d}}
and observe that
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{Y}=\sum_{k\in\Lambda}\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{T})\langle\mathcal{X}_{i},\mathcal{Y}\rangle\mathcal{X}_{i}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Banach’s theorem yields that

\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{n^{\times d}}}|\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{T}\mathcal{Y}|=\max_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1}|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\mathcal{Y}\rangle|.

Use the equality
\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\mathcal{Y}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{T}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{Y}\rangle
, and Banach’s theorem again to deduce

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{n^{\times d}}}|\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{T}\mathcal{X}\rangle|\leqslant\max_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\in\Pi_{n^{\times d}}}|\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{T}\mathcal{Y}\rangle|=\max_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=\|\mathbf{y}\|=1}|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d}\rangle|.

This proves (
4.29
). Assume now that
\mathcal{T}
positive semidefinite, i.e.,

\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{T})>0
for
k\in\Lambda
. Set
\mathcal{T}_{1}=\sum_{k\in\Lambda}\sqrt{\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{T})}\mathcal{X}_{i}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}_{i}}
. Then
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{T}\mathcal{Y}\rangle=\langle\mathcal{T}_{1}\mathcal{X},\mathcal{T}_{1}\mathcal{Y}\rangle
. Use Cauchy-Schwarz inequality for
\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d},\|\mathbf{x}\|=\|\mathbf{y}\|=1
to deduce

|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d}\rangle|\leqslant\sqrt{\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\rangle\langle\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d}\rangle}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec}.

Combine this inequality with (
4.29
) to deduce the equality in (
4.20
) for positive semidefinite
\mathcal{B}
.
Repalce
\mathcal{B}
with
-\mathcal{B}
to deduce the equality in (
4.20
) for negative semidefinite
\mathcal{B}
.

(3-5)
are deduced as in the proof of
(2-4)
in Lemma
4.2
.

(6)
Clearly, every
U\in\mathrm{U}_{n}
maps the positive and negative definite extreme points of the ball
\|\mathcal{X}\|_{bnuc}\leqslant 1
onto themselves. Hence,

\displaystyle(\otimes^{d}U)\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}\overline{(\otimes^{d}U)}=\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}},\quad(\otimes^{d}U)\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+}\overline{(\otimes^{d}U)}=\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+},

\displaystyle(\otimes^{d}U)\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}\overline{(\otimes^{d}U)}=\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1},\quad(\otimes^{d}U)\mathrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d}}\overline{(\otimes^{d}U)}=\mathrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d}}

for
U\in\mathrm{U}_{n}
.
Part
(6)
of Lemma
4.2
that the action of
\mathrm{U}_{n}
on
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
preserves the trace.
Let

\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbf{x}}=\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n}}(U\mathbf{x})^{\otimes d}\otimes\overline{(U\mathbf{x})^{\otimes d}}d\mu_{n},\quad\|\mathbf{x}\|=1.

As
\mu_{n}
is the Haar measure, it follows that
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbf{x}}=\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbf{y}}
for any
\|\mathbf{y}\|=1
.
Thus
\mathcal{Q}_{\mathbf{x}}=\mathcal{Q}
, and
\tr\mathcal{Q}=1
.
Furthermore,
U^{\otimes d}\mathcal{Q}\overline{U^{\otimes d}}=\mathcal{Q}
for any
U\in\mathrm{U}_{n}
.
As every
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
is a linear combination we deduce

(4.30)

\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n}}U^{\otimes d}\otimes\mathcal{B}\otimes\overline{U^{\otimes d}}d\mu_{n}=(\tr\mathcal{B})\mathcal{Q},\quad\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}.

We next observe that
\langle\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\rangle=\langle(\otimes^{d}U)\mathcal{X},(\otimes^{d}U)\mathcal{Y}\rangle
for
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n},U\in\mathrm{U}_{n}
. Hence, if
\mathcal{X}_{k},k\in[{n+d-1\choose d}]
is an orthonormal basis in
\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
, so is
(\otimes^{d}U(\mathcal{X}_{k},k\in[{n+d-1\choose d}]
for any
U\in\mathrm{U}_{n}
. Hence

(\otimes^{d}U)\mathcal{I}_{n+d-1\choose d}\overline{(\otimes^{d}U)}=\mathcal{I}_{n+d-1\choose d}
.
Let
\mathcal{B}=cI_{n+d-1\choose d}
in (
4.30
) to deduce that
\mathcal{Q}=\frac{1}{{n+d-1\choose d}}\mathcal{I}_{{n+d-1\choose d}}
.
This proves (
4.12
).
∎

The following theorem is an analog of Theorem
4.4
:

Theorem 4.11
.

Let
2\leqslant d,n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then

(1)

A bi-symmetric density tensor
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
, with spectral decomposition as in Lemma
4.7
, satisfies the inequalities

(4.31)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{n+d-1\choose d}}\leqslant\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bspec}\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})\leqslant 1,

\displaystyle 1\leqslant\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bnuc}\leqslant\beta^{\prime}_{1,n^{\times d},bh}:=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}\|_{bnuc}.

Equalities in some of the above inequalities hold under the following conditions:

(4.32)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bspec}=\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})\iff\exists\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1\textrm{ such that }\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{X}_{k}\rangle=0\textrm{ if }\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{R})<\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R}),

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bspec}=1\iff\mathcal{R}=\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\overline{\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{n+d-1\choose d}}=\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bspec}\iff\mathcal{R}=\frac{1}{{n+d-1\choose d}}I_{{n+d-1\choose d}},

\displaystyle 1=\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bnuc}\iff\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d}},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bnuc}=\beta^{\prime}_{1,n^{\times d},bh}\Leftarrow\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1,\|\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}\|_{bnuc}=\beta^{\prime}_{1,n^{\times d},bh}.

(2)

The set
\mathrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d}}
is a face of the unit ball of
\|\cdot\|_{bnuc}
of the maximal dimension
{n+d-1\choose d}^{2}-1
. Its supporting hyperplane is

(4.33)

\tr\mathcal{B}=\langle\mathcal{I}_{n+d-1\choose d},\mathcal{B}\rangle\leqslant 1\textrm{ for }\|\mathcal{B}\|_{bnuc}\leqslant 1.

Equality holds if and only if
\mathcal{B}
is strongly separable.
In particular, any strongly separable density tensor is a convex combinations of at most
{n+d-1\choose d}^{2}
rank-one bisymmetric density tensors.

Proof.

The proof this theorem is similar to the proof of Theorem
4.4
and we point out the needed modifications.

(1)
Combine the inequality (
4.20
) with
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{spec}\leqslant\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})\leqslant 1
to deduce the last two inequalities in (
4.31
). The conditions for the equality cases
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bspec}=\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})
and
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bspec}=1
are deduces as in the proof of Theorem
4.4
.

The inequality
\frac{1}{{n+d-1\choose d}}\leqslant\|\mathcal{R}\|_{spec}
follows from (
4.47
). The equality case as in the proof of Theorem
4.4
.

The inequalities (
4.23
) and (
4.14
) yield that
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bnuc}\geqslant\|\mathcal{R}\|_{nuc}\geqslant 1
. Suppose that
\mathcal{R}
is strongly separable.
It is a convex combination of the exteme points of
\mathrm{B}_{\|\cdot\|_{bnuc}}(0,1)
of the form
\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes d}
. Hence,
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bnuc}\leqslant 1
. Therefore,

\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bnuc}=1
. Assume
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bnuc}=1
. As
\tr\mathcal{R}=1
we deduce that a convex combinations of the extreme points of
\mathrm{B}_{\|\cdot\|_{bnuc}}(0,1)
that gives
\mathcal{R}
must be only of the form
\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes d}
. Hence,
\mathcal{R}
is strongly separable.

(2)
Clearly,
\mathrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d}}
is a face of the unit ball of
\|\cdot\|_{bnuc}
. It is left to show that it has the maximal dimension
{n+d-1\choose d}^{2}-1
. Observe that the dimension of the subspace spanned by
\mathcal{R}_{1}-\mathcal{R}_{2}
, where
\mathcal{R}_{1},\mathcal{R}_{2}
are strongly separable density tensors, is the dimension of the face
\mathrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d}}
. Consider now the intersection of the unit ball of
\|\cdot\|_{bnuc}
with the subspace
\tr\mathcal{B}=0
. Clearly, the dimension of this convex set is
{n+d-1\choose d}^{2}-1
. Take
\mathcal{T}\neq 0
in this convex set. It is a convex combination of the extreme points of
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{bnuc}\leqslant 1
.
Clearly, it must be of the form
(\mathcal{R}_{1}-\mathcal{R}_{2})/2
. This proves that the dimension of
\mathrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d}}
is
{n+d-1\choose d}^{2}-1
. The rest of the proof is as in the proof of Theorem
4.4
.
∎

Corollary 4.12
.

Denote by
\mathrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d},int}
the relative interior of
\mathrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d}}
.

(1)

The sets
\mathrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d}}
and
\mathrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d},int}
are invariant under the action of
\mathrm{U}_{n}
.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d},int}
. There exists
r(\mathcal{R})>0
such that
\mathrm{B}_{\|\cdot\|}(\mathcal{R},r(\mathcal{R}))\cap\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}\subset\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n},int}
.

(3)

The density tensor
\frac{1}{{n+d-1\choose d}}I_{{n+d-1\choose d}}
is in
\mathrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d},int}
, where

r(\frac{1}{{n+d-1\choose d}}I_{{n+d-1\choose d}})\geqslant\frac{2}{{n+d-1\choose d}\beta_{1,n^{\times d},bh}}
.

(4)

The inequalities in (
3.19
) hold.

(5)

For
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
the following inequalities hold:

(4.34)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{B}\|_{bnuc}\leqslant{n+d-1\choose d}^{3/2}\|\mathcal{B}\|,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{n+d-1\choose d}^{3/2}}\|\mathcal{B}\|\leqslant\|\mathcal{B}\|_{bspec}.

Proof.

The proofs of
(1)-(3)
an in the proof of Corollary
4.5
.

(4)
Assume that
\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1
. Then
\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
. The first equality in (
2.9
) implies that
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}=\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}^{2}
.
Use inequality (
4.31
) to deduce
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}\geqslant\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bspec}\geqslant{n+d-1\choose d}^{-1}
. Hence,

\|\mathcal{X}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}\geqslant{n+d-1\choose d}^{-1/2}
. Hence,
\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},s,\mathbb{C}}\geqslant{n+d-1\choose d}^{-1/2}
. Use the equality in part
(1)
of Theorem
2.4
to deduce
\beta_{1,n^{\times^{d}},s,\mathbb{C}}\leqslant{n+d-1\choose d}^{1/2}
.

(5)
Assume that

\|\mathcal{B}\|=1
, and consider the eigenvalue expansion as given in Lemma
4.7
:

\mathcal{B}=\sum_{k=1}^{{n+d-1\choose d}}\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{B})\mathcal{X}_{k}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}_{k}},\quad\mathcal{X}_{k}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n},\langle\mathcal{X}_{k},\mathcal{X}_{l}\rangle=\delta_{kl},k,l\in{n+d-1\choose d}.

Part
(4)
yields that
\|\mathcal{X}_{k}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}_{k}}\|_{bnuc}\leqslant{n+d-1\choose d}
. Hence

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{B}\|_{bnuc}\leqslant{n+d-1\choose d}\sum_{k=1}^{{n+d-1\choose d}}|\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{B})|\leqslant

\displaystyle{\displaystyle n+d-1\choose d}{n+d-1\choose d}^{1/2}\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{{n+d-1\choose d}}\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{B})^{2}}={n+d-1\choose d}^{3/2}.

This proves the first inequality in (
4.34
). The second inequality follows from Theorem
A.7
, as
\|\cdot\|_{bspec}
is the dual norm of
\|\cdot\|_{bnuc}
.
∎

4.3.
Symmetric Hermitian tensors

A bi-symmetric Hermitian tensor in
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
is called
symmetric

if
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
. In
[
49
,
8
,
31
]

\mathcal{R}
is called completely symmetric. In view of Definition
4.1
it follows that
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Denote by symmetric positive semi-definite and symmetric density tensors the sets

\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+}:=\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}\cap\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+}
and
\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+,1}:=\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}\cap\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
respectively.

Definition 4.13
.

A symmetric density tensor in
\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+,1}
is called a real strongly separable if it is a convex combination of
\{\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(2d)}:\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1\}
.

It is shown in
[
8
]
that a separable symmetric density tensors, viewed as
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
is a real strongly separable.
We now give a different proof to
[
8
, Theorem 14]
. We need the following lemma:

Lemma 4.14
.

Let
2\leqslant d,n\in\mathbb{N}
. Then for
\mathrm{S}\in\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
the following ineequaities hold:

(4.35)

\|\mathcal{S}\|_{spec}=\|\mathcal{S}\|_{bspec}=\|\mathcal{S}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}},\quad\|\mathcal{S}\|_{nuc}=\|\mathcal{S}\|_{bnuc}=\|\mathcal{S}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}.

Proof.

Banach’s theorem states that

\|\mathrm{S}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}=\max_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1}\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(2d)},\mathcal{S}\rangle=\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{S}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\rangle.

Compare that with (
4.19
) to deduce the inequality
\|\mathcal{S}\|_{bspec}\geqslant\|\mathcal{S}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}
. Recall the inequality (
4.20
) to deduce the inequalities

\|\mathcal{S}\|_{spec}\geqslant\|\mathcal{S}\|_{bspec}\geqslant\|\mathcal{S}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}
.

We now show the inequality
\|\mathcal{S}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\geqslant\|\mathcal{S}\|_{spec}
.
Let
\mathrm{S}=[s_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d},j_{1},\ldots,j_{d})}]
.
Assume that
\|\mathcal{S}\|_{spec}=|\langle\mathcal{X},\mathrm{S}\mathcal{X}\rangle|
for some
\mathcal{X}=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\Pi_{n^{\times d},\mathbb{C}}
. We now show that we can find
\mathcal{Y}=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{y}_{i}\in\Pi_{n^{\times d},\mathbb{R}}
such that
\|\mathcal{S}\|_{spec}=|\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathrm{S}\mathcal{Y}\rangle|

Fix
i_{d},j_{d}\in[n]
and let
\mathcal{S}_{i_{d},j_{d}}\in\S^{2(d-1)}\mathbb{R}^{n}
. View
\mathcal{S}_{i_{d},j_{d}}
as bi-symmetric Hermitian tensor in
\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times(d-1)}}
. Consider the
(d-1)
-sesquilinear form
c_{i_{d},j_{d}}=\langle\mathcal{X}_{d},\mathcal{S}_{i_{d},j_{d}}\mathcal{X}_{d}\rangle
, where
\mathcal{X}_{d}=\otimes_{j=1}^{d-1}\mathbf{x}_{j}\in\Pi_{n^{\times(d-1)},\mathbb{C}}
. Let
C=[c_{i_{d},j_{d}}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
, and observe that
C
is a symmetric matrix. Hence, the spectral norm of
C
is

\displaystyle\|C\|_{\infty}=\max_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},}|\langle\mathbf{y},C\mathbf{y}\rangle|=\max_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},}|\langle\mathbf{y},C\mathbf{y}\rangle|=|\langle\mathbf{y}_{d},C\mathbf{y}_{d}\rangle|=\ \|\mathcal{S}\|_{spec}=

\displaystyle|\langle\mathcal{X}_{d-1}\otimes\mathbf{y}_{d},\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{X}_{d-1}\otimes\mathbf{y}_{d}\rangle|=|\langle\mathbf{y}_{d}\otimes\mathcal{X}_{d},\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{y}_{d}\otimes\rangle\mathcal{X}_{d-1})|.

Continue in ths manner to deduce that
\|\mathcal{S}\|_{spec}=|\langle\mathcal{Y}_{d},\mathcal{S}\mathcal{Y}_{d}\rangle|
for some in
\Pi_{n^{\times d},\mathbb{R}}
. Hence
\|\mathcal{S}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\geqslant\|\mathcal{S}\|_{spec}
. This proves the equality first set of equalities in (
4.35
).

We now show the second set of equalities. Observe the following relations between the extreme points of the unit balls

\|\mathcal{S}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant 1,\|\mathcal{C}\|_{bnuc}\leqslant 1,\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}\leqslant 1
:

\{\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(2d)}:\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1\}\subset\{\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes d}:\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1\}\subset\{\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}:\mathcal{X}\in\Pi_{n^{\times d},\mathbb{C}}\}.

Recall the minimum characterizations of the nuclear norms: (
2.16
), (
4.22
), and (
4.7
). Hence for
\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have the inequalities
\|\mathcal{S}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}\geqslant\|\mathcal{S}\|_{bnuc}\geqslant\|\mathcal{S}\|_{nuc}
.
Recall the maximum characterization of the dual norm (
A.1
). Assume that
\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. As
\|\cdot\|_{bnuc}
agrees with the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}
on
\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
we deduce that
\|\mathcal{S}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant\|\mathcal{S}\|_{bnuc}
.
Hence
\|\mathcal{S}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}=\|\mathcal{S}\|_{bnuc}
. Similar arguments yield that
\|\mathcal{S}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}=\|\mathcal{S}\|_{nuc}
. This proves the second set of equalities in (
4.35
).
∎

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
2\leqslant d,n\in\mathbb{N}
.

(1)

A symmetric density tensor
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+,1}
satisfies:

(4.36)

\displaystyle\frac{(d-1)!}{2\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\big(\frac{n}{2}+k\big)}\leqslant\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant 1,

\displaystyle 1\leqslant\|\mathcal{R}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant\beta_{1,n^{\times(2d)},s,\mathbb{R}}.

Equalities in some of the above inequalities hold under the following conditions:

(4.37)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}=1\iff\mathcal{R}=\mathbf{x}^{\otimes 2d},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1,

\displaystyle\frac{(d-1)!}{2\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\big(\frac{n}{2}+k\big)}=\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\iff\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}^{\star}:=\int_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(2d)}d\lambda_{n},

\displaystyle\textrm{ where }\lambda_{n}\textrm{ is the normalized Lebesque measure}:\lambda_{n}(\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|\mathbf{x}\|=1\})=1,

\displaystyle 1=\|\mathcal{R}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}\iff\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{Sep}_{n^{\times d}}\iff\mathcal{R}\textrm{ a real strongly separable}.

(2)

The set of real strongly separable symmetric density tensors in
\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+,1}
is a face of the unit ball of
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}
in
\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
of the maximal dimension
{n+2d-1\choose 2d}-1
. Its supporting hyperplane is

(4.38)

\tr\mathcal{B}\leqslant 1\textrm{ for }\|\mathcal{B}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant 1.

Equality holds if and only if
\mathcal{B}
is a real strongly separable.
In particular, any real strongly separable density tensor is a convex combinations of at most
{n+2d-1\choose 2d}
rank-one symmetric density tensors.

Proof.

The proof of this theorem is similar to the proof of Theorem
4.11
and we point out the corresponding modifications.

(1)

Assume that
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+,1}
. Recall the equalities (
4.35
). As
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2d},+,1}
(
4.31
) yields that
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}=\|\mathcal{R}\|_{bspec}\leqslant 1
.
The conditions in (
4.32
) yield that
\mathcal{R}=\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes d}
. As
\mathcal{R}
is symmeric we deduce that
\bar{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{x}
, hence
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. This proves the first implication in (
4.37
).

We now show the first inequality in (
4.36
).
Let
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{O}_{n}
the the special orthogonal group acting on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Hence,
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{O}_{n}
acts on
\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
by the diagonal action:
\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(2d)}\mapsto(O\mathbf{x})^{\otimes(2d)}
for
O\in\mathrm{S}\mathrm{O}_{n}
. We denote this action of
O
on
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
by
O(\mathcal{B})
. Let
\nu_{n}
be the Haar measure on
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{O}_{n}
.

Assume that

\min_{\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+,1}}\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}=\|\mathcal{M}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}=\langle\mathbf{y}^{\otimes(2d)},\mathcal{M}\rangle,\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\mathbf{y}\|=1.

Hence,

\min_{\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}}\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}=\|O(\mathcal{M})\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}=\langle(O(\mathbf{y}))^{\otimes(2d)},O(\mathcal{M})\rangle.

Let
\mathcal{R}^{\star}=\int_{\nu_{n}}O(\mathcal{M})d\nu_{n}
. As
\mathcal{M}\in\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+,1}
it follows that
\mathcal{R}^{\star}\in\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+,1}
.
The minimality of
\mathcal{M}
and the triangle inequality yields
\min_{\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{S}^{2d}_{+,1}}\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}=\|\mathcal{R}^{\star}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}
. Let

\mathcal{M}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\otimes 2d},\quad a_{k}\in\mathbb{R},\mathbf{x}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}_{k}\|=1,k\in[m],\quad\|\mathcal{M}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}|a_{k}|,1=\tr\mathcal{M}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}.

Then

\mathcal{R}^{\star}=\int_{\mathrm{S}\mathrm{O}_{n}}(O\mathbf{x})^{\otimes(2d)}d\nu_{n}(O).

Since the action of
\mathrm{S}\mathrm{O}_{n}
on the
(n-1)
-sphere
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}:=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|\mathbf{x}\|=1\}
preserves the restriction of the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
we deduce that the above formula for
\mathcal{R}^{\star}
is equivalent to
\mathbb{R}^{\star}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(2d)}d\lambda_{n}
.
Furthermore, for any
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\mathbf{y}\|=1
one has the equality:

\|\mathcal{R}^{\star}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}=\langle\mathbf{y}^{\otimes 2d},\mathcal{R}^{\star}\rangle=\int_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}\langle\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\rangle^{2d}d\lambda_{n}.

Choose
\mathbf{y}=(1,0,\ldots,0)^{\top}
to deduce
\|\mathcal{R}^{\star}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}=\int_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}}x_{1}^{2d}d\lambda_{n}
. It is well known that the value of this integral is
\frac{\Gamma(n/2)\Gamma(3/2)}{\Gamma((n/2)+d))\Gamma(1/2)}
, where
\Gamma(x)
is the Gamma function. See for example
[
10
]
, or
[
23
, Lemma A.1]
. Use the identity
\Gamma(x+1)=x\Gamma(x)
for
x>0
to deduce the equality
\|\mathcal{R}^{\star}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}=\frac{(d-1)!}{2\prod_{k=0}^{d-1}\big(\frac{n}{2}+k\big)}
. This proves the lower bound in the first inequality of (
4.36
).
The proof that the minimum is achieved only for
\mathcal{R}^{\star}
is similar to the proof of Theorem
4.11
.

We now discuss the second set of inequalities in (
4.36
). The definition of
\beta_{1,n^{\times(2d)},s,\mathbb{R}}
(
2.17
) yields that
\|\mathcal{C}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant\beta_{1,n^{\times(2d)},s,\mathbb{R}}\|\mathcal{C}\|
for
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Recall that
\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
. View
\mathcal{C}
as a linear transformation from
\mathrm{C}^{n^{\times d}}
to itself. Then
\|\mathcal{C}\|=\|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{C})\|_{2}\leqslant\|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{C})\|_{1}
. As
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
we deduce
\|\overrightarrow{\lambda}(\mathcal{R})\|_{1}=\tr\mathcal{R}=1
.
This proves the last inequality in the second set of inequalities in (
4.36
).

In view of (
4.35
) and (
4.14
) we deduce that
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}\geqslant 1
. Recall
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}=1
if and only if
\mathcal{R}
is a separable density tensor in
\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
. In particular,
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}=1
if
\mathcal{R}
is a real strongly separable density tensor. Assume now that
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}=1
. As the extreme points of the ball
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}
are
\{\pm\mathbf{x}^{\otimes(2d)}:\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1\}
it follows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}a_{k}\mathbf{x}_{k}^{\otimes(2d)},\quad\mathbf{x}_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}_{k}\|=1,k\in[m],

\displaystyle 1=\|\mathcal{R}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}|a_{k}|=\tr\mathcal{R}=\sum_{k=1}a_{k}\Rightarrow a_{k}\geqslant 0,k\in[m].

Hence
\mathcal{R}
is a strongly separable density tensor. This finishes the proof of part
(1)
.

(2)
The proof of this part i similar to the proof of part
(2)
of Theorem
4.11
.
∎

4.4.
Bi-skew-symmetric Hermitian tensors

Definition 4.16
.

Assume that
\mathbf{n}=n^{\times d}
and
2\leqslant d\leqslant n
.
Let
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\in[n^{\times d}]
. For a permutation
\omega:[d]\to[d]
denote
\omega(\mathbf{i})=(i_{\omega(1)},\ldots,i_{\omega(d)})
.
A Hermitian tensor
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
is called bi-skew-symmetric (bi-anti-symmetric) Hermitian if for any permutation
\omega\in\Omega_{d}
on has the equality

(4.39)

t_{\omega(\mathbf{i}),\mathbf{j}}=\mathrm{sign}(\omega)t_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}\textrm{ for all }\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in[n^{\times d}],\omega\in\Omega_{d}\quad\mathcal{T}=[t_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}]\in\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}.

Denote by
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}\subset\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
the subspace of bi-skew-symetric Hermitian tensors.

Observe that if
\mathcal{T}
is bi-skew-symmetric Hermitian it follows that for permutations
\omega
and
\psi
we have the equalities
t_{\omega(\mathbf{i})\psi(\mathbf{j})}=\textrm{sign}(\omega\psi)t_{\mathbf{i}\mathbf{j}}
.

The following results are analous to the results of subsection
4.2
.
The proofs of the claims that are similar to the proofs in subsection
4.2
are omitted.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
2\leqslant d\leqslant n

and
0\neq\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
. Denote by
\Lambda\subset[n^{d}]
the set of nonzero eigenvalues of
\mathcal{T}
given by (
4.1
).
Then,
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
for
k\in\Lambda
.
If in addition
\mathcal{T}
has real entries then
\mathcal{X}_{k}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
k\in\Lambda
.
Thus,
\mathrm{range}\,\mathrm{T}\subset\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Hence,

(4.40)

\displaystyle\dim\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}={n\choose d}^{2},

\displaystyle\dim\Re\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}={n\choose d}\left({n\choose d}+1\right)/2,

where
\Re\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}\subset\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
is the set of real valued bi-skew-symetric Hermitian tensors.

Corollary 4.18
.

The set of positive semidefinite bi-skew-symmetric Hermitian tensors with trace one, denoted by
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
, correspond to the convex set of bi-skew-symmetric density tensors generated by Fermions on
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

Definition 4.19
.

A bi-skew-symmetric Hermitian density tensor
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
is called strongly separable if
\mathcal{R}
is a convex combinations of
\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{X}\in[\mathbf{W}]_{1},\mathbf{W}\in\mathrm{Gr}(d,\mathbb{C}^{n})
. Denote by
\mathrm{Sepa}_{n^{\times d}}
the set of strongly separable bi-skew-symmetric Hermitian density tensors in
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
.

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
2\leqslant d<n\in\mathbb{N}
. Denote by
\mathcal{I}_{{n\choose d}}\in\mathrm{A}\H_{n^{\times d}}
the identity tensor acting on
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
, which has a spectral decomposition as in Lemma
4.17
, whose nonzero eigenvalues are
1
. Assume that
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
.

(1)

The following equality holds:

(4.41)

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}:=\max_{\mathcal{Y}\in[\mathbf{W}]_{1},\mathbf{W}\in\mathrm{Gr}(d,\mathbb{C}^{n})}\frac{1}{d!}|\langle\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{B}\mathcal{Y}\rangle|

(2)

Let
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{anuc}
be the dual norm of the restriction
\|\cdot\|_{spec}
to
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
, called an a-nuclear norm:

(4.42)

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{anuc}=\max_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}},\|\mathcal{C}\|_{spec}=1}\langle\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}\rangle.

The extreme points if
\mathrm{B}_{\|\cdot\|_{anuc}}(0,1)
are
\pm\frac{1}{d!}\mathcal{Y}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{Y}},\mathcal{Y}\in[\mathbf{W}]_{1},\mathbf{W}\in\Gr(d,\mathbb{C}^{n})
. Hence,

(4.43)

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{anuc}=\min_{\mathcal{X}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\frac{a_{i}}{d!}\mathcal{Y}_{i}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{Y}_{i}},\mathcal{Y}_{i}\in[\mathbf{W}]_{1},\mathbf{W}\in\mathrm{G}(d,\mathbb{C}^{n}),a_{i}\in\mathbb{R},i\in[r],r\in\mathbb{N}}\sum_{i=1}^{r}|a_{i}|.

Furthermore, the minimum is achieved for some
r\leqslant{n\choose d}^{2}+1
.

(3)

The following inequalities hold:

(4.44)

\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}\geqslant\|\mathcal{B}\|_{anuc}\geqslant\|\mathcal{B}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}.

(4)

Denote

(4.45)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},ah}=\min_{\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}},\|\mathcal{B}\|=1}\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec},\quad\beta_{\infty,n^{\times d},ah}=\max_{\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}},\|\mathcal{B}\|=1}\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec},

\displaystyle\alpha_{1,n^{\times d},ah}=\min_{\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}},\|\mathcal{B}\|=1}\|\mathcal{B}\|_{anuc},\quad\beta_{1,n^{\times d},ah}=\max_{\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}},\|\mathcal{B}\|=1}\|\mathcal{B}\|_{anuc}.

Then

(4.46)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},ah}<\beta_{\infty,n^{\times d},ah}=\frac{1}{d!},\quad\alpha_{1,n^{\times d},ah}=d!<\beta_{1,n^{\times d},ah},

\displaystyle\alpha_{\infty,n^{\times d},ah}\beta_{1,n^{\times d},ah}=1.

The equalities
\beta_{\infty,n^{\times d},ah}=\|\mathcal{B}\|_{\infty},\|\mathcal{B}\|=1
and
\alpha_{1,n^{\times d},ah}=\|\mathcal{C}\|_{\infty},\|\mathcal{C}\|=1
if and only if
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{C}
are of the form
\pm(\mathbf{z}_{1}\wedge\cdots\mathbf{z}_{d})\otimes(\bar{\mathbf{z}}_{1}\wedge\cdots\bar{\mathbf{z}}_{d})
for some orthonormal set of
\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{d}
.

(5)

The group
\mathrm{U}_{n}
acts on
\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
via the diagonal action
\mathcal{R}\mapsto(\otimes^{d}U)\mathcal{R}\overline{(\otimes^{d}U)}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}\mapsto((\otimes^{d}U)\mathcal{X})\otimes\overline{((\otimes^{d}U)\mathcal{X})},

\displaystyle(\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d})\otimes(\overline{\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}})\mapsto((U\mathbf{x}_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge(U\mathbf{x}_{d}))\otimes(\overline{(U\mathbf{x}_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge(U\mathbf{x}_{d})}).

as a group of isometries of the norms
\|\cdot\|_{spec}
and
\|\cdot\|_{anuc}
.
Let
\mu_{n}
be the Haar measure on
\mathrm{U}_{n}
. Then for any
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
the following equality holds:

(4.47)

\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n}}((\otimes^{d}U)\mathcal{R}\overline{(\otimes^{d}U)}d\mu_{n}=\frac{1}{{n\choose d}}\mathcal{I}_{{n\choose d}}.

Proof.

(1)
Assume that
\mathbf{x}_{j}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},j\in[d]
and
\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
. The first equality in (
2.26
) yields

\displaystyle\langle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathcal{X}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d!}}\langle\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d},\mathcal{X}\rangle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\langle(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{i})\otimes(\overline{\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{i}}),\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}\rangle=\langle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathcal{X}\rangle\langle\overline{\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{i}},\overline{\mathcal{X}}\rangle=\langle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathcal{X}\rangle\langle\mathcal{X},\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{i}\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d!}\langle\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d},\mathcal{X}\rangle\langle\mathcal{X},\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}\rangle=\frac{1}{d!}\langle\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d},(\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}})\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}\rangle.

Assume that
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
has a decomposition as in Lemma
4.17
. From the above equalities we deduce

(4.48)

\langle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathcal{B}(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j})\rangle=\frac{1}{d!}\langle\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d},\mathcal{B}(\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d})\rangle.

Assume now that
\|\mathbf{x}_{j}\|=1,j\in[d]
. Then
\|\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}\|\leqslant 1
and equality holds if and only if
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d}
is an orthonormal set in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Hence, (
4.41
) holds.

(2)
. As in the proof of part
(3)
of Lemma (
4.10
).

(3)
. Assume that
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
. Recall that
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{nuc}=\max_{\mathcal{C}\in\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}},\|\mathcal{C}\|_{nuc}=1}\langle\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}\rangle
.
Compare thie characterization to (
4.42
) to deduce the first inequality in (
4.44
). To deduce the second inequality in (
4.44
) we observe that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d!}(\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d})\otimes(\overline{\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}})=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(d!)^{2}}\sum_{\omega,\psi\in\Omega_{d}}(\textrm{sign}(\omega)\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{\omega(j)})\otimes((\textrm{sign}(\psi)\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{\omega(j)}).

Assume that
\|\mathbf{x}_{1}\|=\cdots=\|\mathbf{x}_{d}\|=1
. Then, the above sum is a convex combination of
(d!)^{2}
extreme points of the unit ball of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}
.
Hence, the minimal characterizations
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{anuc}
and
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}
implies the inequality
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{anuc}\geqslant\|\mathcal{B}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}
.

(4)
Let
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}},\|\mathcal{B}\|=1
. Consider the spectral decomposition of
\mathcal{B}
as given in Lemma
4.17
:

\mathcal{B}=\sum_{i=1}^{{n\choose d}}\lambda_{i}(\mathcal{B})\mathcal{X}_{i}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}_{i}},\mathcal{X}_{i}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n},\langle\mathcal{X}_{i},\mathcal{X}_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij},i,j\in[{n\choose d}],\sum_{i=1}^{{n\choose d}}\lambda_{i}^{2}(\mathcal{B})=1.

Use the (
4.48
) and the fact that
|\lambda_{i}|\leqslant 1
to deduce that

\displaystyle|\langle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathcal{B}(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j})\rangle|=\frac{1}{d!}|\sum_{i=1}^{{n\choose d}}\lambda_{i}|\langle\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d},\mathcal{X}_{i}\rangle|^{2}|\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d!}|\sum_{i=1}^{{n\choose d}}|\langle\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d},\mathcal{X}_{i}\rangle^{2}|=\frac{1}{d!}\|\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d}\|^{2}\leqslant\frac{1}{d!}.

Equality
|\langle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathcal{B}(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j})\rangle|=\frac{1}{d!}
holds if and only if
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d}
is an orthonormal set of vectors, and
\mathcal{B}=\pm(\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d})\otimes(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{d})
.
This proves the first inequality in (
4.46
). Other inequaities follow from Theorem
A.7
.

(5)
. Assume that
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
is an orthonormal basis
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. The
U\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,U\mathbf{x}_{n}
is an orthonormal basis in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Recall that an orthonormal basis
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}
corresponds to a unitary matrix
U\in\mathrm{U}_{n}
whose columns are
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}
. Hence,
\mathrm{U}_{n}
acts transitively on orthonormal bases in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
.
Recall that

\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{i_{d}},1\leqslant i_{1}<\cdots<i_{d}\leqslant n
is an orthonormal basis in
\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle I_{n\choose d}=\sum_{1\leqslant i_{1}<\cdots<i_{d}\leqslant n}(\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{i_{d}})\otimes(\overline{\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{i_{d}}})\Rightarrow(\otimes^{d}U)I_{n\choose d}(\overline{\otimes^{d}U})=I_{n\choose d}\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\int_{\mathrm{U}_{n}}(\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{n})\otimes(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{n})d\mu_{n}=\frac{1}{{n\choose d}}I_{{n\choose d}}.

Let
0\neq\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
. Assume that
\mathcal{B}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{a_{i}}{d!}\mathbf{y}_{1,i}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{y}_{d,i}
is the minimal decomposition of
\mathcal{B}
with respect to a-nuclear norm. Thus,
a_{i}\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
, and
\mathbf{y}_{1,i},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{d,i}

is an orthonormal set of vectors in
\mathbb{C}^{n}
for
i\in[m]
. Observe that
\tr B=\frac{1}{d!}\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}
. Hence,
