\int_{U\in\mathrm{U}_{n}}(\otimes^{d}U)\mathcal{B}(\otimes^{d}\bar{U})d\mu_{n}=\frac{\tr\mathcal{B}}{{n\choose d}}I_{{n\choose d}}.

This proves (
4.47
).
∎

The following theorem is an analog of Theorem
4.11
:

Theorem 4.21
.

Let
2\leqslant d\leqslant n
.
Then

(1)

A bi-skew-symmetric density tensor
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
, with spectral decomposition as in Lemma
4.17
, satisfies the inequalities

(4.49)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d!{n\choose d}}\leqslant\|\mathcal{R}\|_{spec}\leqslant\frac{\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})}{d!}\leqslant\frac{1}{d!},

\displaystyle d!\leqslant\|\mathcal{R}\|_{anuc}\leqslant\beta^{\prime}_{1,n^{\times d},ah}:=\max_{\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1}\|\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}\|_{anuc}.

Equalities in some of the above inequalities hold under the following conditions:

(4.50)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}\|_{spec}=\frac{\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})}{d!}\iff\exists\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\langle\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij},i,j\in[d]

\displaystyle\textrm{ such that }\langle\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d},\mathcal{X}_{k}\rangle=0\textrm{ if }\lambda_{k}(\mathcal{R})<\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R}),

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}\|_{spec}=\frac{1}{d!}\iff\mathcal{R}=(\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d})\otimes(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{d}),\langle\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij},i,j\in[d],

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d!{n+d-1\choose d}}=\|\mathcal{R}\|_{spec}\iff\mathcal{R}=\frac{1}{{n+d-1\choose d}}I_{{n+d-1\choose d}},

\displaystyle d!=\|\mathcal{R}\|_{anuc}\iff\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{Sepa}_{n^{\times d}},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{R}\|_{anuc}=\beta^{\prime}_{1,n^{\times d},ah}\Leftarrow\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}},\mathcal{X}\in\mathrm{A}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathcal{X}\|=1,\|\mathcal{X}\otimes\overline{\mathcal{X}}\|_{anuc}=\beta^{\prime}_{1,n^{\times d},ah}.

(2)

The set
\mathrm{Sepa}_{n^{\times d}}
is a face of the ball of
\|\cdot\|_{anuc}
of the maximal dimension
{n\choose d}^{2}-1
. Its supporting hyperplane is

(4.51)

\tr\mathcal{B}=\langle\mathcal{I}_{n\choose d},\mathcal{B}\rangle\leqslant 1\textrm{ for }\|\mathcal{B}\|_{anuc}\leqslant d!.

Equality holds if and only if
\mathcal{B}
is strongly separable.
In particular, any strongly separable density tensor is a convex combinations of at most
{n\choose d}^{2}
bi-skew-symmetric density tensors of the form
(\mathbf{x}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathbf{x}_{d})\otimes(\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{d}),\langle\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij},i,j\in[d]
.

Proof.

In the proof of part
(4)
of Lemma
4.20
replace
\mathcal{B}
by
\mathcal{R}
, and the condition
\sum_{i=1}^{{n\choose d}}\lambda^{2}_{i}(\mathcal{B})=1
by the conditions
\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})\geqslant\cdots\geqslant\lambda_{{n\choose d}}(\mathcal{R})\geqslant 0,\sum_{i=1}^{{n\choose d}}\lambda_{i}(\mathcal{R})=1
.
Observe the inequality
\lambda_{i}(\mathcal{R})\leqslant 1
to deduce the inequality
\|cR\|_{spec}\leqslant\frac{\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{R})}{d!}
. The first two equality cases (
4.49
) are deduced straightforward.

To deduce the lower bound
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{aspec}=\frac{1}{d!{n\choose d}}
we combine the proof of part
(5)
of Lemma
4.20
with the arguments of the proof of similar lower bound in Theorem
4.49
.

All other parts of this theorem are deduced as in the proof of Theorem
4.11
:
∎

Corollary 4.22
.

Denote by
\mathrm{Sepa}_{n^{\times d},int}
the relative interior of
\mathrm{Sepa}_{n^{\times d}}
.

(1)

The sets
\mathrm{Sepa}_{n^{\times d}}
and
\mathrm{Sepa}_{n^{\times d},int}
are invariant under the action of
\mathrm{U}_{n}
.

(2)

Let
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{Sepa}_{n^{\times d},int}
. There exists
r(\mathcal{R})>0
such that
\mathrm{B}_{\|\cdot\|}(\mathcal{R},r(\mathcal{R}))\cap\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}\subset\mathrm{Sepa}_{n^{\times d},int}
.

(3)

The density tensor
\frac{1}{{n\choose d}}I_{{n\choose d}}
is in
\mathrm{Sepa}_{n^{\times d},int}
, where

r(\frac{1}{{n\choose d}}I_{{n\choose d}})\geqslant\frac{2}{{n\choose d}\beta_{1,n^{\times d},ah}}
.

The proof of this corollary is similar to the proof of the corresponding parts of Corollary
4.12
.

4.5.
The set of separable density tensors is semi-algebraic

The main result of this subsection is:

Theorem 4.23
.

The separability sets
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
,
\mathrm{Seps}_{n^{\times d}}
and their restrictions to
\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+,1}
are semi-algebraic sets.

We first explain the notion of a semi-algebraic set and its importance in quantum information theory. Denote by
\mathbb{F}[\mathbf{x}]
the ring of polynomial over the field
\mathbb{F}
with
n
coordinate variables of
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}
. For
p\in\mathbb{F}[\mathbf{x}]
denote by
\mathrm{Z}(p)=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}:\,p(\mathbf{x})=0\}
, the zero set of
p
. A set
\mathrm{V}\subset\mathbb{F}^{n}
is called an algebraic variety if
\mathrm{V}=\cap_{l=1}^{m}\mathrm{Z}(p_{l})
. Algebraic geometry studies various properties of algebraic varieties over the complex numbers
\mathbb{C}

[
33
]
.

For
p\in\mathbb{R}[\mathbf{x}]
denote
\mathrm{Z}_{+}(p)=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:p(\mathbf{x})\geqslant 0\}
. Observe that
\mathrm{Z}(p)=\mathrm{Z}_{+}(p)\cap\mathrm{Z}_{+}(-p)
. A basic semi-algebraic set
W\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is given by
W=\cap_{l=1}^{m}\mathrm{Z}_{+}(p_{l})
. A semialgebraic set is a finite union of basic semialgebraic sets.
In quantum information one needs to consider semi-algebraic sets over
\mathbb{C}^{n}
. We identify
\mathbb{C}^{n}
with
\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\sim\mathbb{R}^{2n}
where
\mathbf{z}=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{i}\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Observe that a complex algebraic variety is a real algebraic variety on
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.

The first simple examples of semi-algebraic sets are
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}},\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+},\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}
. It is enough to consider the sets
\mathrm{H}_{n},\mathrm{H}_{n,+},\mathrm{H}_{n,+,1}
. Clearly
\mathrm{H}_{n}=\mathrm{S}^{2}\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathrm{A}^{2}\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Hence,
\mathrm{H}_{n}
is a real algebraic set.
Recall that a Hermitian matrix is positive semidefinite if and only its all the principal minors are nonnegative. Hence,
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+}
and
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}
are basic semi-algebraic sets.

We next recall the definition of positive partial transport density tensors introduced by Peres for
d=2

[
45
]
:

Definition 4.24
.

Let
2\leqslant d,2\mathbf{1}_{d}\leqslant\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
. For
k\in[d]
denote by
\mathrm{P}\mathrm{T}_{k}:\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}\to\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
the
k
-partial transpose:

(4.52)

\displaystyle\mathrm{P}\mathrm{T}_{k}(\mathcal{B})=\mathcal{C},\mathcal{B}=[b_{(i_{1},\ldots,k_{d})(j_{1},\ldots,j_{d})}],\mathcal{C}=[c_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})(j_{1},\ldots,j_{d})}],

\displaystyle c_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},i_{k},i_{k+1},\cdots,i_{d})(j_{1},\ldots,j_{k-1},j_{k},j_{k+1},\cdots,j_{d})}=b_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{k-1},j_{k},i_{k+1},\cdots,i_{d})(j_{1},\ldots,j_{k-1},i_{k},j_{k+1},\cdots,j_{d})},

\displaystyle(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}),(j_{1},\ldots,j_{f})\in[\mathbf{n}],\quad k\in[d].

Denote by
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},ppt,1}
the biggest convex subset of
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}
which is invariant under
\mathrm{P}\mathrm{T}_{k}
for
k\in[d]
. This convex subset is called the positive partial transpose set of density tensors.

Proposition 4.25
.

Let
2\leqslant d,2\mathbf{1}_{d}\leqslant\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
.

(1)

The set
\mathrm{P}\mathrm{T}_{1},\ldots,\mathrm{P}\mathrm{T}_{d}
is a commutative group of involutions acting on
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
which preserve trace.

(2)

\mathrm{P}\mathrm{T}_{1}\cdots\mathrm{P}\mathrm{T}_{d}(\mathcal{B})=\overline{\mathcal{B}}
.

(3)

(4.53)

\mathrm{P}\mathrm{T}_{k}\left(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j})\otimes(\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{j})\right)=(\otimes_{j=1}^{k-1}\mathbf{x}_{j})\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{k}\otimes(\otimes_{j=k+1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j})\otimes(\otimes_{j=1}^{k-1}\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{j})\otimes\mathbf{x}_{k}\otimes(\otimes_{j=k+1}^{d}\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{j}),

for
k\in[d]
.

(4)

The set
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},ppt,1}
is basic semi-algebraic.

Proof.

The proof of (1)-(3) is straightforward.

(4)
. The set
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},ppt,1}
is obtained from
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
by imposing a number of linear conditions. As
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}
is basic semi-algebraic it follows that is basic semi-algebraic.
∎

An important observation of Peres
[
45
]
(for
d=2
) is:

Corollary 4.26
.

Let
2\leqslant d,2\mathbf{1}_{d}\leqslant\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
. Then
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}\subset\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},ppt,1}
.

It was shown in
[
37
]
that for
\mathbf{n}=(2,2),(2,3)

\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}=\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},ppt,1}
, and in
[
38
]
that for
\mathbf{n}=(2,4),(3,3)

\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}\not\subset\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},ppt,1}
.

The fundamental Tarski-Seidenberg Theorem states

Theorem 4.27
.

Let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be semi-algebraic. Assume that
F:\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
is a polynomial map. The
F(X)
is semi-algebraic.

Definition 4.28
.

Let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a compact set. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
denote by
\mathrm{conv}_{k}(X)
the convex hull spanned by
k
-points in
X
. Let

\mathrm{conv}\,X
be the convex hull of
X
.

Assume that
\dim\mathrm{conv}\,X=m
. Caratheodory’s theorem yields that
\mathrm{conv}_{m+1}(X)=\mathrm{conv}\,X
. The following result is known and we bring its proof for completeness:

Proposition 4.29
.

Let
X\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
be a compact semi-algebraic set. Assume that
m=\dim\mathrm{conv}\,X
. Then for
k\in[m+1]
the convex set
\mathrm{conv}_{k}X
is semi-algebraic. In particular,
\mathrm{conv}\,X
is semi-algebraic.

Proof.

Clearly, the
k-1
dimensional sphere
\mathbb{S}^{k-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
is an algebraic set. Recall that a product of two semi-algebraic sets is semi-algebraic
[
5
]
.
Hence, the set
Y=\mathbb{S}^{k-1}\times X^{\times k}
is semi-algebraic. Let
\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{k})\in Y
, where
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{k})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbf{X}
for
i\in[k]
. Consider the map
p_{k}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{kn}\to\mathbb{R}^{n}
given by

p_{k}(\mathbf{y})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}u_{i}^{2}\mathbf{x}_{i}.

Clearly,
p_{k}
is a polynomial map. Observe that
\mathrm{conv}_{k}(X)=p_{k}(Y)
.
Hence, the Tarski-Seidenberg Theorem states that
\mathrm{conv}_{k}X
is semi-algebraic. As
\mathrm{conv}_{m+1}(X)=\mathrm{conv}\,X
we deduce that
\mathrm{conv}\,X
is semi-algebraic.
∎

The folowing result is well known and we bring its proof for completeness:

Lemma 4.30
.

The set of rank-one tensors plus the zero tensor, denoted as
\Sigma_{\mathbf{n}}\cup\{0\}
, is a complex algebraic variety in
\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}}
.

Proof.

Let
0\neq\mathcal{T}\in\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}}.

View
\mathcal{T}
as a matrix in
\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}}\otimes(\otimes_{j=2}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}})
. This is called an unfolding with respect to
\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}}
. Assume that all
2\times 2
minors of this matrix are zero. Then this matrix is a rank-one matrix of the form
\mathbf{x}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{1}
, where
\mathcal{T}_{1}\in\otimes_{j=2}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}}
. If
d=2
it follows that
\mathcal{T}
has rank-one. Suppose that
d>2
. Unfold
\mathcal{T}
the
j=2
to view
\mathcal{T}
is a matrix on
\mathbb{C}^{n_{2}}\otimes(\otimes_{j\neq 2}\mathbb{C}^{n_{j}})
. Assume now that all
2\times 2
minors of this matrix are zero. It now follows that
\mathcal{T}=\mathbf{x}_{1}\otimes\mathbf{x}_{2}\otimes\mathcal{T}_{2}
. Continue to unfold in each
\geqslant 3
and assume that all
2\times 2
minors of this matrix are zero. We conclude that
\mathcal{T}
has rank one. Observe finally that a zero tensor satisfies all the above quadratic equations.
∎

Proof of Theorem
4.23
.
Recall that the extreme points of
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
, denoted as
\mathrm{E}_{\mathbf{n}}
, are rank-one tensors in
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}
.
Clearly,
\mathrm{E}_{\mathbf{n}}=\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n},+,1}\cap(\Sigma_{(\mathbf{n},\mathbf{n})}\cup\{0\})
. Hence,
\mathrm{E}_{\mathbf{n}}
is a basic semi-algebraic set. Proposition
4.29
yields that
\mathrm{Sep}_{\mathbf{n}}
is semi-algebraic in
\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
.

Similar arguments apply to strongly separable and real strongly separable density tensors in
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
and
\mathrm{S}^{2d}\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+,1}
respectively.∎

Recall that Tarski-Seidenberg Theorem is proved by elimination of quantifiers. The proof is constructive, but the resulting algorithm has a high computational complexity. Collins
[
11
]
introduced the algorithm of cylindrical algebraic decomposition, which allows quantifier elimination over the reals in double exponential time. This complexity is optimal.

Observe that the
\dim\mathrm{H}_{(2,4)}=64
. It seems to be impossible to use a brute force algorithm to find semi-algebraic characterization of
(2,4)
separable states. The dimension of
\mathrm{S}^{4}\mathbb{R}^{3}
is
{2+4-1\choose 4}=5
.
Hence, it seems possible to find a semi-algebraic characterization of real strongly separated density tensors in
\mathrm{S}^{4}\mathbb{R}^{3}_{+,1}
with computer help.

5.
Complexity results on tensors, entanglement and separablity

The main result of this section is:

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
1\leqslant n\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed. The following
quantities are poly-time computable in
d\in\mathbb{N}
:

(1)

The norms
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
on
\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
.

(2)

The norms
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
for
\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}\otimes\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
.

(3)

The norms
\|\cdot\|_{bspec}
and
\|\cdot\|_{bnuc}
for
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
.

5.1.
Poly-time, NP and NP-hard problems

Poly-time problems are computational problems that can be solved in time bounded by a polynomial function of the input size.
These problems include multiplication and inverse of matrices, finding the roots of a polynomial in one variable, eigenvalues and eigenvectors of square matrices, Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization process, singular value decomposition (Schmidt decomposition), and other known algorithms.

The class NP (nondeterministic poly-time) encompasses decision problems where a solution can verified in poly-time. An NP-complete problem is the hardest problem in the class NP: a poly-time algorithm for any NP-complete problem would imply that all problems in the class NP are solvable in poly-time.
An NP-hard problem is a problem that is at least as hard as NP-complete problem.
If a poly-time algorithm exists for any NP-hard problem, then all NP problems are solvable in poly-time.

Since we are interested in computing the various spectral and nuclear norms of tensors the following complexity result is very useful
[
21
]
:

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
and
\nu,\nu^{*}:\mathbb{F}^{n}\to[0,\infty)
be dual norms. Then
\nu(\mathbf{x})
is poly-time computable for
\mathbf{x}
with rational (Gaussian rational) entries if and only if
\nu^{*}(\mathbf{x})
is poly-time computable for
\mathbf{x}
with rational (Gaussian rational) entries.

Let us start with the largest clique problem as in
[
22
]
.
An undirected simple graph is denoted by
G=(V,E)
, where
V
is the set of vertices, and
E
is the set of undirected edges
\{u,v\}\in E,u\neq v\in V
. Assume that the cardinality of
V
is
n
:
|V|=n
. We identify
V
with
[n]
. Then
A(G)=[a_{ij}]\in\{0,1\}^{n\times n}
is a symmetric matrix with zero diagonal whose nonzero entries are equal to
1
. Thus,
a_{ij}=1\iff\{i,j\}\in E
. A clique is a set
C\subset[n]
such for all
i\neq j\in C
we have
\{i,j\}\in E
. Denote by
\kappa(G)
the maximum cardinality of all cliques in
G
. It is known that finding
\kappa(G)
is a NP-complete problem. In
[
44
]
the authors give a characterization for
\kappa(G)
which is equivalent to the following characterization:

(5.1)

1-\frac{1}{\kappa(G)}=\max_{\|\mathbf{x}\|=1,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\sum_{i=j=1}^{n}a_{ij}x_{i}^{2}x^{2}_{j}=\max_{\|\mathbf{x}\|=1,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}}|\sum_{i=j=1}^{n}a_{ij}x_{i}^{2}\bar{x}^{2}_{j}|.

Lemma 5.3
.

The following quantities are NP-hard to compute for a fixed
m\in\mathbb{N}
:

(1)

The norms
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
for tensors in
\mathrm{S}^{4m}\mathbb{F}^{n}
for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
.

(2)

The norms
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}
for tensors in
\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2m},+,1}
and
\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2m}}
.

(3)

The norms
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}
for tensors in
\Re\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2m},+,1}
and
\Re\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2m}}
.

(4)

The norms
\|\cdot\|_{spec}
and
\|\cdot\|_{nuc}
for tensors in
\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2m},+,1}
and
\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2m}}
.

(5)

The norms
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}
and
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
are NP-hard to compute on
\mathbb{F}^{n^{\times(4m)}}
.

Proof.

(1)

Let
f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{(i,j)\in}a_{i,j}x_{i}^{2}x_{j}^{2}
, where
A=[a_{i,j}]=A(G)
. Recall that

f^{m}(\mathbf{x})=\langle\mathcal{S}_{m},\otimes^{d}\mathbf{x}\rangle
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
for a corresponding
\mathcal{S}_{m}\in\mathrm{S}^{4m}\mathbb{R}^{n}
(
5.5
). In view of (
5.1
) it is NP-hard to find
\|\mathcal{S}_{m}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}
for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
. As all entries of
\mathcal{S}_{m}
are nonnegative, it follows that
\|\mathcal{S}_{m}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}\|=\|\mathcal{S}_{m}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\|
. Hence, it NP-hard to compute
\|\mathcal{S}_{m}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}
.
Therefore, it is NP-hard to compute the dual norm
\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{F}}
for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}

[
21
]
.

(2-4)

Observe that the maximum given in (
5.1
) can be associated with the bspectral norm of following bi-symmetric Hermitian tensor
\mathcal{G}=[g_{k,l,p,q}]\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2}}
:

(5.2)

g_{k,l,p,q}=\begin{cases}1\textrm{ if }(k,l)=(i,i),(p,q)=(j,j),\{i,j\}\in E,\\
0\textrm{ otherwise}\end{cases}

Clearly,
\tr\mathcal{G}=0
.
Let
X=[x_{i,j}]\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
be a symmetric matrix. (
X\in\mathrm{S}^{2}\mathbb{C}^{n}
). Consider the sesquilinear form in entries of
X
:

Q(X,\bar{X}):=\sum_{(i,j)\in E}x_{i,i}\bar{x}_{j,j}
. As
|\mathbf{x}_{i,i}\mathbf{x}_{j,j}|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}(|x_{i,i}|^{2}+|x_{j,j}|^{2})
it follows that

|Q(\mathbf{x},\bar{\mathbf{x}})|\leqslant d\sum_{i\in[n]}\|x_{i,i}|^{2}\leqslant d\|X\|^{2}
, where
d
is the maximal degree of vertices in
G
. Recall that
d\leqslant n(n-1)/2
. Let
\mathcal{I}_{n(n+1)/2}
be the identity tensor as defined in Lemma
4.10
.

Define

(5.3)

\mathcal{T}=\frac{4}{n^{2}(n^{2}-1)}\left(\frac{n(n-1)}{2}\mathcal{I}_{n(n+1)/2}+\mathcal{G}\right).

It is straightforward to show that
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2},+,1}
.
We claim that

(5.4)

\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec}=\frac{4}{n^{2}(n^{2}+1)}\left(n(n-1)+1-\kappa(G)\right).

Clearly,
\langle X,\mathcal{I}_{n(n+1)/2}X\rangle=\|X\|^{2}
. Next observe that

\displaystyle|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes 2}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes 2},\mathcal{G}\rangle|=|2\sum_{(i,j)\in E}\bar{x}_{i}^{2}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{2}|\leqslant 2\sum_{(i,j)\in E}|x_{i}|^{2}|x_{j}|^{2}\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{G}\|_{bspec}=\max_{\|X\|=1,X=\diag(\mathbf{x}),\|\mathbf{x}\|=1,\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})^{\top}\geqslant 0}\sum_{(i,j)\in E}x_{i}^{2}x_{j}^{2}=1-\kappa(G).

This proves (
5.4
). Hence, it is NP-hard to compute the
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec}
for some tensor in
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2},+,1}
. In particular, it is NP-hard to compute
\|\cdot\|_{bspec}
. Hence, it is NP-hard to compute the dual norm
\|\cdot\|_{bnuc}

[
21
]
. This proves
(2)-(4)
for
m=1
.
For
m>1
use the tensor
\mathcal{T}^{\otimes m}
.

(5)
Follows from
(1)
, as
\mathrm{S}^{4m}\mathbb{R}^{n}\subset\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times(2m)}}
.
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

The GME is NP-hard to compute for states in
\mathbb{C}^{n^{\times(4m)}}
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

A remarkable result of Gurvits
[
30
]
states deciding if a density tensor
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times 2},+,1}
with Gaussian entries is separable is an NP-hard problem. In view of Theorem
4.4
this is equivalent to statement that deciding if
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{nuc}=1
is NP-hard. This result was extended in
[
25
]
.

5.2.
Coding symmetric and bi-symmetric Hermitian tensors as polynomials

Definition 5.5
.

Denote by
\mathrm{P}(d,n,\mathbb{F})
the linear space of homogeneous polynomials of degree
d
in
n
-variables over
\mathbb{F}
. Let

J(d,n)=\{\mathbf{j}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{d},j_{1}+\cdots+j_{d}=d\},\quad\mathbb{Z}_{+}:=\{0\}\cup\mathbb{N}.

Assume that
\mathbf{j}\in J(d,n)
. Denote by
\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{j}}=x_{1}^{j_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{j_{n}}
. Let
\mathbf{j}^{\prime}=(j_{k_{1}},\ldots,j_{k_{s}}),s\in[d],1\leqslant k_{1}<\cdots<k_{s}\leqslant n
be obtained from
\mathbf{j}
by deleting all
j_{l}=0
in
\mathbf{j}
.
A symmetric tensor
\mathcal{S}=[s_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}]\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
corresponds to
f(\mathbf{x})=\langle\bar{\mathcal{S}},\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\rangle
:

(5.5)

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in J(d,n)}c(\mathbf{j})f_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}x_{1}^{j_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{j_{n}},\quad c(\mathbf{j})=\frac{d!}{j_{1}!\cdots j_{n}!},\mathbf{j}\in J(d,n),

\displaystyle f_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{n}}=s_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}}\textrm{ where }k_{l}\textrm{ appears }j_{k_{l}}\textrm{ times in }(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d}),l\in[s].

The space
\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}\otimes\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
is called the space of bi-symmetric tensors
\mathcal{T}=[t_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}],\mathbf{i},\mathbf{k}\in[\mathbf{n}]
in
\mathbb{F}^{n^{\times(2d)}}
:
t_{\omega(\mathbf{i}),\psi(\mathbf{k})}=t_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{k}}
for
\omega,\psi\in\Omega_{d}
. It corresponds to bi-homogeneous polynomials
\mathrm{P}_{bi}(d,n,\mathbb{F})

f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sum_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}\in J(d,n)}c(\mathbf{j})c(\mathbf{k})f_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}}\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{j}}\mathbf{y}^{\mathbf{l}},

where the correspondence between
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}\otimes\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
and
f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is given as in (
5.5
).

The space
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
corresponds to Hermitian polynomials
f(\mathbf{x},\bar{\mathbf{x}})
:

f(\mathbf{x},\bar{\mathbf{x}})=\sum_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}\in J(d,n)}c(\mathbf{j})c(\mathbf{k})f_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}}\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{j}}\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\mathbf{l}},\quad f_{\mathbf{l},\mathbf{j}}=\overline{f_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}}},\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}\in[\mathbf{n}],

denoted
\mathrm{P}_{her}(d,n,\mathbb{F})
, which is viewed as a real subspace of
\mathrm{P}_{bi}(d,n,\mathbb{F})
.
(Here
\mathrm{P}_{her}(d,n,\mathbb{R})
corresponds to
\Re\mathrm{S}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
.)
All correspondences are one-to-one and onto.

Hence, it is advantageous to replace
\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n},\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}\otimes\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n},\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
by
\mathrm{P}(d,n,\mathbb{F}),\mathrm{P}_{bi}(d,n,\mathbb{F}),\mathrm{P}_{her}(d,n,\mathbb{F})
respectively.
The following lemma summarizes the properties of the above isomorphisms, and recalls Banach’s characterization of the spectral norm of
\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}

[
2
]
:

Lemma 5.6
.

(1)

Define on
\mathrm{P}(d,n,\mathbb{F})
the following inner product
and the Hilbert norm:

(5.6)

\langle\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\rangle=\sum_{\mathbf{j}\in J(d,n)}c(\mathbf{j})\overline{f_{\mathbf{j}}}g_{\mathbf{j}},\quad\|\mathbf{f}\|=\sqrt{\langle\mathbf{f},\mathbf{f}\rangle}.

Then

(a)

Let
\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{j}}=(\delta_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k}})_{\mathbf{k}\in J(d,n)},\mathbf{j}\in J(d,n)
, where
\delta_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{k}}
is Kronecker’s delta function, be the standard basis in
\mathrm{P}(d,n,\mathbb{F})
. Then
\frac{1}{\sqrt{c(\mathbf{j}})}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{j}},\mathbf{j}\in J(d,n)
is an orthonormal basis in
\mathrm{P}(d,n,\mathbb{F})
of cardinality
n+d-1\choose d
.

(b)

The map
L:\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}\to\mathrm{P}(d,n,\mathbb{F})
which is given by
L(\mathcal{S})=f
, where
f(\mathbf{x})=\langle\bar{\mathcal{S}},\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\rangle
, is an isomorphism and an isometry.

(c)

The spectral norm of a symmetric tensor is given by Banach’s characterization
[
2
]
:

(5.7)

\|\mathcal{S}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=\max_{\|\mathbf{x}\|=1,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}}|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{S}\rangle|=\max_{\|\mathbf{x}\|=1,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}}|\Re\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{S}\rangle|=\max_{\|\mathbf{x}\|=1,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}}|f(\mathbf{x})|

(2)

Define on
\mathrm{P}_{bi}(d,n,\mathbb{F})
the following inner product
and the Hilbert norm:

(5.8)

\displaystyle\langle\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\rangle=\sum_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}\in J(d,n)}c(\mathbf{j})c(\mathbf{l})\overline{f_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}}}g_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}}.

Then

(a)

Let
\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}}=(\delta_{(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}),(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{m})})_{(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{m})\in J(d,n)\times J(d,n)},(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l})\in J(d,n)\times J(d,n)
, where
\delta_{(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}),(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{m})}
is Kronecker’s delta function, be the standard basis in
\mathrm{P}_{bi}(d,n,\mathbb{F})
. Then
\frac{1}{\sqrt{c(\mathbf{j})c(\mathbf{l})})}\mathbf{e}_{(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l})},(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l})\in J(d,n)\times J(d,n)
is an orthonormal basis in
\mathrm{P}_{bi}(d,n,\mathbb{F})
of cardinality
{n+d-1\choose d}^{2}
.

(b)

The map
L:\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}\otimes\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}\to\mathrm{P}_{bi}(d,n,\mathbb{F})
which is given by
L(\mathcal{T})=f
, where
f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\langle\bar{\mathcal{T}},\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d}\rangle
, is an isomorphism and an isometry.

(c)

The spectral norm of a bisymmetric tensor is given by Banach’s characterization
[
2
]
:

(5.9)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=\max_{\|\mathbf{x}\|=\|\mathbf{y}\|=1,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}}|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle|=\max_{\|\mathbf{x}\|=\|\mathbf{y}\|=1,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}}\Re\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle=

\displaystyle\max_{\|\mathbf{x}\|=\|\mathbf{y}\|=1,\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}}|f(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})|.

(3)

Define on
\mathrm{P}_{her}(d,n,\mathbb{F})
the inner product
and the Hilbert norm as in (
5.8
)

(a)

Let
\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}},\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}
be defined as in (
2a
). Then an orthonormal basis in
\mathrm{P}_{her}(d,n,\mathbb{F})
is as follows. First, to
\mathbf{j}\in J(d,n)
corresponds
\frac{1}{c(\mathbf{j})}\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{j}}
. Second, to a pair
(\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}),(\mathbf{l},\mathbf{j}),\mathbf{j}\neq\mathbf{l}
corresponds
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2c(\mathbf{j})c(\mathbf{l})}}(\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{j},\mathbf{l}}+\mathbf{e}_{\mathbf{l},\mathbf{j}})
.
The cardinality of this basis is
{n+d-1\choose d}^{2}
.

(b)

The map
L:\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}\to\mathrm{P}_{bi}(d,n,\mathbb{F})
which is given by
L(\mathcal{T})=f
, where
f(\mathbf{x},\bar{\mathbf{x}})=\langle\bar{\mathcal{T}},\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes d}\rangle
, is an isomorphism and an isometry.

(c)

The b-spectral norm of a bi-symmetric Hermitian tensor is:

(5.10)

\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec}=\max_{\|\mathbf{x}\|=1,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}}|f(\mathbf{x},\bar{\mathbf{x}})|=\max_{\|\mathbf{x}\|=1,\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}}\max(f(\mathbf{x},\bar{\mathbf{x}}),-f(\mathbf{x},\bar{\mathbf{x}})).

Proof.

(1)
Straightforward by repeating the arguments in
[
22
,
24
]
. (Observe that in (
5.7
) for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
, by changing the phase of
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
we can replace the
\pm
sign to
+
.)

(2)
:
(a)-(b)
straightforward.
(c)
: We claim that for a bisymmetric tensor
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}\otimes\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
the following equality holds:

(5.11)

\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=\max_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{F}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=\|\mathbf{y}\|=1}|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle|=\max_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{F}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=\|\mathbf{y}\|=1}\Re\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle.

Let

\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=\max_{\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}\in\Pi_{n^{\times d}}}|\langle\mathcal{X}\otimes\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{T}\rangle|=|\langle\mathcal{X}^{\star}\otimes\mathcal{Y}^{\star},\mathcal{T}\rangle|,\mathcal{X}^{\star}=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\star},\mathcal{Y}^{\star}=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{y}_{j}^{\star},\|\mathbf{x}_{j}^{\star}\|=\|\mathbf{y}_{j}^{\star}\|=1,j\in[d].

Let
\mathcal{S}=[\sum_{bj=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{d})\in[n^{\times d}]}t_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}\mathbf{y}_{j_{1}}^{\star}\cdots\mathbf{y}_{j_{d}}^{\star}]
.
Clearly,
\mathcal{S}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{F}^{n}
. Hence
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=\|\mathcal{S}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}=|\langle\mathcal{X}^{\star},\mathcal{S}\rangle|
. Use Banach’s theorem to replace
\mathcal{X}^{\star}
by
(\mathbf{x}^{\star})^{\otimes d}
. Set
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}=[\sum_{\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\in[n^{\times d}]}(\bar{x}^{\star})_{i_{1}}\cdots(\bar{x}^{\star})_{i_{d}}t_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}]
. Use the arguments for
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
to deduce the first equality in (
5.11
). The second equality in (
5.11
). is obtained by changing the phase of
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
. Part
(2b)
yields (
5.9
).

(3)
is straightforward. (Recall that that
f(\mathbf{x},\bar{\mathbf{x}})=\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\rangle
is real valued.)
∎

Note the orthonormal basis in
(1)
is corresponds to the Dicke basis
[
15
]
. See
[
24
, Appendix 1]
for the computation of GME for the Dicke states.

The following lemma is well known and we bring its proof for completeness:

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
2\mathbf{1}_{d}\leqslant\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{d}
. Assume that
\mathcal{X}=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathcal{Y}=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{y}_{j}\in\Pi_{n^{\times d}}
. Then

(5.12)

\|\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}-\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{y}_{j}\|\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{d}\|\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{y}_{j}\|.

Proof.

Use the triangle inequality on the following identity.

(5.13)

\displaystyle\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{x}_{j}-\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathbf{y}_{j}=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{d-1}(\otimes_{l=1}^{d-k-1}\mathbf{x}_{l})\otimes(\mathbf{x}_{d-k}-\mathbf{y}_{d-k})\otimes(\otimes_{d-k+1\leqslant m\leqslant d}\mathbf{y}_{m}).

∎

5.3.
Approximations of spectral and nuclear norms

Recall that a finite set
\mathrm{C}\subset\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
is an
\varepsilon
-covering set of
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
if
\cup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathrm{C}}\mathrm{B}(\mathbf{x},\varepsilon)\supset\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
. Lemma 2.1 in
[
46
]
gives an explicit simple
\frac{1}{m}
-covering set of
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
:

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
. Define

(5.14)

\displaystyle\mathrm{A}=\{\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:a_{i}=\frac{h}{nm},h=0,\pm 1,\ldots,\pm nm\},

\displaystyle\mathrm{C}(m,n)=\{\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{a}\|}\mathbf{a}:\mathbf{a}\in A\setminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\}.

Then
\mathrm{C}(m,n)
is an
\frac{1}{m}
-covering of
\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
of cardinality
(2nm+1)^{n}-1
.

Observe that
-\mathrm{C}(m,n)=\mathrm{C}(m,n)
.
For
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
we view
\mathbb{C}^{n}\sim\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\sim\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, where
\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{i}\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}
, and
\|\mathbf{z}\|=\sqrt{\|\mathbf{x}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{y}\|^{2}}
. Then
\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)\sim\mathrm{C}(m,2n)
corresponds to
\frac{1}{m}
-covering set of
\{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}:\|\mathbf{z}\|=1\}
,
We treat in this subsection all the norms that we discuss as real norms. Our approximation of the norms we discuss are as real norms.

Define

(5.15)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n}=\max_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathrm{C}(m,n)}|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle|,\quad\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C},m,n}=\max_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)}|\Re\langle\mathbf{z}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle|,\quad\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n}=\max_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{C}(m,n)}|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle|,\quad\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}\otimes\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C},m,n}=\max_{\mathbf{z},\mathbf{w}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)}|\Re\langle\mathbf{z}^{\otimes d}\otimes\mathbf{w}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle|,\quad\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}\otimes\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n},

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec,m,n}=\max_{\mathbf{z}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)}|\langle\mathbf{z}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{z}}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle|,\quad\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
2\leqslant n,d\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed.

(1)

The quantities in (
5.15
) are real norms that satisfy the following inequalities for specified values of
m
:

(5.16)

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant\frac{1}{1-\frac{d}{m}}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n},\quad\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}\textrm{ and }d<m,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C},m,n}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}\leqslant\frac{1}{1-\frac{d}{m}}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C},m,n},\quad\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}\textrm{ and }d<m,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant\frac{1}{1-\frac{2d}{m}}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n},\quad\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}\otimes\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}\textrm{ and }2d<m,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C},m,n}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}\leqslant\frac{1}{1-\frac{2d}{m}}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C},m,n},\quad\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}\otimes\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}\textrm{ and }2d<m,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec,m,n}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec}\leqslant\frac{1}{1-\frac{2d{n+d-1\choose d}^{3/2}}{m}}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec,m,n},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}\textrm{ and }2d{n+d-1\choose d}^{3/2}<m.

(2)

Assume that
d
satisfy the above inequalities for the corresponding norms.
Denote by
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R},m,n},\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{C},m,n},\|\mathcal{T}\|_{nuc,m,n}
the corresponding real dual norms. Then the following inequalities hold:

(5.17)

\displaystyle(1-\frac{d}{m})\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R},m,n}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R},m,n},\quad\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}\textrm{ and }d<m,

\displaystyle(1-\frac{d}{m})\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{C},m,n}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{C},m,n}\quad\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}\textrm{ and }d<m,

\displaystyle(1-\frac{2d}{m})\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R},m,n}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R},m,n},\quad\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}\otimes\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}\textrm{ and }2d<m,

\displaystyle(1-\frac{2d}{m})\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{C},m,n}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{C},m,n},\quad\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}\otimes\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}\textrm{ and }2d<m,

\displaystyle(1-\frac{2d{n+d-1\choose d}^{3/2}}{m})\|\mathcal{T}\|_{nuc,m,n}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{nuc\leqslant}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{nuc,m,n},

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}\textrm{ and }2d{n+d-1\choose d}^{3/2}<m.

Proof.

(1)
As
\mathrm{C}(m,n)\subset\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
and
\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)\subset\{\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}:\|\mathbf{z}\|=1\}
, use the characterizations (
2.3
), (
2.15
), (
5.11
) and (
4.28
) to deduce the first inequality in the five inequalities of (
5.16
). We now show how to deduce the second inequality in the five inequalities of (
5.16
). We start with the proof of the inequality
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant\frac{1}{1-\frac{d}{m}}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n}
for
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
Assume that
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}=|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle|
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
. Let
\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{C}(m,n)
such that
\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|\leqslant\frac{1}{m}
. Clearly,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}=|\langle\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d}+(\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}-\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d}),\mathcal{T}\rangle|\leqslant|\langle\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle|+|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}-\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle|\leqslant

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n}+|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}-\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle|.

Assume that
\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{y}
, and let
(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})=\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|\mathbf{z}
, where
\mathbf{z}=\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})
.
Use Banach’s characterization of
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}
and the equality (
5.13
) to deduce
|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}-\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d}),\mathcal{T}\rangle\leqslant d\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}
. That is,
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n}+\frac{d}{m}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}
.
Assume that
d<m
and deduce the second inequality of the first set of inequalities
in (
5.16
).
To deduce the inequality

\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}\leqslant\frac{1}{1-\frac{d}{m}}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C},m,n}
for
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
and
d<m
we assume that
\|cT\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}=\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle
for some
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1
. Suppose that
\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)
, and
\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|\leqslant 1/m
. Then

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}=\Re\langle\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle+\Re\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}-\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\mathbb{C},m,n}+|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}-\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d}|,\mathcal{T}\rangle|\leqslant

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\mathbb{C},m,n}+\frac{d}{m}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}\Rightarrow\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}\leqslant\frac{1}{1-\frac{d}{m}}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C},m,n}\textrm{ if }d<m.

Next, we point out briefly how to deduce the inequality
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant\frac{1}{1-\frac{2d}{m}}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n}
for
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}\otimes\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
2d<m
. First, use Banach’s theorem to deduce that
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}=|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle
for some
\mathbf{x},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\in\mathbb{S}^{n-1}
. Assume that
\mathbf{y},\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\in\mathrm{C}(m,n)
such that
\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|,\|\tilde{\mathbf{x}}-\tilde{\mathbf{y}}\|\leqslant\frac{1}{m}
. Hence,

\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n}+|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes d}-\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d}\otimes\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle|.

Use the equality (
5.13
) and the maximum characterization of
\mathcal{T}
to deduce the inequality
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n}+\frac{2d}{m}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}
. Assuming that
2d<m
we deduce the inequality

\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant\frac{1}{1-\frac{2d}{m}}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n}
. The inequality for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
deduced similarly to the case
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
.

We now show prove the last inequality in (
5.16
). Assume that
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
. Let
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec}=|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle|
for some
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{n},\|\mathbf{x}\|=1
. Recall that
\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle\in\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)
such that
\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|\leqslant\frac{1}{m}
. Use the above arguments and Lemma
5.7

to deduce

\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec}\|\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec,m,n}+|\langle\mathbf{x}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{x}}^{\otimes d}-\mathbf{y}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{y}}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle|\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec,m,n}+2d\|\mathcal{T}\|.

The second inequality in (
4.34
) yields

\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec}\|\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec,m,n}+2d{n+d-1\choose d}^{3/2}\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec}.

Assume that
2d{n+d-1\choose d}^{3/2}<m
and deduce the last inequality in (
5.16
).

Thus, under the assumptions that
m
satisfies specified inequalities we that the quantities in (
5.15
) are real norms.

(2)
We first prove the first set of inequalities in (
5.17
).
Since
\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a vector space over
\mathbb{R}
, the norms
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}
and

\|\cdot\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}
are real dual norms. The inequality
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}
, and the maximum characterization of the dual norms (
A.1
) yields the inequality
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R},m,n}\geqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}
. Assume that
\nu
and
\nu^{*}
are dual norm. Let
t>
and define a new norm
\nu_{1}=t\nu
. Then
\nu_{1}^{*}=t^{-1}\nu
. Hence, the second inequality of the first set of inequalities in (
5.17
) yield that
(1-d/m)\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R},m,n}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}
.

Assume now that
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{C}^{n}
. The definition (
A.1
) of
\nu^{*}
implies that
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}
is a real dual norm of
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}
. Therefore, the previous arguments yield the second set of the inequalities in (
5.17
). Similar arguments yield the rest of the inequalities in (
5.17
).
∎

5.4.
Proof of Theorem
5.1

We first show that the norms
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{F}}
and
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bspec}
are

\varepsilon
-approximable in poly-time in the input
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{-1}]^{n}
,
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
and
d
, where
n
is fixed and
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,1)
.
We start with the case
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Choose

(5.18)

m=\lceil\frac{d(1+\varepsilon)}{\varepsilon}\rceil,\quad\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,1).

Recall that the cardinality
\mathrm{C}(m,n)
is of order
(2mn+1)^{n}=O\left(\big(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\big)^{n}\right)
.
We need to estimate the number of flops and the size of storage to compute
\langle\mathbf{u}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle
for
\mathbf{u}=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{d})^{\top}\in\mathrm{C}(m,n)
. We code the tensors
\mathbf{u}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{Q}^{n}
by the coefficients of polynomials
f(\mathbf{x})
and
g(\mathbf{x})
given by (
5.5
). We assume that the entries of the tensor
\mathcal{T}
is given by
g_{\mathbf{j}},\in J(d,n)
.
The coefficient
f_{\mathbf{i}},\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in J(d,n)
is
u_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots u_{n}^{i_{n}}
.
Hence
\langle{\mathbf{u}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}}=\langle\mathbf{f},\mathbf{g}\rangle
as in (
5.6
), and
\langle\mathbf{u}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle=\mathbf{g}(\mathbf{u})
. Observe that
|{n+d-1\choose d}|=|{n+d-1\choose n-1}|=O(d^{n-1})
for a fixed
n
and
d\gg 1
.
Recall that the computation of
c(\mathbf{j})=\frac{d!}{j_{1}!\cdots j_{n}!}
needs
2d
multiplications and one division. Furthermore
c(\mathbf{j})\leqslant d!=O(2^{d(\log_{2}d}-\log_{2}e)
. Hence, computing
\langle\mathbf{u}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle,\mathbf{u}\in\mathrm{C}(m,n)
is polynomial in the storage entries of
f_{\mathbf{j}}\in\mathbb{Q}
and
\log m
. However, to compute the first maximum in (
5.15
) one needs
O\left(\big(\frac{d}{\varepsilon}\big)^{n}\right)
computations of
\langle\mathbf{u}^{\otimes d},\mathcal{T}\rangle
. This shows that the computation of
\|cT\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n}
is polynomial in the input
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{Q}^{n}
,
\frac{1}{\varepsilon}
and
d
. Polynomiality in
\varepsilon
is
\varepsilon^{-n}
.
Our assumption (
5.18
) combined with the first set of inequalites in (
5.16
) yields

(5.19)

\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant(1+\varepsilon)\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n}.

Similar results hold for other spectral norms in Theorem
5.1
.
It was shown in
[
24
]
that the GME of symmetric states is poly-time computable in
d
for a fixed value of
n\geqslant 2
, using poly-time solvability of certain systems of polynomial equations.

We now discuss poly-time approximation of the nuclear norms in Theorem
5.1
. Let us first discuss
\varepsilon
-approximation of
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}
by
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R},m,n}
for
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The condition (
5.18
) combined with the first set of inequalities in (
5.17
) yields:

(5.20)

(1-\varepsilon)\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R},m,n}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R},m,n}.

Since we showed that
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{R},m,n}
is poly-time computable, Theorem 3 in
[
21
]
yields that
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R},m,n}
is poly-time computable.
Hence,
\varepsilon
-approximation of
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{R}}
is poly-time computable.
Similar results hold for other nuclear norms in Theorem
5.1
.

In what follows, we show directly how to compute the nuclear norms in Theorem
5.1
using linear programming. Our approach is similar to
[
46
]
.
We restrict our discussion to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{bnuc,m,n}
.
The characterization of
\|\cdot\|_{bspec,m,n}\|
in (
5.15
) for
2d{n+d-1\choose d}^{3/2}<m
yields that the extreme points the norm
\|\cdot\|_{bnuc,m,n}\|
on
\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
are
\pm\mathbf{w}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{w}}^{\otimes d}
for
\mathbf{w}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)
. Note, that from the definition of
\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)
it follows that
-\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)=\overline{\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)}=\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)
.
Assume that
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}\cap\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{-1}]^{n}\otimes\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{-1}]^{n}
. That is,
\mathcal{T}
has Gaussian rational entries. To
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)
we correspond polynomials
\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{z},\bar{\mathbf{z}}),\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{w}}(\mathbf{z},\bar{\mathbf{z}})
as in Definition
5.5
and Lemma
5.6
:

(5.21)

\mathbf{f}=[f_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}}],\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{w}}=[g_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j},\mathbf{w}}],\quad g_{\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j},\mathbf{w}}=\mathbf{w}^{\mathbf{i}}\bar{\mathbf{w}}^{\mathbf{j}},\mathbf{i},\mathbf{j}\in J(d,n),\mathbf{w}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n).

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
\mathcal{T}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}\cap\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{-1}]^{n}\otimes\mathrm{S}^{d}\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{-1}]^{n}
. Assume that to tensors
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathbf{w}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{w}}^{\otimes d}
correspond vectors
\mathbf{f}
and
\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{w}}
as in (
5.21
).
Suppose furthermore that
2d{n+d-1\choose d}^{3/2}<m
. Then
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,m,n}
is the solution of the following linear programming problem:

(5.22)

\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,m,n}=\min_{\mathbf{f}=\sum_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)}a_{\mathbf{w}}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{w}}-b_{\mathbf{w}}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{w}},a_{\mathbf{w}},b_{\mathbf{w}}\geqslant 0}\sum_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)}a_{\mathbf{w}}+b_{\mathbf{w}}.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the above characterization where
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{1,m,n}=1
. As the extreme points of the unit ball
\|\mathcal{X}\|_{1,m,n}\leqslant 1
are
\pm\mathbf{w}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{w}}^{\otimes d},\mathbf{w}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)
,
\mathcal{T}
is a convex combination of
\pm\mathbf{w}^{\otimes d}\otimes\bar{\mathbf{w}}^{\otimes d},\mathbf{w}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)
. That is

\mathbf{f}=\sum_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)}a^{\prime}_{\mathbf{w}}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{w}}-b^{\prime}_{\mathbf{w}}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{w}},a^{\prime}_{\mathbf{w}},b^{\prime}_{\mathbf{w}}\geqslant 0,\sum_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)}a^{\prime}_{\mathbf{w}}+b^{\prime}_{\mathbf{w}}=1.

Assume that
\mathbf{f}=\sum_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)}a_{\mathbf{w}}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{w}}-b_{\mathbf{w}}\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{w}},a_{\mathbf{w}},b_{\mathbf{w}}\geqslant 0
.
As
\|\pm\mathbf{g}_{\mathbf{w}}\|_{1,m,n}=1
the triangle inequality yields

1=\|\mathbf{f}\|_{1,m,n}\leqslant\sum_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathrm{C}_{\mathbb{C}}(m,n)}a_{\mathbf{w}}+b_{\mathbf{w}}
.
∎

We now estimate the number of flops to find
\mathcal{T}\|_{1,\mathbb{C},m,n}
. Let us recall the complexity result in
[
41
]
. Assume that we have
a linear programming problem of the form
min_{A\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{b},\mathbf{x}\geqslant 0}\mathbf{c}^{\top}\mathbf{x}
, where
A\in\mathbb{Q}^{M\times N},\mathbf{c}\in\mathbb{Q}^{N}
. Then the arithmetic complexity, (the number of flops), is of order
O(\sqrt{M}(MN+M^{2}))
.
For a given
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
we want to have the inequality

(5.23)

(1-\varepsilon)\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bnuc,m,n}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bnuc}\leqslant\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bnuc,m,n}.

Use the last set of inequalities in (
5.17
) to deduce that

m=\lceil\frac{2d{n+d-1\choose d}^{3/2}}{\varepsilon}\rceil=O(\frac{d^{(3n-1)/2}}{\varepsilon}).

Observe next that for the minimum problem (
5.22
) the values of
M
and
N
are as follows:

M=2{n+d-1\choose d}^{2}=O(d^{2(n-1)}),\quad N=(2m(2n)+1)^{2n}-1=O(\frac{d^{(3n-1)n}}{\varepsilon^{2n}}).

Hence, the arithmetic complexity of finding
\|\mathcal{T}\|_{bnuc,m,n}
that satisfies (
5.23
) is
O(\frac{d^{3(n-1)+(3n-1)n}}{\varepsilon^{2n}})
.

Corollary 5.11
.

Assume that
\mathcal{R}\in\mathrm{B}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}\cap\otimes^{2d}\mathbb{Q}[\sqrt{-1}]^{n}
, where
n\geqslant 2
is a fixed integer. For a given
\varepsilon\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}\cap(0,1)
one can decide in poly-time in
d
if the distance of
\mathcal{T}
to strongly separable density tensors less than
\varepsilon
. More precisely, the arithmetic complexity of this problem is of order
O(\frac{d^{3(n-1)+(3n-1)n}}{\varepsilon^{2n}})
.

6.
Open problems

Open problems

For the following problems assume that
2\leqslant d,n
.

(1)

Is
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}=\|\mathcal{B}\|_{spec}
for all
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{H}_{\mathbf{n}}
?

(2)

Is
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{1,\mathbb{C}}=\|\mathcal{B}\|_{aspec}
for all
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
for
d<n
?

(3)

Assume that
d=\lceil\frac{n}{2}\rceil
. Is the computation of
\|\mathcal{B}\|_{\infty,\mathbb{C}}
is NP-hard in
n
for
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d}}
. Is the assertion that
\mathcal{B}\in\mathrm{A}\mathrm{H}_{n^{\times d},+,1}
is within
\varepsilon
-distance from
\mathrm{Sepa}_{n^{\times d}}
NP-hard?

Appendix A
Comparison of a general norm to Euclidean norm

Let
\mathbb{F}
be either the field of the real numbers
\mathbb{R}
or complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
. For
\mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}
we define
\mathbf{x}^{*}=(\bar{x}_{1},\ldots,\bar{x}_{n})
. (Note that for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
we have
\mathbf{x}^{*}=\mathbf{x}^{\top}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
.) The inner product in
\mathbb{F}^{n}
is given by
\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle=\mathbf{x}^{*}\mathbf{y}
. The Euclidean norm is given by
\|\mathbf{x}\|=\sqrt{\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}\rangle}
. A set
\mathrm{K}\subset\mathbb{F}^{n}
is convex,
if for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{K}
the interval
[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}]:=\{\mathbf{z}=t\mathbf{x}+(1-t)\mathbf{y}:0\leqslant t\leqslant 1\}
is in
\mathrm{K}
.
Let
\Delta^{m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
be the simplex of probability vectors. Clearly,
\Delta^{m}
is a convex set.
For a given set
\mathrm{S}\subset\mathbb{F}^{n}
, the set cospan
(\mathrm{S}):=\{\mathbf{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i},(a_{1},\ldots,a_{m})\in\Delta^{m},\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathrm{S},i\in[m],m\in\mathbb{N}\}
is a convex set spanned by
\mathrm{S}
.
A point
\mathbf{z}\in\mathrm{K}
is called an extreme point of
\mathrm{K}
if for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{K}
such that
\mathbf{z}\in[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}]
the equality
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{y}=z
holds.

Assume that
\mathrm{K}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a compact convex set. Minkowski’s Theorem states that the set of extreme points of
K
, denoted as
\mathrm{E}(\mathrm{K})
, is the minimal set of
\mathrm{K}
that cospans
\mathrm{K}

[
47
, page 427]
.
Assume that
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
. Caratheodory’s theorem
[
47
]
states that every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathrm{K}
is cospanned by at most
n+1
extreme points. Assume that
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
. By viewing
\mathbb{C}^{n}
as
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
we deduce that every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathrm{K}\subset\mathbb{C}^{n}
is cospanned by at most
2n+1
extreme points.

Assume that
\nu:\mathbb{F}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}:=[0,\infty)
is a norm on
\mathbb{F}^{n}
. That is

\displaystyle\nu(\mathbf{x})=0\iff\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0},

\displaystyle\nu(a\mathbf{x})=|a|\nu(\mathbf{x})\textrm{ for }a\in\mathbb{F},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}^{n},

\displaystyle\nu(\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y})\leqslant\nu(\mathbf{x})+\nu(\mathbf{y}).

Denote
B_{\nu}(\mathbf{a},r)=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}:\nu(\mathbf{x}-a)\leqslant r\}
. Let
\mathrm{B}(\mathbf{a},r):=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}:\,\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{a}\|\leqslant r\}
.

The dual norm
\nu^{*}
on
\mathbb{F}^{n}
is given by

(A.1)

\nu^{*}(\mathbf{x})=\max_{\nu(\mathbf{y})\leqslant 1}\Re\langle\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\rangle.

Recall that
\nu^{**}=\nu
, and
\|\cdot\|^{*}=\|\cdot\|
.

Denote by
\mathrm{E}(\nu)
the extreme points of the ball
\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(\mathbf{0},1)
. Observe that
\mathrm{E}(\nu)
is a balanced set:
\zeta\mathrm{E}(\nu)=\mathrm{E}(\nu)
for
\zeta\in\mathbb{F},\|\zeta|=1
. Hence, (
A.1
) is equivalent to

(A.2)

\nu^{*}(\mathbf{x})=\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{E}(\nu)}\Re\langle\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\rangle.

(Recall that the set
\mathrm{E}(\nu)
may not be compact
[
43
]
.)
Vice versa, assume
\mathrm{E}\subset\mathbb{F}^{n}
is a bounded balanced set that spans
\mathbb{F}^{n}
. Let
\nu
be a norm whose unit ball is the closure of cospan
(\mathrm{E})
. Then
\nu^{*}
is given by

(A.3)

\displaystyle\nu^{*}(\mathbf{x})=\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{E}}\Re\langle\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\rangle,

\displaystyle\mathrm{E}(\nu)\subseteq\textrm{closure}(\mathrm{E}).

The following lemma is well known and we bring its proof for completeness:

Lemma A.1
.

Let
\nu
be a norm on
\mathbb{F}^{n}
. Then

(A.4)

\|\mathbf{y}\|^{2}\leqslant\nu(\mathbf{y})\nu^{*}(\mathbf{y}).

Assume that the linear functional
\varphi_{\mathbf{y}}:\mathbb{F}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
is given by
\varphi_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x})=\Re\langle\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\rangle
.
Equality holds in (
A.4
) holds for
\mathbf{y}\neq\mathbf{0}
if and only one of the following two equivalent conditions hold.

(1)

The inequality
\varphi_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x})\leqslant\nu(\mathbf{y})\nu^{*}(\mathbf{y})
is a supporting hyperplane of
\mathrm{B}_{\nu^{*}}(\mathbf{0},\nu^{*}(\mathbf{y}))
at
\mathbf{y}
.

(2)

The inequality
\varphi_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x})\leqslant\nu(\mathbf{y})\nu^{*}(\mathbf{y})
is a supporting hyperplane of
\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(\mathbf{0},\nu(\mathbf{y}))
at
\mathbf{y}
.

Proof.

Assume that
\mathbf{y}\neq\mathbf{0}
, and set
\mathbf{y}_{1}=\frac{1}{\nu(\mathbf{y})}\mathbf{y}
. The characterization (
A.1
) yields that
\nu^{*}(\mathbf{y})\geqslant\Re\langle\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{y}\rangle=\frac{\|\mathbf{y}\|^{2}}{\nu(\mathbf{y})}
. This establishes the inequality (
A.4
).

We now consider the equality case in (
A.4
).
(1)
. Suppose first that the equality case holds in (
A.4
). Then
\varphi_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{y})=\nu(\mathbf{y})\nu^{*}(\mathbf{y})
. Assume that
\mathbf{x}\in\mathrm{B}_{\nu^{*}}(\mathbf{0},\nu^{*}(\mathbf{y}))
. We claim that

(A.5)

\varphi_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x})\leqslant\nu(\mathbf{y})\nu^{*}(\mathbf{y}).

Suppose first that
\varphi_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x})\leqslant 0
. Then (
A.5
) trivially holds.
Assume that
\varphi_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x})>0
.
By considering
t\mathbf{x},t\geqslant 1
such that
\nu^{*}(t\mathbf{x})=\nu^{*}(\mathbf{y})
we can assume without loss of generality that
\nu^{*}(\mathbf{x})=\nu*(y)
.
Use (
A.1
) to deduce that

\varphi_{\mathbf{y}}(\frac{1}{\nu(\mathbf{y})}\mathbf{x})=\Re\langle\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{x}\rangle\leqslant\nu^{*}(\mathbf{x})=\nu^{*}(\mathbf{y}),

which proves (
A.5
).

Assume now that
\varphi_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x})\leqslant\nu(\mathbf{y})\nu^{*}(\mathbf{y})
is a supporting hyperplane of
\mathrm{B}_{\nu^{*}}(\mathbf{0},\nu^{*}(\mathbf{y}))
at
\mathbf{y}
. Then
\nu(\mathbf{y})\nu^{*}(\mathbf{y})=\varphi(\mathbf{y})
.

As
\nu^{**}=\nu
we deduce
(2)
from
(1)
from by replacing
\nu
with
\nu^{*}
.
∎

Theorem A.2
.

Let

(A.6)

\alpha(\nu)=\min_{\|\mathbf{x}\|=1}\nu(\mathbf{x}),\quad\beta(\nu)=\max_{\|\mathbf{x}\|=1}\nu(\mathbf{x}).

Then

(A.7)

\alpha(\nu^{*})=\frac{1}{\beta(\nu)},\quad\beta(\nu^{*})=\frac{1}{\alpha(\nu)}.

Furthermore,

(A.8)

\displaystyle(\|\mathbf{x}\|=1)\wedge(\nu(\mathbf{x})=\alpha(\nu))\Rightarrow\nu(\mathbf{x})\nu^{*}(\mathbf{x})=1,

\displaystyle(\|\mathbf{y}\|=1)\wedge(\nu(\mathbf{y})=\beta(\nu))\Rightarrow\nu(\mathbf{y})\nu^{*}(\mathbf{y})=1.

In particular, the following implications hold for
\|\mathbf{x}\|=\|\mathbf{y}\|=1
:

(A.9)

\displaystyle\alpha(\nu)=\nu(\mathbf{x})\iff\beta(\nu^{*})=\nu^{*}(\mathbf{x}),

\displaystyle\beta(\nu)=\nu(\mathbf{y})\iff\alpha(\nu^{*})=\nu(\mathbf{y}).

Proof.

Observe that (
A.6
) is equivalent to the sharp inequalities

(A.10)

\alpha(\nu)\|\mathbf{x}\|\leqslant\nu(\mathbf{x})\leqslant\beta(\nu)\|\mathbf{x}\|\textrm{ for all }\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}.

Combine the above inequality with the equality
\|\mathbf{x}\|^{*}=\|\mathbf{x}\|
to deduce

\nu^{*}(\mathbf{x})=\max_{\mathbf{y}\neq\mathbf{0}}\frac{|\langle\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\rangle|}{\nu(\mathbf{y})}\Rightarrow\frac{1}{\beta(\nu)}\|\mathbf{x}\|\leqslant\nu^{*}(\mathbf{x})\leqslant\frac{1}{\alpha(\nu)}\|\mathbf{x}\|.

We claim that the above inequalities are sharp. If not, apply the above inequalities to
\nu=\nu^{**}
to obtain a contradiction to the sharp inequalities in (
A.10
). This establishes (
A.7
).

We first prove the first implication in (
A.8
). The inequality
\nu(\mathbf{x})\geqslant\alpha(\nu)\|\mathbf{x}\|
yields
\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(0,\alpha(\nu))\subset\mathrm{B}(0,1)
.
Assume that
\|\mathbf{x}\|=1
and
\nu(\mathbf{x})=\alpha(\nu)
. That is, and
\mathbf{x}\in\partial\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(0,\alpha(\nu))\cap\partial\mathrm{B}(0,1)
. Let
\varphi_{\mathbf{x}}=\Re\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle
. Then the supporting hyperplane of
\mathrm{B}(0,1)
at
\mathbf{x}
is
\varphi_{x}(\mathbf{y})\leqslant 1
. Hence,
\varphi_{x}(\mathbf{y})\leqslant 1
is also a supporting hyperplane of
\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(0,\alpha(\nu))
at
\mathbf{x}
. As
\nu=\nu^{**}
, any supporting hyperplane of
\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(0,\alpha(\nu))=\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(0,\nu(x))
at
\mathbf{x}
is of the form
\varphi_{\mathbf{z}}\leqslant\nu(\mathbf{x})
for some
z,\nu^{*}(\mathbf{z})=1
. That is,
\mathbf{z}=\frac{1}{\nu^{*}(\mathbf{x})}\mathbf{x}
. Hence,

\nu(\mathbf{x})=\Re\langle\mathbf{z},\mathbf{x}\rangle\Rightarrow\nu(\mathbf{x})\nu^{*}(\mathbf{x})=\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}\rangle.

We now prove the second implication in (
A.8
). Assume that
\|\mathbf{y}\|=1
and
\nu(\mathbf{y})=\beta(\nu)
. Then,
\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(0,\beta(\nu))\supset\mathrm{B}(0,1)
, and
\mathbf{y}\in\partial\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(0,\beta(\nu))\cap\partial\mathrm{B}(0,1)
. Let
\varphi_{\mathbf{z}}
be the supporting hyperplane of

\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(0,\nu(\mathbf{y}))
at
\mathbf{y}
. That is,
\varphi_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{x})=\Re\langle\mathbf{z},\mathbf{x}\rangle
, where
\nu^{*}(\mathbf{z})=1
, and
\varphi_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{x})\leqslant\varphi_{\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{y})=\nu(\mathbf{y})
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(0,\nu(\mathbf{y}))
. Hence,
\varphi_{\frac{1}{\nu(\mathbf{y})}\mathbf{z}}(\mathbf{x})\leqslant 1
is a supporting hyperplane of
\mathrm{B}(0,1)
at
\mathbf{y}
. Recall that the unique supporting hyperplane of
\mathrm{B}(0,1)
at
\mathbf{y}
is
\varphi_{\mathbf{y}}(\mathbf{x})=\Re\langle\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x}\rangle\leqslant 1
. Therefore
\mathbf{z}=\nu(\mathbf{y})\mathbf{y}
, and

\langle\mathbf{y},\mathbf{y}\rangle=1=\nu^{*}(\mathbf{z})=\nu^{*}(\nu(\mathbf{y})\mathbf{y})=\nu(\mathbf{y})\nu^{*}(\mathbf{y}).

Implications (
A.9
) follows straightforward from (
A.8
).
∎

A.1.
The
\nu
\rank
of a vector

Definition A.3
.

Let
\nu
be a norm on
\mathbb{F}^{n}
. For a vector
\mathbf{0}\neq\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}
the
\nu
\rank
of
\mathbf{x}
is the minimal
r
such that the unit vector
\frac{1}{\nu(\mathbf{x})}\mathbf{x}
is a convex combination of
r
extreme points of
\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(0,1)
, denoted as
\nu
\rank\mathbf{x}
. Let
\nu
\rank\mathbf{0}=0
.
The maximal
\nu
\rank\mathbf{x}
is denoted by
\max
\nu\mathrm{rank}
.

Caratheodory’s theorem yields that

(A.11)

\mathrm{max}\nu\mathrm{rank}\leqslant\begin{cases}n+1\textrm{ if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\\
2n+1\textrm{ if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}.\end{cases}

Recall that a face
\mathbf{F}
of
\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(\mathbf{0},1)
is a closed convex set
\mathbf{F}\subset\partial\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(\mathbf{0},1)
such that for any
\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{y}\in\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(\mathbf{0},1)
, such that if
[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}]\cap\mathbf{F}
contains an interior point
z\in[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}]
then
[\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}]\subset\mathbf{F}
. A face
\mathbf{F}
is called exposed if there exists
\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{F}^{n},\nu^{*}(\mathbf{z})=1
such that

(A.12)

\mathbf{F}=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{F}^{n}:\nu(\mathbf{x})=1,\Re\langle\mathbf{z},\mathbf{x}\rangle=1\}.

It is straightforward to show that any maximal face of
\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(\mathbf{0},1)
is exposed.

Lemma A.4
.

Let
\nu
be a norm on
\mathbb{F}^{n}
. Then

(1)

The set of the extreme points
\mathrm{E}
of
\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(\mathbf{0},1)
is closed if and only if
\nu\mathrm{rank}
is lower semicontinuous.

(2)

The maximum dimension of an exposed face of
\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(\mathbf{0},1)
is an upper bound for
\mathrm{max}\nu\mathrm{rank}-1
.

Proof.

(1)
Assume that
\mathrm{E}
is not closed. Then there exists a sequence of
x_{i},i\in\mathrm{E},i\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathbf{x}_{i}=x,\nu(\mathbf{x})=1
, such that

\mathbf{x}\not\in\mathrm{E}
. Clearly,
\nu\rank\mathbf{x}_{i}=1,i\in\mathbb{N}
and
\nu\rank\mathbf{x}\geqslant 2
. Hence
\nu
rank is not lower semicontinuous.

Assume that
\mathrm{E}
is a closed set. Let
\mathbf{x}\neq 0
, and assume that
\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathbf{x}_{i}=\mathbf{x}
, and
\lim_{i\to\infty}\nu\textrm{rank}\,\mathbf{x}_{i}=k\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence,
\nu\textrm{rank}\,\mathbf{x}_{i}=k
for
i\geqslant N
. Thus
\frac{1}{\nu(\mathbf{x}_{i})}\mathbf{x}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{i,j}y_{j,i}
, where
a_{i,j}>0,y_{j,i}\in\mathrm{E},j\in[k],\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{i,j}=1
and
i\geqslant N
. By taking a subsequence of
i\geqslant N
and using the compactness of
\mathrm{E}
and the interval
[0,1]
we deduce that
\frac{1}{\nu(\mathbf{x})}\mathbf{x}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{j}\mathbf{y}_{j}
, where
a_{j}\geqslant 0,\mathbf{y}_{j}\in\mathrm{E},\sum_{j=1}^{k}a_{j}=1
.

(2)

Assume that
\nu(\mathbf{x})=1
, and max
\nu
rank
=\nu\rank\mathbf{x}=r
. Thus
\mathbf{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}
, where
a_{i}>0,\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathrm{E}(\nu),i\in[r],\sum_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}=1
. Observe that cospan
(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{r})
is contained in a maximal face
\mathcal{F}
of
\mathrm{B}_{\nu}(\mathbf{0},1)
, whose extreme points are
\mathcal{F}\cap\mathrm{B}(\mathbf{0},1)
. As
\mathcal{F}
is compact, Caratheodory’s theorem yields that
\mathbf{x}
is a convex combination of at most
\dim\mathcal{F}+1
extreme points of
\mathcal{F}
.
∎
